Constructible sheaf complexes in complex geometry and Applications Thanks: L. Maxim is partially supported by the Simons Foundation Collaboration Grant #567077. J. Schürmann is funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) Project-ID 427320536 – SFB 1442, as well as under Germany’s Excellence Strategy EXC 2044 390685587, Mathematics Münster: Dynamics – Geometry – Structure.
Laurenţiu G. Maxim, Jörg Schürmann

Abstract
. We present a detailed introduction of the theory of constructible sheaf complexes in the complex algebraic and analytic setting. All concepts are illustrated by many interesting examples and relevant applications, while some important results are presented with complete proofs. This paper is intended as a broadly accessible user’s guide to these topics, providing the readers with a taste of the subject, reflected by concrete examples and applications that motivate the general theory. We discuss the stability of constructible sheaf complexes under the standard functors, and explain the relation of these functors to perverse sheaves and the perverse t t -structure. We introduce the main results of stratified Morse theory in the framework of constructible sheaves, for proving the basic vanishing and finiteness results. Applications are given to various index theorems, the functorial calculus of characteristic cycles of constructible functions, and to weak Lefschetz and Artin-Grothendieck type theorems. We recall the construction of Deligne’s nearby and vanishing cycle functors, prove that they preserve constructible complexes, and discuss their relation with the perverse t t -structure. We finish this paper with a description and applications of the Kähler package for intersection cohomology of complex algebraic varieties, and the recent study of perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
32S60, 32S20, 32S05, 32S25, 32S40, 32S50, 58K05, 58K30, 14B05

1.
Introduction

The main goal of this paper is to provide a user’s guide, both for the novice and the expert, for the theory of (weakly) constructible sheaf complexes in complex geometry and their many applications. Our guiding principle for writing these notes was to provide an explicit and geometric introduction of the mathematical concepts, while also explaining some of the most important examples of the general theory. For this reason, we aim to present as many of the basic results as possible, sometimes even with complete proofs in some special important cases. Moreover, these results and definitions are then always explained and illustrated by many examples.

Constructible (complexes of) sheaves
are the algebraic counterpart of the decomposition of a variety into manifolds pieces (strata), and they are, roughly speaking, obtained by gluing local systems defined along strata of a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
.
Perverse sheaves
are an important class of constructible complexes, introduced in
[
6
]
as a formalization of the celebrated Riemann-Hilbert correspondence of Kashiwara
[
65
]
, which relates the topology of algebraic, resp., analytic varieties (intersection homology) and the algebraic, resp., analytic theory of differential equations (holonomic
D
-modules).

In recent years, constructible sheaf complexes and especially perverse sheaves have become indispensable tools for studying complex algebraic and analytic varieties.
They have seen spectacular applications in geometry and topology (e.g., the decomposition theorem
[
6
]
and the topology of complex algebraic maps), but also in fields such as representation theory (e.g., proof of the Kazhdan-Lusztig conjecture, proof of the geometrization of the Satake isomorphism, and proof of the fundamental lemma in the Langlands program) or combinatorics (e.g., Stanley’s proof of McMullen’s conjecture, or the resolution of the Dowling-Wilson top-heavy conjecture); see, e.g.,
[
25
,
92
]
for more recent surveys of such applications.
Furthermore, perverse sheaves and the
nearby and vanishing cycle functors
of Deligne
[
52
]
are the backbone of Saito’s mixed Hodge module theory
[
109
,
110
]
, a far-reaching generalization of Deligne’s mixed Hodge theory.

However, despite their fundamental importance, perverse sheaves as special constructible complexes of sheaves remain rather mysterious objects. It is our hope that the present paper will help readers become better acquainted with various aspects of the general theory.
Those looking to delve further into more specialized topics or wishing to explore problems of current research will find ample references to facilitate navigation of both classic and recent literature.

Let us next give a brief summary of the content of the paper.

In Section
2
, we define the notion of (weak) constructibility, and discuss the stability of (weakly) constructible sheaf complexes under the standard functors. As far as possible, we allow also weakly constructible sheaf complexes, where one does not impose any finiteness conditions for the stalks
(and which is sometimes even more natural or simpler to work with, especially as long as no duality is used). Similarly, we try to work in such a way that it applies to the complex algebraic as well as complex analytic context (sometimes only under suitable compactness assumptions in the complex analytic context). The presented calculus includes
external products, Künneth isomorphisms, Verdier duality
and the relation to the
Euler characteristic calculus of constructible functios
.

We also introduce here the perverse t-structure and perverse sheaves (with respect to middle perversity), and explain their relation with the standard functors
(following their counterparts in
l
-adic cohomology as presented in
[
6
, Chapter 4]
). Several aspects of the general theory are worked out in detail for intersection cohomology complexes, which provide some of the main examples of perverse sheaves. Furthermore, for constructible sheaf complexes of

R
-modules with
R
a
Dedekind domain
, we also consider the
dual perverse t-strucure
and its relation to the
rectified homological depth
of Grothendieck, as studied by Hamm and Lê
[
56
]
(also for the corresponding homotopical notion).

In Section
3
, we explain the basic results from
[
113
]
about
stratified Morse theory
in the framework of (weakly) constructible sheaves in the
complex context
, continuing and extending the recent survey of Goresky
[
49
]
in this handbook series, as well as Massey’s survey
[
88
]
. We follow here the notions of the geometric stratified Morse theory of Goresky-MacPherson
[
47
]
, so that one can easily compare the results of our paper with those of
loc.cit.
. We introduce, for example, the sheaf theoretic counterparts of the
local and normal Morse data
,
as well as their relations for a
stratified Morse critical point
of a
C^{\infty}
-function on a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety.
The normal Morse data of (weakly) constructible sheaf complexes are studied via the

complex link
of a stratum of a Whitney stratification, which allows to prove the basic
vanishing and finiteness theorems
by induction on the dimension of the underlying complex analytic variety. In particular, we get a description of the (dual) perverse t-structure in terms of properties of the normal Morse data.
Moreover, we also explain some relations to the general
micro-local sheaf theory
of Kashiwara-Schapira
[
67
]
, e.g. like a description of the
micro-support
of a (weakly) constructible sheaf complex in terms of the normal Morse data.

We use the language of
stratified Morse theory for constructible functions and sheaves
to also give in this section an introduction to the functorial theory of
Lagrangian cycles
in the complex analytic and algebraic context. We discuss from this viewpoint the
Euler isomorphism
between constructible functions and Lagrangian cycles given by the
characteristic cycle
of a constructible function, together with some index theorems.
Examples are given to
Poincaré-Hopf index theorems for singular spaces
,
effective characteristic cycles on abelian varieties

[
4
]
and the

global Euler obstruction for affine varieties

[
120
]
, as well as the famous
local Euler obstruction
of MacPherson
[
83
]
.
We also explain (using this language of
stratified Morse theory for constructible functions
) the translation into the context of Lagrangian cycles of the following operations for constructible functions and sheaves: external product, proper direct image, non-characteristic pullback and specialization (i.e., nearby cycles),
together with an intersection formula for vanishing cycles.

Finally, the last part of Section
3
deals with applications of the
stratified Morse theory for constructible sheaves
to
vanishing and weak Lefschetz theorems
in the complex algebraic and analytic context. This includes different versions of the
Artin vanishing theorem
for complex algebraically
(weakly) constructible complexes on an
affine
variety, and
vanishing theorems
for (weakly) constructible complexes on complex analytic

Stein and
q
-complete varieties
, as well as relative counterparts for morphisms given by
Artin-Grothendieck types theorems
in the complex algebraic and analytic context.

In Section
4
, we recall the construction of Deligne’s
nearby and vanishing cycle functors

[
52
]
, and prove that they preserve (weakly) constructible complexes. For the constructible context we also need and explain the calculation of their
(co)stalks
in terms of the
Milnor fibers
for the corresponding local Milnor fibrations,

[
72
]
based on the existence of an adopted Whitney stratifications satisfying the
a_{f}
-condition of Thom

[
14
,
57
,
76
]
.
Then we state the relation of
nearby and vanishing cycle functors
with duality
[
89
]
, and prove their relation with the (dual) perverse
t
-structure and perverse sheaves (from the point of view of stratified Morse theory, as developed in the previous section), i.e.,

\psi_{f}[-1]\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{f}[-1]\quad\text{are {\it t-exact} for the (dual) perverse t-structure.}

We also include here a discussion on the
Thom-Sebastiani theorem
for vanishing cycles
[
87
,
113
]
, and give a brief description of Beilinson’s
and Deligne-Verdier’s procedure for
gluing perverse sheaves
via vanishing cycles
[
7
,
128
]
.
As a final application we explain the equality
Rf_{!}=Rf_{*}
on the level of Grothendieck groups of algebraically constructible complexes
[
70
,
129
]
,
as well as analytically constructible functions in a compactifiable complex analytic context
[
113
]
.

In Section
5
, we overview properties of the
intersection cohomology
groups of complex algebraic varieties, which generalize the corresponding features of the cohomology groups of smooth varieties. These properties, consisting of
Poincaré duality, (weak and hard) Lefschetz theorems
and the
decomposition theorem
, are collectively termed the
Kähler package
for intersection cohomology
[
6
,
24
,
25
,
109
,
110
]
.

We also mention briefly a recent combinatorial application of the Kähler package for intersection cohomology, namely a proof (for realizable matroids) by Huh-Wang
[
59
]
of the
Dowling-Wilson top-heavy conjecture

[
32
,
33
]
.

Finally, in Section
6
, we survey recent developments in the study of
perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties
. We also include several concrete applications of this theory, e.g., to the study of homotopy types of complex algebraic manifolds (formulated in terms of their cohomology jump loci), as well as new topological characterizations of semi-abelian varieties
[
77
,
80
,
79
]
.

We assume reader’s familiarity with derived categories and the derived calculus; for a quick refresher on these topics the interested reader may consult
[
31
]
,
[
92
]
or
[
67
, Chapter I-III]
.

We work in the complex algebraic or analytic setting with reduced Hausdorff spaces, e.g., in the complex algebraic context we are working with the complex analytic space associated to a reduced separated scheme of finite type over
\spec({\mathbb{C}})
. Unless otherwise specified, all dimensions are taken to be complex dimensions.
In the complex analytic setting we always assume that our spaces have bounded dimension and a countable topology, e.g., the disjoint union of all

{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
’s (
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
) is not allowed.

2.
Constructible and perverse sheaf complexes

2.1.
Constructibility

Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety (here a variety does not need to be irreducible). It is well known that such a variety can be endowed with a
Whitney stratification
, i.e., a (locally) finite partition
\mathscr{S}
into non-empty, connected, locally closed nonsingular subvarieties
S
of
X
(called “strata”) which satisfy the following properties:

(i)

Frontier condition
: for any stratum
S\in\mathscr{S}
, the frontier
\partial S:={\bar{S}}\setminus S
is a union of strata of
\mathscr{S}
, where
{\bar{S}}
denotes the closure of
S
.

(ii)

Constructibility
: the closure
{\bar{S}}
and the frontier
\partial S
of any stratum
S\in\mathscr{S}
are closed complex algebraic (respectively, analytic) subspaces in
X
.

These conditions already imply that
X
gets an induced dimension filtration

X_{\bullet}:\emptyset:=X_{-1}\subset X_{0}\subset\cdots\subset X_{n}=X

by the closed algebraic (or analytic) subsets
X_{i}:=\bigcup_{\dim S\leq i}S
(
0\leq i\leq n=\dim X
), with the strata
S
of dimension
i
given by the connected components of
X_{i}\backslash X_{i-1}
.

In addition, whenever two strata
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are such that
S_{2}\subseteq{\partial S_{1}}
, the pair
(S_{2},S_{1})
is required to satisfy the following Whitney b-regularity conditions that guarantee that the variety
X
is topologically or cohomologically equisingular along each stratum
(as in
[
113
, Section 4.2]
):

(iii)

Whitney b-condition
: If
x_{n}\in S_{1}
and
y_{n}\in S_{2}
are sequences converging to
x\in S_{2}
such that the tangent planes
T_{x_{n}}S_{1}
converge to some limiting plane
\tau
and the secant lines
l_{n}=\overline{x_{n},y_{n}}
converge to some limiting line
l
(in some local coordinates), then
l\subset\tau
.

Note that the Whitney b-condition implies the following.

(iv)

Whitney a-condition
: If
x_{n}\in S_{1}
is a sequence converging to
x\in S_{2}
such that the tangent planes
T_{x_{n}}S_{1}
converge to some limiting plane
\tau
, then
T_{x}S_{2}\subset\tau
.

These conditions are independent of the choice of local coordinates, and any algebraic (or analytic) partition
\mathscr{S}

as above has a refinement to an algebraic (or analytic) Whitney stratification (see, e.g.,
[
125
, Theorem 1.2 and Proposition 2.1]
,
[
126
]
, as well as the references given in
[
47
, Section 1.7]
). Moreover, if the Whitney b-conditions hold for all
connected components
S
of the
X_{i}\backslash X_{i-1}
and a filtration
X_{\bullet}
by closed algebraic (or analytic) subsets as above,
then the partition
\mathscr{S}
with these strata
S
is (locally) finite and satisfies the frontier condition (see, e.g.,

[
113
, Section 4.2.1]
) as well as the constructibility condition.

Example 2.1

(Whitney umbrella)
.

The singular locus of the Whitney umbrella

X=\{z^{2}=xy^{2}\}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{3}

is the
x
-axis, but the origin is “more singular” than any other point on the
x
-axis. A Whitney stratification of
X
is given by the strata

S_{1}=X\setminus\{x-\text{axis}\},\quad S_{2}=\{(x,0,0)\mid x\neq 0\},\quad S_{3}=\{(0,0,0)\}.

Another example is given by
X
a complex manifold, with strata
S
given by its connected components.

In the following, let
R
be a noetherian and commutative ring of finite global dimension. Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety, and denote by
D^{b}(X;R)
the derived category of bounded complexes of sheaves of
R
-modules. By our assumptions on
X
and
R
, these bounded derived categories

D^{b}(-;R)
are closed under Grothendieck’s six operations:
Rf_{*}
,
Rf_{!}
,
f^{*}
,
f^{!}
,
R{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}
and
\overset{L}{\otimes}
, with
f
an algebraic (or analytic) morphism.

Definition 2.2
.

A sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}
of
R
-modules on
X
is said to be
weakly constructible
if there is a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
so that the restriction

{\mathscr{F}}|_{S}
of
{\mathscr{F}}
to every stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
is an
R
-local system (i.e., a locally constant sheaf). In this case we also say that
{\mathscr{F}}
is

{\mathscr{S}}
-weakly constructible
. A (
{\mathscr{S}}
-)weakly constructible sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}
on
X
is called
(
{\mathscr{S}}
-)constructible
, if all stalks

{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
for
x\in X

are finitely generated
R
-modules. A bounded complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is called (weakly) constructible if all its cohomology sheaves
{\mathscr{H}}^{j}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
are (weakly) constructible. Similarly for
{\mathscr{S}}
-(weakly) constructible complexes in case one works with a fixed Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
.

Note that the category
Sh_{({\mathscr{S}}-)wc}(X)
of (
{\mathscr{S}}
-)weakly constructible sheaves on
X
is an abelian subcategory of the category of all sheaves of
R
-modules on
X
, which is stable under extensions (like the subcategory of
R
-local systems). Similarly for the category
Sh_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X)
of
(
{\mathscr{S}}
-)constructible sheaves (like the subcategory of
R
-local systems with finitely generated stalks), since we assume
R
to be noetherian.

Example 2.3
.

On a point space
X=\{pt\}
any sheaf is weakly constructible. For general
X
, the constant sheaf
{R}_{X}
(resp., an
R
-local system
{\mathscr{L}}
on
X
) is (weakly) constructible on
X
with respect to any Whitney stratification. On the other hand, if
i\colon X\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
denotes the inclusion of the closed analytic subset
X:=\{\frac{1}{n}\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, then the direct image sheaf
i_{*}{R}_{X}
is constructible on

{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. But if
k\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
is the open inclusion, then the closure
\bar{X}=X\cup\{0\}
in
{\mathbb{C}}
is not analytic and
the direct image sheaf
k_{*}i_{*}{R}_{X}=(k\circ i)_{*}R_{X}
is not weakly constructible on
{\mathbb{C}}
.

We let
D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
be the full triangulated subcategory of
D^{b}(X;R)
consisting of (weakly) constructible complexes (that is, complexes which are (weakly) constructible with respect to
some
Whitney stratification). We will also use the simpler notation
D^{b}_{(w)c}(X)
if the coefficient ring
R
is understood from the context. Similarly for
D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-(w)c}(X;R)
in case we work with (weakly) constructible complexes with respect to a
fixed
Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
. By viewing a sheaf as a complex concentrated in degree zero, one gets an isomorphism of Grothendieck groups (and similarly for weakly constructible sheaves)

(1)

\begin{CD}K_{0}\left(Sh_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X)\right)@>{\sim}>{}>K_{0}\left(D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X;R)\right)\>,\end{CD}

whose inverse is given by taking the alternating sum of (classes of) cohomology sheaves (see
[
113
, Lemma 3.3.1]
).

Remark 2.4
.

Even in the complex analytic setting a sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}
(resp., a bounded sheaf complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
) on
X
is (weakly) constructible iff this is
locally the case, in the sense that there is an open covering
(U_{i})
of
X
such that
{\mathscr{F}}|_{U_{i}}
(resp.,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}|_{U_{i}}
) is (weakly) constructible for all
i

(see
[
31
, Proposition 4.1.13]
and compare also with
[
53
, Corollary 3.4]
). In fact, the complement of the largest open
U
of
X
such
{\mathscr{F}}|_{U}
is locally constant (resp.,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}|_{U}
has locally constant cohomology sheaves) is then an analytic subset of
X
(see
[
31
, Proposition 4.1.12]
).

The derived category
D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
of bounded (weakly) constructible complexes is closed under Grothendieck’s six operations:
Rf_{*}
,
Rf_{!}
,
f^{*}
,
f^{!}
,
R{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}
and
\overset{L}{\otimes}
.
More precisely, one has the following (e.g., see
[
11
]
or the unified treatment of
[
113
, Theorem 4.0.2 and Proposition 4.0.2]
):

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, with
j\colon U\hookrightarrow Y
the inclusion of the open complement of a closed algebraic (or analytic) subset.

(a)

If
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(Y;R)
, then
f^{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet},f^{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
.

(b)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
and
f
is an algebraic map, then
Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(Y;R)
.

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
and
f
is an analytic map so that the restriction of
f
to
{\rm supp}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is proper (e.g.,
f
is proper), then
Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(Y;R)
.

(c)

If
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(Y;R)
, then
Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet},Rj_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(Y;R)
.

(d)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
, then
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\otimes}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet},R{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
.

Similarly for weakly constructible instead of constructible complexes.

In fact, the above theorem in an application of the following more precise version, where one considers a stratified morphism with respect to given Whitney stratifications (e.g., see
[
11
]
or
[
113
, Proposition 4.0.2 and Corollary 4.2.1]
):

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a stratified morphism of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties mapping all strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
of a Whitney stratification of
X
into strata
T\in{\mathscr{T}}
of a Whitney stratification of
Y
, with
j\colon U\hookrightarrow Y
the inclusion of the open complement of a closed union of strata of
{\mathscr{T}}
(with its induced Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{T}}|_{U}
).

(a)

If
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{T}}-c}(Y;R)
, then
f^{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet},f^{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}(X;R)
.

(b)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}(X;R)
and
f
is a

stratified submersion (i.e., it maps all strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
submersively to a stratum
T\in{\mathscr{T}}
) so that the restriction of
f
to
{\rm supp}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is proper (e.g.,
f
is proper), then
Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{T}}-c}(Y;R)
.

(c)

If
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{T}}|_{U}-c}(U;R)
, then
Rj_{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet},Rj_{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{T}}-c}(Y;R)
.

(d)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}(X;R)
, then
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\otimes}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet},R{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}(X;R)
.

Similarly for weakly constructible instead of constructible complexes.

By considering the constant map
c\colon X\to pt
to a point, one gets the following.

Example 2.7
.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, and let
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of a closed algebraic (or analytic) subset given as a union of strata of
{\mathscr{S}}
with its induced Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}|_{Z}
.

(1)

The constant sheaf
R_{X}=c^{*}R
is constructible with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}
, with

H^{k}_{c}(X;R)=H^{k}(Rc_{!}c^{*}R)\quad\text{resp.,}\quad H^{k}(X;R)=H^{k}(Rc_{*}c^{*}R)\quad(k\in{\mathbb{Z}})

the corresponding
cohomology (with compact support) of
X
.

(2)

The sheaf complex
i^{!}R_{X}=i^{!}c^{*}R
is constructible with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}|_{Z}
, with

H^{k}_{Z}(X;R)=H^{k}(Rc_{*}i^{!}c^{*}R)=H^{k}(X,X\backslash Z;R)\quad(k\in{\mathbb{Z}})

the corresponding cohomology with support in
Z
of
X
(or relative cohomology of the pair
(X,X\backslash Z)
).

(3)

The
dualizing complex

{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet}:=c^{!}R
of
X
is constructible with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}
, with

H_{k}(X;R)=H^{-k}_{c}(X;{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet})=H^{-k}(Rc_{!}c^{!}R)\quad(k\in{\mathbb{Z}})

resp.,

H_{k}^{BM}(X;R)=H^{-k}(X;{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet})=H^{-k}(Rc_{*}c^{!}R)\quad(k\in{\mathbb{Z}})

the corresponding
(Borel-Moore) homology of
X
. If
X
is smooth (or more generally an
R
-homology manifold) of pure dimension
d
, then

{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet}=c^{!}R\simeq c^{*}R[2d]
, which implies the

Poincaré duality
:

H_{2d-k}(X;R)\simeq H^{k}_{c}(X;R)\quad\text{and}\quad H_{2d-k}^{BM}(X;R)\simeq H^{k}(X;R)\quad(k\in{\mathbb{Z}})\>.

Corollary 2.8

(External tensor product)
.

Let
X_{i}
be complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, with

{\mathscr{F}}_{i}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X_{i};R)
for
i=1,2
. Consider the external tensor product

{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}:=p_{1}^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\otimes}p_{2}^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X_{1}\times X_{2};R)\>,

with
p_{i}\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{i}
the projection on the corresponding factor for
i=1,2
. If
{\mathscr{F}}_{i}^{\bullet}
is (weakly) constructible
with respect to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
of
X_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then
{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}
is
(weakly) constructible with respect to the product Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}\times{\mathscr{S}}_{2}
of
X_{1}\times X_{2}
(with strata
S_{1}\times S_{2}
for

S_{1}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{1}
and
S_{2}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{2}
).

The external tensor product of (weakly) constructible complexes behaves nicely with respect to products of two morphisms, as the following result shows
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Eq. (1.16) on p.78, Proposition 2.0.1 and Corollary 2.0.4]
for more general versions).

Proposition 2.9

(Künneth isomorphisms)
.

Let
f_{k}\colon X_{k}\to Y_{k}
be two morphisms of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, with

i_{k}\colon Z_{k}\hookrightarrow X_{k}
the inclusion of a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subset, and
j_{k}\colon U_{k}:=X_{k}\backslash Z_{k}\hookrightarrow X_{k}

the inclusion of the open complement (
k=1,2
).

(1)

For any
{\mathscr{F}}_{k}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(Y_{k};R)
for
k=1,2
one has:

(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq f_{1}^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\boxtimes}f_{2}^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

(2)

For any
{\mathscr{F}}_{k}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X_{k};R)
for
k=1,2
one has:

R(f_{1}\times f_{2})_{!}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq Rf_{1!}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\boxtimes}Rf_{2!}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

(3)

For any
{\mathscr{F}}_{k}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(Y_{k};R)
for
k=1,2
one has:

(i_{1}\times i_{2})^{!}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq i_{1}^{!}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet})\right)\overset{L}{\boxtimes}i_{2}^{!}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

(4)

For any
{\mathscr{F}}_{k}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(X_{k};R)
for
k=1,2
one has in the complex algebraic context:

R(f_{1}\times f_{2})_{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq Rf_{1*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\boxtimes}Rf_{2*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

(5)

For any
{\mathscr{F}}_{k}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(X_{k};R)
for
k=1,2
one has:

R(j_{1}\times j_{2})_{*}(j_{1}\times j_{2})^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq Rj_{1*}j_{1}^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\boxtimes}Rj_{2*}j_{2}^{*}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

By taking for both morphisms
f_{k}
the constant map
f_{k}=c:X_{k}\to pt
to a point space (
k=1,2
) one gets the following.

Example 2.10

(Classical Künneth formulae)
.

For any
{\mathscr{F}}_{k}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(X_{k};R)
for
k=1,2
one has:

R\Gamma_{c}\left(X_{1}\times X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq R\Gamma_{c}\left(X_{1};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\otimes}R\Gamma_{c}\left(X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>,

R\Gamma_{Z_{1}\times Z_{2}}\left(X_{1}\times X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq R\Gamma_{Z_{1}}\left(X_{1};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\otimes}R\Gamma_{Z_{2}}\left(X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>,

and in the complex algebraic context:

R\Gamma\left(X_{1}\times X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq R\Gamma\left(X_{1};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\otimes}R\Gamma\left(X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

In the special case when
R
is a field, one further gets

H^{*}_{c}\left(X_{1}\times X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq H^{*}_{c}\left(X_{1};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\otimes H^{*}_{c}\left(X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>,

and in the complex algebraic context also

H^{*}\left(X_{1}\times X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\boxtimes{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq H^{*}\left(X_{1};{\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\otimes H^{*}\left(X_{2};{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

Another important application of the general calculus of (weakly) constructible complexes deals with
Verdier duality

(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Corollary 4.2.2]
):

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, with morphisms
f\colon Z\to X
and
g\colon X\to Y
.

(1)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is
{\mathscr{S}}
-(weakly) constructible, then its
Verdier dual

{\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}:=R{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet})

is also
{\mathscr{S}}
-(weakly) constructible, with

f^{!}\left({\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq{\mathscr{D}}\left(f^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\quad\text{resp.,}\quad Rg_{*}\left({\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq{\mathscr{D}}\left(Rg_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

(2)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is constructible, then
biduality holds:

(2)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq{\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

so that
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is (
{\mathscr{S}}
-)constructible iff its Verdier dual
{\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is (
{\mathscr{S}}
-)constructible.
Moreover

f^{*}\left({\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq{\mathscr{D}}\left(f^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\quad\text{resp.,}\quad Rg_{!}\left({\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq{\mathscr{D}}\left(Rg_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

Here, for the last isomorphism one has to assume that
Rg_{!}\left({\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)
is constructible (e.g., as in the algebraic context).

Note that already for a point space
X=\{pt\}
the biduality result uses our assumption that
R
is a noetherian and commutative ring of finite global dimension (see also
[
67
, Exercise I.30]
). Similarly, Verdier duality commutes for constructible sheaf complexes with external tensor products
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Corollary 2.0.4]
in a more general context).

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
X_{i}
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety with
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}_{i}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
for
i=1,2
. Then

{\mathscr{D}}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\simeq{\mathscr{D}}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{1}^{\bullet}\right)\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{D}}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{2}^{\bullet}\right)\>.

In particular,
{\mathbb{D}}_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}^{\bullet}\simeq{\mathbb{D}}_{X_{1}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathbb{D}}_{X_{2}}^{\bullet}
, so that one gets the classical Künneth formula for homology, e.g., for
R
a field:

H_{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2};R)\simeq H_{*}(X_{1};R)\otimes H_{*}(X_{2};R)\>,

and in the algebraic context also for Borel Moore homology, e.g., for
R
a field:

H^{BM}_{*}(X_{1}\times X_{2};R)\simeq H^{BM}_{*}(X_{1};R)\otimes H^{BM}_{*}(X_{2};R)\>.

The general calculus of constructible sheaves also includes finiteness results for the cohomology (with compact support) of constructible sheaf complexes.

Corollary 2.13
.

Assume that
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
and that either

(a)

X
is a complex algebraic variety, or

(b)

X
is an analytic space and
{\rm supp}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is compact.

Then the hypercohomology groups
H^{i}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
and
H_{c}^{i}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
are finite type
R
-modules for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(which are zero for
|i|
large enough).

Assume
X
is a compact analytic space with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
so that
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion of the open complement of a closed union of strata. If
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}-c}(U;R)
, then also
the hypercohomology groups
H^{i}(U;{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})
and
H_{c}^{i}(U;{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})
are finite type
R
-modules for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
(which are zero for
|i|
large enough).

With
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
as in the above corollary, we make the following.

Definition 2.14
.

Assume
R
is a field. The
(compactly supported) Euler characteristic of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
is defined as:

\chi_{(c)}(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}):=\chi(H^{*}_{(c)}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})):=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{i}\dim_{R}H_{(c)}^{i}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}).

(Here, we use the notation
\chi_{(c)}
and
H_{(c)}^{i}
to indicate that the definition applies to the compactly supported Euler characteristic
\chi_{c}
by using
H_{c}^{i}
, as well as to the usual Euler characteristic
\chi
by using
H^{i}
.)

As it will be explained later on (see Example
4.37
, and also
[
113
, Section 2.3 and Section 6.0.6]
), in this complex context we have the equality:

(3)

\chi(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=\chi_{c}(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}).

Moreover, this Euler characteristic depends only on the associated constructible function

(4)

\chi_{stalk}\left({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)\in CF(X)

given by the stalkwise Euler characteristic
\chi_{stalk}\left({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)(x):=\chi\left({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}_{x}\right)

for
x\in X
, with
CF(X)
the corresponding abelian group of constructible functions given by (locally) finite
{\mathbb{Z}}
-linear combinations of indicator functions
1_{Z}
, for
Z\subset X
a closed irreducible algebraic (or analytic)
subset of
X
. Similarly for the abelian group
CF_{{\mathscr{S}}}(X)
of
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible functions given by (locally) finite
{\mathbb{Z}}
-linear combinations of indicator functions
1_{S}
or
1_{\bar{S}}
for
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
a stratum, i.e.,
{\mathbb{Z}}
-valued functions which are constant on all strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
. This
induces a surjective group homomorphism

(5)

\chi_{stalk}\colon\>K_{0}\left(D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X;R)\right)\to CF_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X)\>,

with

(6)

\chi_{c}(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=\sum_{S\in{\mathscr{S}}}\>\chi_{c}(S)\cdot\chi_{stalk}\left({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\right)(S)

for an
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
on
X
in the complex algebraic context (or analytic context with
X
compact).
Here
\chi_{c}(S):=\chi_{c}(R_{S})=\chi(H_{c}^{*}(S;R))
is the corresponding Euler characteristic of a stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
.

Let us also mention here the following (co)stalk calculation.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}}\in D_{c}^{b}(X;R)
,
x\in X
, and
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion. Then

(7)

\mathscr{H}^{j}({\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}})_{x}\simeq{H}^{j}(i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}})\simeq H^{j}(\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x};\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}),

(8)

{H}^{j}(i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}})\simeq{H}_{c}^{j}(\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x};\mathscr{F}^{\bullet})\simeq{H}^{j}(\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x},\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x}\setminus{x};\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}),

where
\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x}
is the intersection of
X
with an open small
\epsilon
-ball neighborhood of
x
in some local embedding
of
X
in
\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Here,
i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
and
i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
are called the stalk and, respectively, costalk of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
at
x
.

In fact, this will be an easy application of the Morse theoretical results explained later on, since the proper real analytic function
r
given by the squared distance to
x

(in these local coordinates) has no stratified critical values in an interval
\,]0,\epsilon[\,
for
\epsilon>0
small enough
(see Lemma
3.3
and, e.g.,
[
113
, Lemma 5.1.1]
).

2.2.
Perverse sheaves

Perverse sheaves are an important class of constructible complexes, introduced in
[
6
]
as a formalization of Kashiwara’s Riemann–Hilbert correspondence
[
65
]
(see also
[
58
]
), which relates the topology of complex algebraic, resp., analytic varieties (intersection homology) and the algebraic, resp., analytic theory of differential equations (holonomic
D
-modules). We recall their definition below.

Definition 2.16
.

(a) The
perverse t-structure
on
D_{c}^{b}(X;R)
consists of the two strictly full subcategories
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
of
D_{c}^{b}(X;R)
defined as:

{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R):=\{{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)\mid\dim{\rm supp}^{-j}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\leq j,\forall j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\},

{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R):=\{{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)\mid\dim{\rm cosupp}^{j}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\leq j,\forall j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\},

where, for
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
denoting the point inclusion, we define the
j
-th support
and, respectively, the
j
-th cosupport
of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
by:

{\rm supp}^{j}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=\overline{\{x\in X\mid H^{j}(i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\neq 0\}},

{\rm cosupp}^{j}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=\overline{\{x\in X\mid H^{j}(i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\neq 0\}}.

(For a constructible complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
, the sets
\mathrm{supp}^{j}(\mathscr{F}^{\bullet})
and
\mathrm{cosupp}^{j}(\mathscr{F}^{\bullet})
are closed algebraic (or analytic) subvarieties of
X
, hence their dimensions are well defined.)

(b) For a given Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
this also induces the
perverse t-structure
on
D_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}^{b}(X;R)
with

{{}^{p}D}_{{\mathscr{S}}}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
, resp.,
{{}^{p}D}_{{\mathscr{S}}}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
defined by the same (co)support conditions.

(c) A (
{\mathscr{S}}
-)constructible complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X;R)
is called a
perverse sheaf
on
X
if

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in Perv_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R):={{}^{p}D}_{({\mathscr{S}})}^{\leq 0}(X;R)\cap{{}^{p}D}_{({\mathscr{S}})}^{\geq 0}(X;R).

The category of (
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible) perverse sheaves is the
heart
of the perverse t-structure, hence it is an abelian category, and it is stable by extensions (see, e.g.,
[
6
, Theorem 1.3.6]
).

Remark 2.17
.

(a) The same definition also defines the
perverse t-structure
for (
{\mathscr{S}}
-)

weakly constructible complexes with heart the abelian category of
(
{\mathscr{S}}
-)weakly constructible perverse sheaves.

(b) An algebraically (weakly) constructible complex is perverse if and only if it is so when viewed as an analytically (weakly) constructible complex.

(c)
If
R
is a
field
, the Universal Coefficient Theorem can be used to show that the Verdier duality functor
{\mathscr{D}}\colon D^{b}_{c}(X;R)\to D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
satisfies:

(9)

{\rm cosupp}^{j}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})={\rm supp}^{-j}({\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}),

In particular,
{\mathscr{D}}
exchanges
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
, so that it
preserves (
{\mathscr{S}}
-) constructible perverse sheaves with field coefficients.

Recall here that two subcategories
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
of
D_{({\mathscr{S}}-w)c}^{b}(X;R)
define a
t-structure
just means (see, e.g.,
[
6
, Definition 1.3.1]
):

(1)

{\rm{Hom}}_{D^{b}(X;R)}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}[-1])=0
for all
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
and
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
.

(2)

{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
is stable under
[1]
, and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
is stable under
[-1]
.

(3)

For any
{\mathscr{E}}^{\bullet}\in D_{({\mathscr{S}}-w)c}^{b}(X;R)
there is a distinguished triangle

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{E}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}[-1]\overset{[1]}{\longrightarrow}

for some
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
and
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
.

Then it is enough to check these properties for a fixed Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, where they can be proved by induction on
\dim X
via the

gluing of t-structures
as in
[
6
, Corollary 2.1.4, Proposition 2.1.14]
.
Here it is important to note that the conditions
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
and, resp.,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
can also be described in terms of a fixed Whitney stratification of
X
for which
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is
{\mathscr{S}}
-(weakly) constructible. Indeed, the perverse t-structure can be characterized as follows:

Theorem 2.18
.

Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
is (weakly) constructible with respect to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
. Then:

(i)

stalk vanishing:

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)

\iff

\forall S\in{\mathscr{S}}
,
\forall x\in S
:
H^{j}(i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
for all
j>-\dim S
.

(ii)

costalk vanishing:

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)

\iff

\forall S\in{\mathscr{S}}
,
\forall x\in S
:
H^{j}(i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
for all
j<\dim S
.

Of course the isomorphism class of the stalk
i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
for
x\in S
as above does not depend on the choice of the point
x\in S
. In fact, if
i_{S}\colon S\hookrightarrow X
denotes the inclusion of the stratum
S
, the stalk vanishing condition is equivalent to

(i’)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)

\iff

\forall S\in{\mathscr{S}}
:
{\mathscr{H}}^{j}(i_{S}^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
for all
j>-\dim S
.

Similarly for the costalk
i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
for
x\in S
, since this costalk is also isomorphic to
k_{x}^{!}i_{S}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
with
k_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow S
the inclusion of the point
\{x\}
into
S
. But
i_{S}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
has locally constant cohomology sheaves so that

k_{x}^{!}i_{S}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[2\dim S]\simeq k_{x}^{*}i_{S}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
for all
x\in S
. And then the costalk condition is equivalent to

(ii’)

\;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)

\iff

\forall S\in{\mathscr{S}}
:
{\mathscr{H}}^{j}(i_{S}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
for all
j<-\dim S
.

Example 2.19
.

Assume
X
is of pure complex dimension with
c\colon X\to pt
the constant map to a point space. As usual, for an
R
-module
M
we denote by
M_{X}=c^{*}M
the constant
R
-sheaf on
X
with stalk
M_{x}=M
for all
x\in X
. Then:

(a)

{M}_{X}[\dim X]\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
. More generally, if
{\mathscr{L}}
is a local system on
X
, then
{\mathscr{L}}[\dim X]\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)

(since this is a local condition).

(b)

If
X
is smooth with the trivial stratification, and
{\mathscr{L}}
is a local system on
X
, then
{\mathscr{L}}[\dim X]
is perverse on
X

(since in this case
i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{L}}[2\dim X]\simeq i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{L}}
for all
x\in X
).

(c)

The
intersection (
IC
) complexes
on
X
of Goresky-MacPherson
[
46
]
(for the middle perversity) are examples of perverse sheaves. For a given Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
these are defined by further imposing, for all strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
with
\dim S<\dim X
, the stronger stalk/costalk vanishing conditions in Theorem
2.18
obtained by replacing
>
with
\geq
and
<
with
\leq
. Such an
IC
complex on
X
is
{\mathscr{S}}
-weakly constructible and determined by its restriction to the top dimensional strata, which is (isomorphic to) a shifted local system
{\mathscr{L}}[\dim X]
, so we may denote it unambiguously by
IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})
. It is
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible if
{\mathscr{L}}
has finitely generated stalks. If
{\mathscr{L}}
is the constant sheaf
R
on the top dimensional strata, we use the notation
IC_{X}
. In the end, these perverse
IC
-complexes do not depend on the chosen Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, but only on the

generically defined
local system
{\mathscr{L}}
(on the complement of a closed algebraic or analytic subset of
X
of dimension smaller than

\dim X
).

(d)

If
X
is a local complete intersection then
{R}_{X}[\dim X]
is a perverse sheaf on
X
(see Example
4.29
and, e.g.,

[
113
, Example 6.0.11]
). More generally, if
{\mathscr{L}}
is a local system on
X
, then
{\mathscr{L}}[\dim X]
is perverse on
X
. By (a) one only has to show that
{\mathscr{L}}[\dim X]\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
. Since this is a local condition in the classical topology, we can assume
{\mathscr{L}}=M_{X}
is a constant sheaf, with
i\colon X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
the inclusion of a closed analytic subset defined as the zero set of
k
holomorphic functions on a pure dimensional complex manifold
X^{\prime}
with
\dim X^{\prime}-k=\dim X
. Then
M_{X}=i^{*}M_{X^{\prime}}
with

M_{X^{\prime}}[\dim X^{\prime}]\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X^{\prime};R)
by (b), so that the claim follows from Proposition
4.28
below (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Proposition 6.0.2]
).

To simplify the formulation of some results, let us recall the following.

Definition 2.20
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, define the following two strictly full subcategories
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
of

D_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}^{b}(X;R)
,
resp.,
D_{({\mathscr{S}}-)wc}^{b}(X;R)
by

{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R):={{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)[-n]\quad\text{and}\quad{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R):={{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)[-n]

so that
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq-1}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)\subset{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 1}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)\subset{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
.

Remark 2.21
.

The two subcategories
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
define a

shifted perverse t-structure
on

D_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}^{b}(X;R)

and
D_{({\mathscr{S}}-)wc}^{b}(X;R)
. But for most of the results of this paper (especially those proved by stratified Morse theory later on), we only need the following obvious properties (as in
[
113
, Chapter VI]
):

(1)

The zero object belongs to
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
.

(2)

{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
are stable by extensions.

(3)

{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}(X;R)
is stable under
[1]
, and
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}(X;R)
is stable under
[-1]
.

Example 2.22
.

On a point space
X=\{pt\}
one gets:

(1)

{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}(\{pt\};R)
is given by the bounded complexes of
R
-modules, whose cohomology is concentrated in degree
\leq n
.
This condition is stable under shifting a complex to the left (i.e., the shift
[1]
).

(2)

{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}(\{pt\};R)
is given by the bounded complexes of
R
-modules, whose cohomology is concentrated in degree
\geq n
.
This condition is stable under shifting a complex to the right (i.e., the shift
[-1]
).

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, whose
fiber dimension is bounded by
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Then:

(a)

f^{!}
maps
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}(Y;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n-d}(X;R)
.

(b)

f^{*}
maps
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}(Y;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n+d}(X;R)
.

This follows from the definitions using
i_{x}^{!}f^{!}\simeq i_{f(x)}^{!}
and
i_{x}^{*}f^{*}\simeq i_{f(x)}^{*}
for all
x\in X
.
As an example, one can take for
f
a locally closed inclusion or an unramified covering map, both of which have fiber dimension
d=0
.

Example 2.24
.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety of dimension
d=\dim X
, with
c\colon X\to pt
a constant map. Then

{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet}=c^{!}R\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq-d}(X;R)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{X}=c^{*}R\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq d}(X;R)\>.

Corollary 2.25
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a
smooth morphism (i.e., a submersion) of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, with constant relative (or fiber) dimension

d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Then
f^{!}\simeq f^{*}[2d]
so that

(a)

f^{*}
maps
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}(Y;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n+d}(X;R)
.

(b)

f^{!}
maps
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}(Y;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n-d}(X;R)
.

In particular
f^{!}[-d]\simeq f^{*}[d]
maps
Perv(Y;R)
into
Perv(X;R)
.

Example 2.26
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a
smooth
morphism (i.e., a submersion) of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, with constant relative (or fiber) dimension

d
. Assume
f
is surjective and
Y
(and then also
X
) is pure dimensional. Then

f^{*}IC_{Y}({\mathscr{L}})[d]\simeq IC_{X}(f^{*}{\mathscr{L}})

for a generically defined local system
{\mathscr{L}}
on
Y
, with
f^{*}{\mathscr{L}}
the corresponding generically defined local system on
X
defined by pullback.

The following result will be very important for the applications of the
stratified Morse theory for constructible sheaves
in the next sections.

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
Y\hookrightarrow M
be a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subvariety of an ambient complex algebraic (or analytic) manifold

M
.
Assume
N\hookrightarrow M
is a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) submanifold of constant codimension
d=\dim M-\dim N
, which is

transversal to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
Y

(i.e.,
N
is transversal to all strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
). Then
X:=Y\cap N
gets an induced Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}
with strata
S^{\prime}
the connected components of the intersections
S\cap N
for
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
, with
\dim S\cap N=\dim S-d
for all
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
(and
S\cap N\neq\emptyset
). Let
i\colon X=Y\cap N\hookrightarrow Y
be the (stratified) closed inclusion. Then

(a)

i^{*}
maps
{{}^{p}D}_{{\mathscr{S}}}^{\leq n}(Y;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n-d}_{{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}}(X;R)
.

(b)

i^{*}
maps
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}_{{\mathscr{S}}}(Y;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n-d}_{{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}}(X;R)
.

In particular
i^{*}[-d]
maps
Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}}(Y;R)
into
Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}^{\prime}}(X;R)
.

Proof.

Consider the following cartesian diagram of closed inclusions for
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
:

\begin{CD}S^{\prime}:=S\cap N@>{i_{S^{\prime}}}>{}>X=Y\cap N\\
@V{i^{\prime}}V{}V@V{}V{i}V\\
S@>{i_{S}}>{}>Y\>.\end{CD}

Then (a) follows from
i^{*}_{S^{\prime}}i^{*}\simeq i^{\prime*}i^{*}_{S}
for checking the stalk vanishing condition (i’).
Similarly (b) follows from the
base change isomorphism

(10)

i^{!}_{S^{\prime}}i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq i^{\prime*}i^{!}_{S}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\quad\text{for ${\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-wc}(X;R)$}

for checking the costalk vanishing condition (ii’).
∎

Example 2.28
.

Consider the context of Proposition
2.27
, with
Y
(and therefore also
X=Y\cap N
) pure dimensional. Let
{\mathscr{L}}
be a local system on the open subset
U
of
Y
given by the top dimensional stratum of
{\mathscr{S}}
(which is then dense in
Y
). Similarly
U\cap N
is open and dense in
X
. Then

i^{*}IC_{Y}({\mathscr{L}})[-d]\simeq IC_{X}(i^{\prime*}{\mathscr{L}})\>,

with
i^{\prime}\colon U\cap N\to U
the induced inclusion.

In the base change isomorphism (
10
) used above, we can even assume that
S\hookrightarrow Y
is a
closed
stratum, by restriction to the open complement of
\partial S
. Then it is a special case (with
Y^{\prime}=S
) of the following more general result
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Proposition 4.3.1 and Remark 4.3.6]
).

Theorem 2.29

(Base change isomorphisms)
.

Let
Y\hookrightarrow M
be a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subvariety of an ambient complex algebraic (or analytic) manifold
M
.
Assume
N\hookrightarrow M
is a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) submanifold, which is transversal to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
Y
.
Let
i\colon Y^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y
be the inclusion of a closed union of strata of
{\mathscr{S}}
, with
j\colon U:=Y\backslash Y^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y
the inclusion of the open complement with its induced stratification
{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}
. Consider the cartesian diagram

\begin{CD}X^{\prime}:=Y^{\prime}\cap N@>{i^{\prime}}>{}>X:=Y\cap N@<{j^{\prime}}<{}<U^{\prime}:=U\cap N\\
@V{}V{k^{\prime}}V@V{}V{k}V@V{}V{k^{\prime\prime}}V\\
Y^{\prime}@>{i}>{}>Y@<{j}<{}<U\>.\end{CD}

Then one has the following base change isomorphisms:

(11)

k^{*}Rj_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq Rj^{\prime}_{*}k^{\prime\prime*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\quad\text{for ${\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}-wc}(U;R)$}

and

(12)

k^{\prime*}i^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq i^{\prime!}k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\quad\text{for ${\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-wc}(Y;R)$.}

Next we study the relation between the
perverse t-structure
and
external tensor products
.

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
X_{i}
be complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties (
i=1,2
). Then

(a)

The external tensor product
\overset{L}{\boxtimes}
induces

\overset{L}{\boxtimes}:\>{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}(X_{1};R)\times{{}^{p}D}^{\leq m}(X_{2};R)\to{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n+m}(X_{1}\times X_{2};R)\>.

(b)

Assume
R
is a field. Thent
\overset{L}{\boxtimes}
induces

\overset{L}{\boxtimes}:\>{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}(X_{1};R)\times{{}^{p}D}^{\geq m}(X_{2};R)\to{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n+m}(X_{1}\times X_{2};R)\>.

In particular, if
R
is a field,
\overset{L}{\boxtimes}
induces

\overset{L}{\boxtimes}:\>Perv(X_{1};R)\times Perv(X_{2};R)\to Perv(X_{1}\times X_{2};R)\>.

Property (a) follows from
i_{(x_{1},x_{2})}^{*}(-\overset{L}{\boxtimes}-)\simeq i_{x_{1}}^{*}(-)\overset{L}{\otimes}i_{x_{2}}^{*}(-)
and the right exactness
of the tensor product
\otimes
. Property (b) is a consequence of the Künneth isomorphism
i_{(x_{1},x_{2})}^{!}(-\overset{L}{\boxtimes}-)\simeq i_{x_{1}}^{!}(-)\overset{L}{\otimes}i_{x_{2}}^{!}(-)
and the exactness
of the tensor product
\otimes
for
R
a field.

Example 2.31
.

Let
X_{i}
be pure dimensional complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties (
i=1,2
), with
R
a field. Then

IC_{X_{1}}({\mathscr{L}}_{1})\overset{L}{\boxtimes}IC_{X_{2}}({\mathscr{L}}_{2})\simeq IC_{X_{1}\times X_{2}}({\mathscr{L}}_{1}\boxtimes{\mathscr{L}}_{2})

for a generically defined local system
{\mathscr{L}}_{i}
on
X_{i}
(
i=1,2
).

Example 2.32
.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety, with
R
a field.
If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in Perv(X;R)
and
{\mathscr{L}}
is a locally constant sheaf on
X
, then

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}\in Perv(X;R)
. In particular, if
X
is also pure dimensional, one gets:

IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}}_{1})\otimes{\mathscr{L}}_{2}\simeq IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}}_{1}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}_{2}),

with
{\mathscr{L}}_{1}
a generically defined local system on
X
, and
{\mathscr{L}}_{2}
a local system on all of
X
. As a special case, one also gets:

IC_{X}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}\simeq IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})

if
X
is pure dimensional and
{\mathscr{L}}
is a local system on
X
.
These assertions can be checked locally in the analytic topology (so it suffices to assume that
{\mathscr{L}}_{2}
is a constant sheaf), by using Proposition
2.30
and Example
2.31
in which we take
X_{2}
to be a point space.

Corollary 2.33
.

Let
X_{i}\hookrightarrow M
be closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subvarieties of the complex algebraic (or analytic) manifold
M
of pure dimension
d
(
i=1,2
). Let
{\mathscr{S}}_{i}
be Whitney stratifications of
X_{i}
(
i=1,2
) which are
transversal in
M
, i.e., all strata
S_{1}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{1}
are transversal to all strata
S_{2}\in{\mathscr{S}}_{2}
. This is equivalent to the diagonal embedding
\Delta\colon M\to M\times M
being transversal to the product stratification
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}\times{\mathscr{S}}_{2}
of
X_{1}\times X_{2}
. In particular,
X_{1}\cap X_{2}\simeq\left(X_{1}\times X_{2}\right)\cap\Delta(M)
gets an induced Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}\cap{\mathscr{S}}_{2}
with strata the connected components of the intersections
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
. Then one gets for the induced map
\Delta\colon X_{1}\cap X_{2}\to X_{1}\times X_{2}
the following:

(a)

The tensor product
\overset{L}{-\otimes-}\simeq\Delta^{*}(-\overset{L}{\boxtimes-})
induces

\overset{L}{\otimes}:\>{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n}_{{\mathscr{S}}_{1}}(X_{1};R)\times{{}^{p}D}^{\leq m}_{{\mathscr{S}}_{2}}(X_{2};R)\to{{}^{p}D}^{\leq n+m-d}_{{\mathscr{S}}_{1}\cap{\mathscr{S}}_{2}}(X_{1}\cap X_{2};R)\>.

(b)

Assume
R
is a field. Then the tensor product
\overset{L}{-\otimes-}\simeq\Delta^{*}(-\overset{L}{\boxtimes-})
induces

\overset{L}{\otimes}:\>{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n}_{{\mathscr{S}}_{1}}(X_{1};R)\times{{}^{p}D}^{\geq m}_{{\mathscr{S}}_{2}}(X_{2};R)\to{{}^{p}D}^{\geq n+m-d}_{{\mathscr{S}}_{1}\cap{\mathscr{S}}_{2}}(X_{1}\cap X_{2};R)\>.

In particular, if
R
is a field,
\left(-\overset{L}{\otimes}-\right)[-d]
induces

Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}_{1}}(X_{1};R)\times Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}_{2}}(X_{2};R)\to Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}_{1}\cap{\mathscr{S}}_{2}}(X_{1}\cap X_{2};R)\>.

Example 2.34
.

In the context of Corollary
2.33
, let
X_{i}
be in addition pure dimensional (
i=1,2
), with
R
a field. Then

\left(IC_{X_{1}}({\mathscr{L}}_{1})\overset{L}{\otimes}IC_{X_{2}}({\mathscr{L}}_{2})\right)[-d]\simeq IC_{X_{1}\cap X_{2}}({\mathscr{L}}_{1}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}_{2})

for a local system
{\mathscr{L}}_{i}
defined on the open dense subset
U_{i}
of
X_{i}
given by the top dimensional stratum (
i=1,2
).
Here
X_{1}\cap X_{2}
is also pure dimensional with
{\mathscr{L}}_{1}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}_{2}
defined on the open dense subset
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
of

X_{1}\cap X_{2}
.

The existence of the perverse t-structure on
D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
implies the existence of
perverse truncation functors

{{}^{p}\tau}_{\leq 0},{{}^{p}\tau}_{\geq 0}
, which are adjoint to the inclusions

{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)\hookrightarrow D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)\hookleftarrow{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)\>.

In particular, for every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, there are adjunction maps
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}\to{}^{p}\tau_{\geq k}\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
and
{}^{p}\tau_{\leq k}\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}\to\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
(see, e.g.,
[
6
, Proposition 1.3.3]
).
These perverse truncation functors can be used to associate to any constructible complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
its
perverse cohomology
sheaves defined as:

{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}):={{}^{p}\tau}_{\leq 0}{{}^{p}\tau}_{\geq 0}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[i])\in Perv(X;R).

These are
{\mathscr{S}}
-(weakly) constructible for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

{\mathscr{S}}
-(weakly) constructible.
It then follows that
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
if and only if
{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
for all
i>0
. Similarly,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
if and only if
{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
for all
i<0
. In particular,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in Perv(X;R)
if and only if
{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
for all
i\neq 0
and
{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{0}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})={\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

(see, e.g.,
[
6
, Proposition 1.3.7]
). Perverse cohomology sheaves can be used to calculate the (hyper)cohomology groups of any
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}\in D_{(w)c}^{b}(X)
via the
perverse cohomology spectral sequence

(13)

E_{2}^{i,j}=H^{i}(X;{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}^{j}}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}))\Longrightarrow H^{i+j}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}).

Definition 2.35
.

A functor
F\colon D_{1}\to D_{2}
of triangulated categories with t-structures is
left t-exact
if
F(D_{1}^{\geq 0})\subseteq D_{2}^{\geq 0}
,
right t-exact
if
F(D_{1}^{\leq 0})\subseteq D_{2}^{\leq 0}
, and
t-exact
if
F
is both left and right t-exact.

Example 2.36
.

The inclusion of full subcategories

D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-(w)c}(X;R)\subset D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)\quad\text{and}\quad D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)wc}(X;R)\subset D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X;R)

are t-exact with respect to the perverse t-structures.

Remark 2.37
.

If
F
is a t-exact functor, it restricts to a functor on the corresponding hearts. More generally, if
F\colon D_{1}\to D_{2}
is a functor of triangulated categories with t-structures, and we let

\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
be the corresponding hearts with
k_{i}\colon\mathcal{C}_{i}\hookrightarrow D_{i}
, then

{}^{p}F:=\ ^{t}H^{0}\circ F\circ k_{1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{2}

is called the
perverse functor associated to
F
. (Here, if
\tau_{\leq 0}
and
\tau_{\geq 0}
are the truncation functors on a triangulated category
D
with heart
\mathcal{C}
, we set
{}^{t}H^{0}:=\tau_{\geq 0}\tau_{\leq 0}=\tau_{\leq 0}\tau_{\geq 0}\colon D\to\mathcal{C}
.)
In this paper we work only with the perverse t-structure, so a t-exact functor preserves perverse sheaves.

Example 2.38
.

Let
X
be a complex analytic (or algebraic) variety, and let
Z\subseteq X
be a closed subset. Fix a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of the pair
(X,Z)
, i.e.,
Z
is a union of strata of
{\mathscr{S}}
. Then
Z
and
U:=X\setminus Z
inherit Whitney stratifications as well, and if we denote by
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
and
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
the stratified inclusion maps, then the functors
j^{*}=j^{!}
,
i^{!}
,
i^{*}
,
i_{*}=i_{!}
,
j_{!}
and
Rj_{*}
preserve (weak) constructibility with respect to the above fixed stratifications, with
j^{*}i_{!}=0
and
i^{!}Rj_{*}=0
. Moreover, the functors
j_{!}
,
i^{*}
are right t-exact, the functors
j^{!}=j^{*}
,
i_{*}=i_{!}
are t-exact, and
Rj_{*}
,
i^{!}
are left t-exact. Similarly for the functors
j^{*}=j^{!}
,
i^{!}
,
i^{*}
,
i_{*}=i_{!}
,
j_{!}j^{*}
and
Rj_{*}j^{*}
(as well as
j_{!}
and
Rj_{*}
in the complex algebraic context), if we do not fix a Whitney stratification.

Example 2.39
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a
smooth
morphism (i.e., a submersion) of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, with constant relative (or fiber) dimension

d
. Then
f^{!}[-d]\simeq f^{*}[d]
is t-exact. Similarly, the external tensor product
\overset{L}{\boxtimes}
is t-exact in each variable in case
R
is a field.

Example 2.40
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a finite map (i.e., proper, with finite fibers). Then
Rf_{*}=f_{!}
is t-exact (see Example
3.31
). If, moreover,
X
is pure dimensional
with
f
surjective and generically bijective, then
Rf_{*}IC_{X}\simeq f_{*}IC_{X}\simeq IC_{Y}
. The latter fact applies, in particular, to the case when
X
is the (algebraic) normalization of
Y
.

The perverse cohomology sheaf construction provides a way to get perverse sheaves out of any (weakly) constructible complex. Another important method for constructing perverse sheaves, the
intermediate extension
, will be discussed below.

Let
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of an open constructible subset of the complex algebraic (or analytic) variety
X
, with
i\colon Z=X\setminus U\hookrightarrow X
the closed inclusion. We can also work with a fixed Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
so that
U
is an open union of strata of
{\mathscr{S}}
. A complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
is a (weakly) constructible
extension
of
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(U;R)
if
j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
. In what follows, we are interested to find perverse extensions of
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in Perv_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{U})}(U;R)
.

Definition 2.41
.

The
intermediate extension
of the perverse sheaf
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in Perv_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{U})}(U;R)
is the image
j_{!*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
in the abelian category
Perv_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
of the morphism

{{}^{p}j}_{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}:={{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{0}\left(j_{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\right)\to{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{0}\left(Rj_{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\right)=:{{}^{p}j}_{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}.

Here,
{{}^{p}j}_{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\to{{}^{p}j}_{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
is obtained by applying the functor
{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{0}
to the natural morphism
j_{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\to Rj_{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
in

D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
. In the analytic context we explicitly have to assume that
j_{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
(and then also
Rj_{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\simeq Rj_{*}j^{*}j_{!}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
) is (weakly) constructible, e.g.,
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in Perv(U;R)
is (weakly) constructible with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}
.

Example 2.42
.

If
X
is of pure dimension
n
, and
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion of a smooth open subset whose complement is an algebraic (or analytic) subset of dimension
<\dim X
, then for a local system
{\mathscr{L}}
on
U
one has that
{\mathscr{L}}[n]\in Perv(U;R)
and

IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})\simeq j_{!*}({\mathscr{L}}[n])\>.

Example 2.43
.

Let
X
be smooth of pure dimension one and let
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of a Zariski open and dense subset. If
{\mathscr{L}}
is a local system on
U
, then:

IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})\simeq j_{!*}({\mathscr{L}}[1])\simeq(j_{*}{\mathscr{L}})[1]\>.

The intermediate extension functor plays an important role in describing the simple objects in the abelian category of (
{\mathscr{S}}-)
constructible perverse sheaves on
X
. Indeed, we have the following.

Proposition 2.44
.

Consider the context of Definition
2.41
above.

(a)

The intermediate extension
j_{!*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
of
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in Perv_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{U})}(U;R)
has no non-trivial sub-object and no non-trivial quotient object whose supports are contained in
Z=X\setminus U
.

(b)

Moreover, if
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in Perv_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{U})}(U;R)
is a simple constructible object then
j_{!*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in Perv_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X;R)
is also simple constructible.

Moreover, the following important result holds, see
[
6
, Theorem 4.3.1]
(where the assumptions are needed for the use of biduality and the stability of perverse sheaves under duality):

Theorem 2.45
.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic variety, assume that the coefficient ring
R
is a field, and consider (constructible)
perverse sheaves
Perv(X;R)\subset D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
.

(a)

The category of perverse sheaves
Perv(X;R)
is artinian and noetherian, i.e., every perverse sheaf on
X
admits an increasing finite filtration with quotients simple perverse sheaves
.

(b)

The simple
R
-perverse sheaves on
X
are the twisted intersection complexes
IC_{\overline{V}}({\mathscr{L}})
(regarded as complexes on
X
via extension by zero), where
V
runs through the family of smooth connected constructible subvarieties of
X
,
{\mathscr{L}}
is a simple (i.e., irreducible)
R
-local system of finite rank on
V
, and
\overline{V}
is the closure of
V
in
X
.

Remark 2.46
.

A similar result as in Theorem
2.45
is also true for a compact analytic variety, or if one works in the algebraic or analytic context with
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible perverse sheaves
Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}}(X;R)\subset D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}(X;R)
for
a fixed Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
with only
finitely
many strata. In the later case one needs to use in (b) for
V
only the strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
.

Example 2.47
.

Let
R
be a field of coefficients, and let
X={\mathbb{C}}
,
U={\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with open inclusion
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
, and
Z=\{0\}
with closed inclusion
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
. Let
{\mathscr{L}}
be a local system of finite rank on
U
with stalk
V
and monodromy automorphism
h\colon V\to V
. Then
{\mathscr{L}}[1]\in Perv(U;R)
and
IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})\simeq j_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]
. Moreover, it is an instructive exercise to show that the following assertions hold:

(a)

j_{!}{\mathscr{L}}[1]
and
Rj_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]
are perverse sheaves on
X
.

(b)

There is a short exact sequence of perverse sheaves on
X
:

(14)

0\longrightarrow IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})\longrightarrow Rj_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]\longrightarrow i_{!}IC_{Z}(V_{h})\longrightarrow 0\ ,

where
V_{h}=\mathrm{Coker}~(h-1)
. In particular, if the local system
{\mathscr{L}}
is simple, then the perverse sheaf
Rj_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]
admits the filtration

IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})\subset Rj_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]

with simple quotients
IC_{Z}({V_{h}})
and
IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})
.

(c)

There is a short exact sequence of perverse sheaves on
X
:

(15)

0\longrightarrow i_{!}IC_{Z}(V^{h})\longrightarrow j_{!}{\mathscr{L}}[1]\longrightarrow IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})\longrightarrow 0\ ,

where
V^{h}=\mathrm{Ker}~(h-1)
. Hence, if the local system
{\mathscr{L}}
is simple, then the perverse sheaf
j_{!}{\mathscr{L}}[1]
admits the filtration

IC_{Z}(V^{h})\subset Rj_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]

with simple quotients
IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})
and
IC_{Z}({V^{h}})
.

The next result describes the behavior of the intermediate extension with respect to the dualizing functor (see, e.g.,
[
92
, Proposition 8.4.15]
).

Proposition 2.48
.

Consider the context of Definition
2.41

with coefficient ring
R
a field, and let
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in Perv_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{U})}(U;R)
be (
{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}
-)constructible. Then

(16)

{\mathscr{D}}(j_{!*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})\simeq j_{!*}({\mathscr{D}}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}).

In particular,
{\mathscr{D}}IC_{X}\simeq IC_{X}
for
X
pure dimensional.

2.3.
Strongly perverse sheaves. dual t-structure and rectified homological depth

In this section we only consider (
{\mathscr{S}}-
)constructible sheaves so that biduality is available, with a
dual perverse t-structure

{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R):=\mathcal{D}\left({{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R):=\mathcal{D}\left({{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)\right)

on
D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X;R)
.
Here
\mathcal{D}
denotes the Verdier duality functor on
X
. But only for
R
a
Dedekind domain
one can give a more explicit description of this

dual perverse t-structure
.
If
R
s a field, the Universal Coefficient Theorem yields that
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
if and only if
\mathcal{D}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
. More generally, one has the following result
(see also
[
6
, Section 3.3]
):

Proposition 2.49
.

Assume that the ring
R
is a Dedekind domain (e.g., a field or a PID). If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
is constructible with respect to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
, then
\mathcal{D}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
, or equivalently
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
,
if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
;

(ii)

for any stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
and any
x\in S
, the costalk cohomology
H^{\dim S}(i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is torsion-free.

Proof.

Let
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
and
x\in S
, with inclusion
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
.
Properties of the dualizing functor and the Universal Coefficient Theorem yield:

H^{j}(i_{x}^{*}\mathcal{D}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq H^{j}(\mathcal{D}i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq{\rm Hom}(H^{-j}(i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}),R)\oplus{\rm Ext}(H^{-j+1}(i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}),R).

The desired equivalence can now be checked easily.
In particular,

{{}^{p}D}^{\geq-1}(X;R)\subset{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)\subset{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R).

∎

Definition 2.50
.

Assume that
R
is a Dedekind domain.
We say that
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
is
strongly perverse
if
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
and
\mathcal{D}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
.
Equivalently,

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)\cap{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R).

If
R
is a field, the notions of perverse sheaf and strongly perverse sheaf coincide.
If
R
is a Dedekind domain, then
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is strongly perverse (with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}
) if and only if
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is perverse and property (ii) above holds (i.e., costalks of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
in the lowest possible degree are torsion-free on each stratum).

Example 2.51
.

Let
X
be a pure dimensional complex algebraic or analytic variety, and let
R
be a Dedekind domain.
Then
IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})
is strongly perverse if the generically defined local system
{\mathscr{L}}
has finitely generated and torsion-free stalks
(since then condition (ii) only needs to be checked for the top dimensional strata
S
, with
H^{\dim S}(i_{x}^{!}{\mathscr{L}}[\dim X])\simeq{\mathscr{L}}_{x}
for
x
in such a stratum
S
).

Strongly perverse sheaves are related to the notion of
rectified homological depth
, which we now define.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic or analytic variety. Following
[
113
, Definition 6.0.4]
, we make the following.

Definition 2.52
.

The
rectified homological depth

{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)
of
X
with respect to the commutative base ring
R
is
\geq d
(for some
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
) if

(17)

\mathcal{D}({R}_{X}[d])\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)\>,

or, equivalently,

(18)

R_{X}\in{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\geq d}(X;R)\>.

The
rectified homological depth

{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)
of
X\neq\emptyset
with respect to the commutative base ring
R
is the maximum of such integers
d
.

As indicated in
[
113
, p. 387]
, the above definition agrees with the notion of
rectified homological depth
introduced by Hamm and Lê
[
56
]
(following an earlier definition of Grothendieck, together with the corresponding homotopical notion) in more geometric terms. In the following, let
\dim_{-}X
be the minimum of the dimension of the irreducible components of
X
. So for
X\neq\emptyset
smooth, this is the minimum of the dimension of the connected components of
X
. Similarly,
\dim_{-}X=\dim_{-}X_{reg}
,
for
X_{reg}
the open dense regular part of
X
.

Example 2.53
.

(a)

One always has
{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)\leq\dim_{-}X
, and
{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=\dim_{-}X
if
X
is smooth and nonempty (since then

{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet}\simeq R_{X}[2\dim X]
with
\dim X
viewed as a locally constant function). Moreover,
{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=\dim X
implies that
X
is pure dimensional
(by looking at the regular part).

(b)

If
X
is a pure-dimensional local complete intersection,
then
{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=\dim X
(see Example
4.29
and, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 6.0.11]
).

In view of Example
2.19
(a) and Definition
2.50
, one has the following equivalence (see also
[
113
, (6.14)]
):

Proposition 2.54
.

Let
R
be a Dedekind domain.
For any nonempty complex algebraic or analytic variety
X
one has:

{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=\dim X

\iff

X
is pure-dimensional and
R_{X}[\dim X]
is strongly perverse.

As a consequence, Proposition
2.49
yields the following.

Corollary 2.55
.

Let
X
be a nonempty pure-dimensional complex algebraic or analytic variety with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
.

(a)

If
R
is a field, then:

{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=\dim X

\iff

R_{X}[\dim X]
is perverse.

(b)

If
R
is a Dedekind domain (e.g., a PID), then
{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=\dim X
if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

R_{X}[\dim X]
is perverse.

(ii)

for any stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
and any
x\in S
with
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
, the costalk cohomology
H^{\dim S}(i_{x}^{!}R_{X}[\dim X])
is torsion-free.

Let us finish this section with citing the following result from
[
6
, Section 3.3]
.

Proposition 2.56
.

Assume that the ring
R
is a Dedekind domain (e.g., a field or a PID). If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
is constructible with respect to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
, then
\mathcal{D}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}(X;R)
, or equivalently
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)
,
if and only if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 1}(X;R)
;

(ii)

for any stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
and any
x\in S
, the stalk cohomology
H^{-\dim S+1}(i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is a torsion module.

In particular,
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)\subset{{}^{p^{+}}D}^{\leq 0}(X;R)\subset{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 1}(X;R)
.

Assume
R
is a Dedekind domain. Let us denote by
D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)tc}(X;R)
the full triangulated subcategory of
({\mathscr{S}}
-)constructible sheaf complexes
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)c}(X;R)
, with all stalk cohomology modules
H^{k}(i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
a torsion module for all
x\in X,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. These are also stable by the usual truncation functors.
Similarly, in the context of Example
2.38
, all considered functors preserve the subcategory
D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-tc}(-;R)
of these torsion
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible sheaf complexes. For
i_{!}=i_{*}
and
j^{*}=j^{!}
this is trivial, and for
i^{!}
it follows from the corresponding property for
Rj_{*}
and the standard distinguished triangle

i_{*}i^{!}\longrightarrow id\longrightarrow Rj_{*}j^{*}\longrightarrow\>.

But the stalk cohomology modules of the
open push-forward
Rj_{*}
can be expressed in terms of the compact link
[
113
, Remark 4.4.2]
, and by the Künneth formula they vanish after tensoring with the quotient field
Q(R)
of
R
. Then the usual perverse t-structure and its truncation functors restrict to the perverse t-structure

(19)

\left({{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R),{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R)\right)\quad\text{on $D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-tc}(X;R)$,}

and the corresponding heart of
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible torsion perverse sheaves

Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R)\>.

Example 2.57
.

Let
X
be a pure dimensional complex algebraic or analytic variety with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, and let
R
be a Dedekind domain.
Then
IC_{X}({\mathscr{L}})
is a
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible torsion perverse sheaf if the local system
{\mathscr{L}}
defined on the open top dimensional strata has only finitely generated torsion stalks.

Corollary 2.58
.

Assume
R
is a Dedekind domain. Then one gets for the
shifted Verdier duality functor
({\mathscr{D}}(-))[1]
:

(a)

({\mathscr{D}}(-))[1]
maps
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R)
.

(b)

({\mathscr{D}}(-))[1]
maps
{{}^{p}D}^{\geq 0}_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R)
into
{{}^{p}D}^{\leq 0}_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R)
.

In particular, the category
Perv_{{\mathscr{S}}-t}(X;R)
of
{\mathscr{S}}
-constructible torsion perverse sheaves is stable under the
shifted Verdier duality functor
({\mathscr{D}}(-))[1]
.

Example 2.59
.

Assume
R
is a Dedekind domain. Then on a point space
X=\{pt\}
the
shifted duality functor

({\mathscr{D}}(-))[1]
is given by

R{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}(-,R)[1]\simeq{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}(-,Q(R)/R):\>D^{b}_{tc}(\{pt\};R)\to D^{b}_{tc}(\{pt\};R)\>,

preserving finitely generated torsion modules (viewed as complexes concentrated in degree zero). Here
Q(R)
is the quotient field of
R
so that the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow Q(R)\longrightarrow Q(R)/R\longrightarrow 0

gives the injective resolution
[Q(R)\longrightarrow Q(R)/R]
of
R
used for calculating

R{\mathscr{H}}om^{\bullet}(-,R)
.

In fact this example is also used on a general
X
for showing that the Verdier duality functor
{\mathscr{D}}
preserves
D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)tc}(X;R)
, together with

i_{x}^{*}{\mathscr{D}}\simeq{\mathscr{D}}i_{x}^{!}
for all
x\in X
.
