and therefore finite.

Example 3.49
.

If
f\colon M\to N
is a
submersion
, then
f
is
non-characteristic
with respect to any closed
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-conic subset
C\subset T^{*}N
,
since then
kern(^{t}f^{\prime})=f^{*}(T^{*}_{N}N)
is the zero-section of
f^{*}(T^{*}N)
.

More generally,
transversality
with respect to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{T}}
of a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subset

Y\hookrightarrow N
can be characterized as follows (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 4.3.2]
and
[
67
, Definition 4.1.5]
).

Example 3.50
.

The morphism
f\colon M\to N
is
transversal
to
{\mathscr{T}}
, i.e.,
f
is transversal to all strata
T\in{\mathscr{T}}
, if and only if
f
is
non-characteristic
with respect to
the closed
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-conic subset
T^{*}_{{\mathscr{T}}}N\subset T^{*}N
. In this case,
X:=f^{-1}(Y)
gets an induced Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}=f^{-1}{\mathscr{T}}
with strata
S
given by the connected components of the locally closed complex submanifolds
f^{-1}(T)\subset M
(for
T\in{\mathscr{T}}
).
Here, the codimension
\hbox{\rm codim}\,f^{-1}(T)=\hbox{\rm codim}\,T
is preserved, i.e.,

\dim f^{-1}(T)=\dim T+\dim M-\dim N\quad\text{for all $T\in{\mathscr{T}}$.}

Moreover,
\,{}^{t}f^{\prime}\colon f_{\pi}^{-1}(T^{*}_{T}N)\to T^{*}M
is injective with image
T^{*}_{f^{-1}(T)}M
. By Poincaré duality one gets an induced
pullback map of Lagrangian cycles

(66)

\,{}^{t}f^{\prime}_{*}f_{\pi}^{*}\colon L({\mathscr{T}},T^{*}N)\to L({\mathscr{S}},T^{*}M),

with

\,{}^{t}f^{\prime}_{*}f_{\pi}^{*}\left(\left[\overline{T^{*}_{T}N}\right]\right)=\left[\overline{T^{*}_{f^{-1}(T)}M}\right]\quad\text{ for all $T\in{\mathscr{T}}$.}

Moreover, normal slices to
f^{-1}(T)\subset M
and
T\subset N
get identified via
f
, so that the corresponding Euler characteristics of
normal Morse data do not change
under pullback of constructible functions given by
f^{*}\alpha:=\alpha\circ f
for
\alpha\in CF_{{\mathscr{T}}}(Y)
.
In particular, the characteristic cycle map
CC
commutes with this pullback only up to a sign:

(-1)^{\dim M-\dim N}\cdot CC(f^{*}\alpha)=\,^{t}f^{\prime}_{*}f_{\pi}^{*}CC(\alpha)\quad\text{for all $\alpha\in CF_{{\mathscr{T}}}(Y)$.}

For example, if
Y
(and hence also
X=f^{-1}(Y)
) is pure-dimensional, then one gets for such a transversal map

f^{*}(Eu_{Y})=Eu_{X}\>.

More generally one has the following result (see, e.g.,
[
118
, Theorem 3.1]
).

Theorem 3.51
.

Let
f\colon M\to N
be a morphism of complex algebraic (or analytic) manifolds of dimension
m=\dim M,n=\dim N
,
with
Y\subset N
a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subvariety and
X:=f^{-1}(Y)\subset M
.
Assume that
f
is
non-characteristic with respect to the support

C:=supp(CC(\alpha))\subset T^{*}N|_{Y}
of the characteristic cycle
CC(\alpha)
of a constructible
function
\alpha\in CF(Y)
. Then
C^{\prime}:=\,^{t}f^{\prime}\left(f_{\pi}^{-1}(C)\right)
is pure
m
-dimensional,
with

(67)

\,{}^{t}f^{\prime}_{*}f_{\pi}^{*}(CC(\alpha))=(-1)^{m-n}\cdot CC(f^{*}(\alpha))\>.

In particular, the left hand side is a Lagrangian cycle in
T^{*}M|_{X}
.

The following Example
3.52
has nice applications in
geometric representation theory
for the
Weyl group

{\mathbb{W}}

of a connected semisimple complex Lie group
G
(see, e.g.,
[
44
]
).
Here,
M_{1}=M_{2}=G/B
is the
Flag manifold
of
G
(with
B\subset G
a Borel subgroup), and the Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
G/B\times G/B

is given by the finitely many
G
-orbits
S_{w}
of the diagonal
G
-action (indexed by
w\in{\mathbb{W}}
). Then any
\alpha\in CF_{{\mathscr{S}}}(G/B\times G/B)
satisfies the following assumption for

C:=\bigcup_{w\in{\mathbb{W}}}T^{*}_{S_{w}}(G/B\times G/B)\subset T^{*}(G/B\times G/B)

the corresponding
Steinberg variety
.

Example 3.52
.

Let
M_{i}
be three complex algebraic (or analytic) complex manifolds of dimension
m_{i}=\dim M_{i}
(
i=1,2,3
).
Assume
\alpha\in CF(M_{1}\times M_{2})
satisfies
one of the following two equivalent conditions for a closed
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-conic subset
C\subset T^{*}(M_{1}\times M_{2})

with
supp(CC(\alpha))\subset C
:

(1)

The projection
T^{*}(M_{1}\times M_{2})=T^{*}M_{1}\times T^{*}M_{2}\to T^{*}M_{1}\times M_{2}
restricted to
C
is proper
and therefore finite,

(2)

C\cap M_{1}\times T^{*}M_{2}
is contained in the zero section of
T^{*}(M_{1}\times M_{2})
.

Then the embedding
d\colon M_{1}\times M_{2}\times M_{3}\to(M_{1}\times M_{2})\times(M_{2}\times M_{3})
induced by the diagonal embedding

M_{2}\to M_{2}\times M_{2}
is

non-characteristic
with respect to
supp(CC(\alpha\boxtimes\beta))
for
any

\beta\in CF(M_{2}\times M_{3})
,
so that

\,{}^{t}d^{\prime}_{*}d_{\pi}^{*}(CC(\alpha\boxtimes\beta))=(-1)^{m_{2}}\cdot CC(d^{*}(\alpha\boxtimes\beta))\>.

Another application of Theorem
3.51
is the following
intersection formula
(see, e.g.,
[
118
, Corollary 3.1]
).

Corollary 3.53
.

Let
M
a complex algebraic (or analytic) manifold of dimension
m=\dim M
, with
\alpha,\beta\in CF(M)
given constructible functions.
Assume that the diagonal embedding
d\colon M\to M\times M
is non-characteristic with respect
to
supp(CC(\alpha\boxtimes\beta))
, with
supp(\alpha\cdot\beta)
compact.

Then also
supp(CC(\alpha)\cap CC(\beta))\subset T^{*}M
is compact, with

(68)

\int_{M}\alpha\cdot\beta\>d\chi=(-1)^{m}\cdot deg(CC(\alpha)\cap CC(\beta))\>.

Example 3.54
.

The assumption
d\colon M\to M\times M
is
non-characteristic
with respect
to
supp(CC(\alpha\boxtimes\beta))
for
\alpha,\beta\in CF(M)
holds in the following cases:

(1)

\alpha
, resp.,
\beta
is constructible with respect to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, resp.,
{\mathscr{T}}
of
M
, with
{\mathscr{S}}
and
{\mathscr{T}}
intersecting transversally (i.e., all strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
and
T\in{\mathscr{T}}
intersect transversally in
M
),
so that
d\colon M\to M\times M
is transversal to the product stratification
{\mathscr{S}}\times{\mathscr{T}}
of
M\times M
.

(2)

\alpha
and
\beta
are
splayed

in the sense of
[
113
, Definition 2.1]
, i.e., for any
p\in M
there are locally analytic isomorphisms
M=V_{1}\times V_{2}

of analytic manifolds so that
\alpha=\pi_{1}^{*}(\alpha^{\prime})
and
\beta=\pi_{2}^{*}(\beta^{\prime})
for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in CF(V_{1})
and

\beta^{\prime}\in CF(V_{2})
, with
\pi_{i}\colon V_{1}\times V_{2}\to V_{i}
the projection (
i=1,2
). For
\alpha=1_{X}
and
\beta=1_{Y}
, with
X,Y\subset M
closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subvarieties, this just means that
X
and
Y
are
splayed
in the sense of Aluffi-Faber
[
1
,
2
]
.

We next explain the relation between
specialization
of Lagrangian cycles and
nearby cycles
for constructible functions
(see, e.g.,
[
14
,
43
,
107
]
).

Let
f\colon N\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a submersion of complex algebraic (or analytic) manifolds,
with
k\colon M:=\{f=0\}\hookrightarrow N
the inclusion of a smooth hypersurface with open complement
U=\{f\neq 0\}
. Consider the exact sequence of vector bundles on
N

0\to\langle df\rangle=kern(p)\to T^{*}N\to T_{f}^{*}\to 0\>,

with the projection
p\colon T^{*}N\to T^{*}_{f}
dual to the inclusion
T_{f}\to TN
of the subvector bundle of tangents to the fibers of
f
.
Let
X\hookrightarrow N
be a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subvariety endowed with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
so that

X\cap\{f=0\}=:X_{0}
is a union of strata, with
{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
, resp.,
{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}
the induced Whitney stratification of
X_{0}
, resp.,
X\cap U
.
After shrinking to an open neighborhood, we can and will assume by the
curve selection lemma
that
f|_{S}
is a submersion for all strata

S\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}
. Finally, we assume that
{\mathscr{S}}
satisfies the following
a_{f}
-condition of Thom
:

(69)

\begin{cases}&\text{If $x_{n}\in S^{\prime}$, for $S^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}$, is a sequence converging to $x\in S\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}$,}\\
&\text{such that $kern(df_{x_{i}}|_{T_{x_{i}}S^{\prime}})=T_{x_{i}}(S^{\prime}\cap\{f=f(x_{i})\})$}\\
&\text{converges to some limiting plane $\tau$, then $T_{x}S\subset\tau$.}\end{cases}

By a classical theorem of Hironaka
[
57
, Corollary 1 to Theorem 2, p.248]
(and see also
[
76
, Corollary 1.3.5.1]
),
one can always refine a given stratification so that it satisfies the

a_{f}
-condition. In fact, by a more recent result
[
14
, Theorem 4.2.1]
, this
a_{f}
-condition is true for any Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
as above
(with
X_{0}
a union of strata).

Then
T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}N\cap kern(p)
is contained in the zero-section
T_{N}^{*}N|_{U}
of
T^{*}N|_{U}
,
since
f\colon S^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a submersion for
S^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}
.
Therefore
p^{\prime}=p\colon T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}N\to T^{*}_{f}|_{S}
is a proper injection with image

p^{\prime}(T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}N)=:T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}f
,
the
relative conormal space
of
f|_{S^{\prime}}
.
Here we consider the commutative diagram

(70)

\begin{CD}T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}N@>{}>{}>T^{*}N|_{U}@>{j}>{}>T^{*}N\\
@V{p^{\prime}}V{}V@V{p^{\prime}}V{}V@V{}V{p}V\\
T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}f@>{}>{}>T_{f}^{*}|_{U}@>{j^{\prime}}>{}>T^{*}_{f}@<{i}<{}<T^{*}M=T^{*}_{f}|_{M}\>.\end{CD}

Note that by the
a_{f}
-condition the closure
\overline{T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}f}\subset T^{*}_{f}
for a stratum
S^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}
is contained in the closed subset

T^{*}_{{\mathscr{S}}}f:=\bigcup_{S\in{\mathscr{S}}}T^{*}_{S}f\>\hookrightarrow T^{*}_{f}\>,

with
T^{*}_{S}f:=T^{*}_{S}M
for
S\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
and
p^{\prime}\colon T^{*}_{{\mathscr{S}}}N|_{U}\to T^{*}_{f}|_{U}
proper.
Then the specialization of Lagrangian cycles

sp\colon L({\mathscr{S}},T^{*}N)\to L({\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}},T^{*}M)

is defined as the composition of the following homomorphisms
(with
n=\dim N=\dim M+1
):

(71)

\begin{CD}H^{BM}_{2n}(T^{*}_{{\mathscr{S}}}N,{\mathbb{Z}})@>{j^{*}}>{}>H^{BM}_{2n}(T^{*}_{{\mathscr{S}}}N|_{U},{\mathbb{Z}})@>{p^{\prime}_{*}}>{}>H^{BM}_{2n}(T^{*}_{{\mathscr{S}}}f|_{U},{\mathbb{Z}})\\
@V{sp}V{}V@A{j^{\prime*}}A{\wr}A\\
L({\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}},T^{*}M)=H^{BM}_{2n-2}(T^{*}_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}}M,{\mathbb{Z}})@<{i^{*}}<{}<H^{BM}_{2n}(T^{*}_{{\mathscr{S}}}f,{\mathbb{Z}})\>,\end{CD}

with the Gysin map
i^{*}
again defined by Poincaré duality as the intersection with
[T^{*}M]
.
So
sp([T^{*}_{S}N])=0
for
S\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
and

sp([\overline{T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}N}])=\left[\overline{T^{*}_{S^{\prime}}f}\right]\cap[T^{*}M]\quad\text{for $S^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{S}}|_{U}$.}

The
nearby cycles
of constructible functions used in the next Theorem are induced from Deligne’s nearby cycle functor
\psi_{f}

for constructible sheaf complexes as discussed later on in Section
4
, fitting into a commutative diagram

(72)

\begin{CD}K_{0}(D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}(X;R))@>{\psi_{f}}>{}>K_{0}(D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}-c}(X_{0};R))\\
@V{\chi_{stalk}}V{}V@V{}V{\chi_{stalk}}V\\
CF_{{\mathscr{S}}}(X)@ >\psi_{f}>>CF_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}}(X_{0})\>.\end{CD}

With these notations, we can now show the following result (see, e.g.,
[
107
, Theorem 4.3]
and compare with
[
14
,
43
]
),
which is also used in
[
118
]
in the proof of Theorem
3.51
above.

Theorem 3.55
.

Let
\alpha\in CF_{{\mathscr{S}}}(X)
be given. Then

(73)

sp(CC(\alpha))=CC(-\psi_{f}(\alpha))\in L({\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}},T^{*}M)\>,

with
\psi_{f}(\alpha)(x):=\int_{M_{f|_{X},x}}\alpha d\chi
, for
M_{f|_{X},x}
a local Milnor fiber of
f|_{X}
at
x\in X_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
\beta\in CF_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}}(X_{0})
be defined by
CC(\beta)=sp(CC(\alpha))\in L({\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}},T^{*}M)
.
Then we need to show that
\beta=-\psi_{f}(\alpha)
. For this, we calculate
\beta(x)

for
x\in X_{0}
as in Corollary
3.43
via

\beta(x):=\sharp_{dr^{\prime}_{x}}(\>sp(CC(\alpha))\cap[dr^{\prime}(\{r^{\prime}<\epsilon\})]\>)\>,

with
r^{\prime}:=r|_{M}
, for
r\colon(N,x)\simeq({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)\to[0,\infty[\;
given by
r(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\cdot\bar{z}_{i}
the distance function to
0
(in local coordinates of
N
), and

0<\epsilon
small enough.
But this intersection number is locally constant in the family
T^{*}_{f}|_{\{f=w\}}
for
|w|\ll 1
small compared to
\epsilon
,
since
\{r=\epsilon\}
is transversal to all
S\cap\{f=w\}
, for
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
and
|w|\ll 1
small, by the
a_{f}
-condition as well as the Whitney condition
for
{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
.
So instead of specializing at
w=0
, we can do this at a small stratified regular value
w\neq 0
and use Example
3.50
:

\beta(x)=-\>\sharp(\>CC(\alpha|_{\{f=w\}})\cap[dr^{\prime\prime}(\{r^{\prime\prime}<\epsilon\})]\>)\>,

with
r^{\prime\prime}:=r|_{\{f=w\}}
. Note that the sign comes from
\dim N-\dim\{f=w\}=1
, i.e., we intersect transversally with a submanifold of codimension
1
.
And the last intersection number is by Equation (
50
) given by

\>\sharp(\>CC(\alpha|_{\{f=w\}})\cap[dr^{\prime\prime}(\{r^{\prime\prime}<\epsilon\})]\>)=\>\int_{M_{f|_{X},x}}\,\alpha\>d\chi=\psi_{f}(\alpha)(x)\>,

with
M_{f|_{X},x}:=X\cap\{f=w\}\cap\{r\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\}
, for
0<|w|\ll\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
, a local Milnor fiber of
f|_{X}
at
x\in X_{0}
.
∎

Note that the
a_{f}
-condition is also needed to have such a
local Milnor fibration
with
Milnor fiber
M_{f,x}
for a general holomorphic function germ

f\colon(X,x)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
on a singular complex analytic variety
X
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 1.3.3]
and
[
72
]
).
The definition of
nearby cycles
of constructible functions in terms of (weighted) Euler characteristics of these
local Milnor fibers
goes back to Verdier

[
127
]
.

As an example, let us explain how Theorem
3.55
implies Theorem
3.51
for the inclusion
k\colon M=\{f=0\}\hookrightarrow N
of a global smooth hypersurface (i.e., of codimension one), with
f\colon N\to{\mathbb{C}}
a submersion and the notations as before. Consider the following cartesian diagram:

(74)

\begin{CD}T^{*}N|_{M}@>{k_{\pi}}>{}>T^{*}N@<{j}<{}<T^{*}N|_{U}\\
@V{\,{}^{t}k^{\prime}}V{}V@V{p}V{}V@V{}V{p^{\prime}}V\\
T^{*}M@>{i}>{}>T_{f}^{*}@<{j^{\prime}}<{}<T^{*}_{f}|_{U}\>.\end{CD}

Assume that
k\colon M\hookrightarrow N
is
non-characteristic
with respect to
supp(CC(\alpha))\subset T^{*}N
for a given
\alpha\in CF(N)
, so that

\,{}^{t}k^{\prime}\colon k_{\pi}^{-1}(supp(CC(\alpha)))\to T^{*}M
is proper. After shrinking
N
we can then assume that also

p\colon supp(CC(\alpha))\to T_{f}^{*}
is proper.
Then one gets by base change

j^{\prime*}p_{*}CC(\alpha)=p^{\prime}_{*}j^{*}CC(\alpha)\>,

and

\,{}^{t}k^{\prime}_{*}k_{\pi}^{*}CC(\alpha)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i^{*}p_{*}CC(\alpha)=sp(CC(\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle CC(-\psi_{f}(\alpha))=-CC(k^{*}\alpha)\>.

Here, the fact that
\psi_{f}(\alpha)=k^{*}\alpha
follows from (
79
) below, since we have

0=\psi_{f}(\alpha)(x)-k^{*}\alpha(x)=\psi_{f}(\alpha)(x)-\alpha(x)
for all
x\in M
by the
non-characteristic assumption, which gives
df_{x}\not\in supp(CC(\alpha))
for all
x\in M
.

We finish this section with citing from
[
114
, Corollary 0.3]
(and compare with
[
107
, Theorem 4.5]
)
a nice intersection formula related to the vanishing cycle functor
\varphi_{f}
introduced in Section
4
.
For a description of the
characteristic cycle of vanishing cycles
we refer to
[
86
, Theorem 2.10]
.

Theorem 3.56

(Global intersection formula for vanishing cycles)
.

Let
M
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) manifold and

f\colon M\to{\mathbb{C}}
an algebraic (or holomorphic) function, with
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(M;R)
, resp.,
\alpha\in CF(M)
be given.
Suppose that the intersection
of
df(M)
and the support
of the characteristic cycle of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
, resp.,
\alpha
is
contained in a
compact complex algebraic (or analytic) subset
I\subset T^{*}M
, with
K:=\pi(I)\subset\{f=0\}

for
\pi\colon T^{*}M\to M
the projection.
Then one has

(75)

\chi\bigl(R\Gamma(K,\varphi_{f}[-1]\;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\bigr)=\sharp\bigl(\;[CC({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})]\cap[df(M)]\;\bigr)\>,

resp.,

(76)

-\int_{K}\varphi_{f}(\alpha)\>d\chi=-\chi(K,\varphi_{f}(\alpha))=\sharp\bigl(\;[CC(\alpha)]\cap[df(M)]\;\bigr)\>.

Example 3.57
.

Let
f\colon M\to\{0\}
be the constant zero function so that
df(M)=T^{*}_{M}M
is the zero section of
T^{*}M
,
with
-\varphi_{f}(\alpha)=\alpha
for all
\alpha\in CF(M)
. Assume
M=:K
is compact.
Then we recover the
global index formula
(
52
):

\int_{X}\,\alpha\>d\chi=\sharp(\>CC(\alpha)\cap[T^{*}_{M}M]\>)\>.

In the special case
I=\{\omega\}
given by a point

\omega\in T^{*}M
, we
get back by Theorem
3.56

a formula conjectured by Deligne (with
x:=\pi(\omega)
and
K:=\{x\}
):

(77)

\chi\bigl((\phi_{f}[-1]\;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})_{x}\bigr)=\sharp_{df_{x}}\bigl(\;[CC({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}]\cap[df(M)]\;\bigr)

and

(78)

-\varphi_{f}(\alpha)(x)=\alpha(x)-\int_{M_{f;x}}\,\alpha\,d\chi=\sharp_{df_{x}}\bigl(\;[CC(\alpha)]\cap[df(M)]\;\bigr)\>,

with
M_{f,x}
the

local Milnor fiber
of
f
in
x
. In particular, for
df_{x}\not\in supp(CC(\alpha))
, we get

(79)

-\varphi_{f}(\alpha)(x)=\alpha(x)-\psi_{f}(\alpha)(x)=\alpha(x)-\int_{M_{f;x}}\,\alpha\,d\chi=0\>.

Example 3.58
.

Let
f\colon(M,x)=({\mathbb{C}}^{n+1},0)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
be a holomorphic function germ with an

isolated singularity
in
0\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}
.
Then the local Milnor fiber
M_{f,0}
has the homotopy type of a wedge of a finite number of
n
-spheres (for
n\geq 1
). The
Milnor number

\mu(f)
is the number of these
n
-spheres, so that

\chi(M_{f,x},{\mathbb{Q}})=1+(-1)^{n}\cdot\mu(f)\>.

For
n=0
, the local Milnor fiber
M_{f,0}
consists of
1+\mu(f)
points.
If we apply the above formula to the perverse sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}={\mathbb{Q}}_{M}[n+1]
, resp.,
\alpha=(-1)^{n+1}\cdot 1_{M}
,
with
CC({\mathbb{Q}}_{M}[n+1])=CC((-1)^{n+1}\cdot 1_{M})=T^{*}_{M}M
the zero section of
T^{*}M
, we recover the formula

\mu(f)=\sharp_{df_{x}}\bigl(\;[T^{*}_{M}M]\cap[df(M)]\;\bigr)

for the Milnor number, i.e.,

\mu(f)=\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\frac{\left({\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}}\right)_{0}}{\left(\nabla f\right)_{0}}\>

for the Milnor number in terms of the
Jacobian ideal
\left(\nabla f\right)_{0}\subset\left({\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n+1}}\right)_{0}
.

The
local intersection formula for the vanishing cycle functor
(
75
) is due
to Dubson
[
37
, Theorem 1]
, Ginsburg
[
43
, Proposition 7.7.1]
,
Lê
[
75
, Theorem 4.1.2]
and Sabbah
[
107
, Theorem 4.5]
.
For a discussion of the history of this
local intersection formula for vanishing cycles

we recommend the paper
[
75
]
.

But most of these
references are in the language of
holonomic D-modules
or
perverse sheaves
. So the assumption on the
intersection for a constructible complex of sheaves
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

corresponds to an assumption on the
micro-support

\mu supp({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
.
To be able to state the result also for constructible functions, it is important to work with the weaker assumption about the
support of
the characteristic cycle

supp(CC({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}))\subset\mu supp({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
.

Let us finally point out that MacPherson’s theory
[
83
]
of

Chern classes
c_{*}
of singular varieties and constructible functions
in the embedded context can easily be recovered and improved form the
functorial theory of characteristic cycles
as presented above, e.g., like the following results about
c_{*}
:

(1)

Functoriality of
c_{*}
for proper morphism via Proposition
3.46
.

(2)

Multiplicativity of
c_{*}
with respect to external products via Example
3.45
.

(3)

Specialization of
c_{*}
in one parameter families via Theorem
3.55
.

(4)

A Verdier-Riemann-Roch Theorem for
c_{*}
with respect to non-characteristic pullbacks (e.g., submersions or transversal pullbacks)
via Theorem
3.51
.

(5)

An intersection formula for
c_{*}
via Theorem
3.51
in the context of Example
68
.

In fact, the most recent approach of
[
3
]
deduces
c_{*}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in CF(X)
from
CC(\alpha)
, viewed as a
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
-invariant
cycle, via intersection with the zero-section
T^{*}_{M}M
in
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant Borel-Moore homology.
For further reading also on relations between
MacPherson Chern classes

c_{*}
and
characteristic cycles
we refer to, e.g.,

[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
19
,
43
,
44
,
107
,
115
,
118
,
117
,
127
]
.

3.5.
Vanishing results

Let us now explain other applications of the
stratified Morse theory for constructible sheaves
and of Theorem
3.19
,
to
vanishing and weak Lefschetz theorems
in the complex algebraic and analytic context.
In the following we are back to the general context that the base ring
R
is commutative and noetherian, of finite global dimension, and we work with (weakly) constructible complexes of sheaves of
R
-modules.

First we consider the complex algebraic context with an
affine
complex algebraic variety
X\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
and
the
strongly plurisubharmonic and semi-algebraic
distance function

r\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\,;\>r(z):=\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\>z_{i}\bar{z}_{i}\>.

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-wc}(X;R)
is
{\mathscr{S}}
-weakly constructible with respect to a
complex algebraic
Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
,
then the proper semi-algebraic distance function
r
has only
finitely
many stratified critical values so that one gets
as in the proof of Theorem
3.38
and Example
3.41
:

R\Gamma(X\cap\{r\leq b\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\quad\text{for $b>0$ large enough,}

and

R\Gamma_{c}(X\cap\{r<b\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma_{c}(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\quad\text{for $b>0$ large enough.}

Then Theorem
3.19
implies for
f:=r
(with
q=0
, since
r
is strongly plurisubharmonic)
directly the following important
Artin-Grothendieck type result
(see also
[
113
, Corollary 6.0.4]
for a more general version):

Theorem 3.59

(Artin vanishing Theorem)
.

Let
X
be an affine complex algebraic variety,
with
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
a complex algebraically (weakly) constructible sheaf complex
(respectively, complex algebraically constructible in case of a Dedekind domain
R
, if we want to use the dual perverse t-structure).
Then:

(1)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X;R)\Rightarrow R\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(\{pt\};R)\subset D^{b}_{(w)c}(\{pt\};R)
,

(2)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X;R)\Rightarrow R\Gamma_{c}(X,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(\{pt\};R)\subset D^{b}_{(w)c}(\{pt\};R)
.

Remark 3.60
.

The same proof as indicated above gives the
Artin vanishing Theorem
more generally for an
open semi-algebraic
subset
j\colon X\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}

of a complex algebraic variety
X^{\prime}
, if it has a proper
strongly plurisubharmonic and semi-algebraic
function

r\colon X\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
, and we consider only (weakly) constructible sheaves of the form
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}=j^{*}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}
for some algebraically (weakly) constructible

{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X^{\prime},R)
.

Compare with
[
6
, Section 4]
, especially

[
6
, Theorem 4.1.1]
),
for the corresponding relative counterpart for an affine morphism
in the context
of the
perverse t-structure
in
l
-adic
cohomology. The relative version of Theorem
3.59

for an
affine complex algebraic morphism
is also true, as we will see later on.
For now, let us only illustrate this Theorem
3.59
by the following example
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 6.0.4]
).

Example 3.61

(Weak Lefschetz theorem for singular spaces)
.

Let
X
be a closed algebraic subvariety of the complex projective space, with
H
a hyperplane.
Consider the open inclusion
j\colon U:=X\backslash H\hookrightarrow X
, with
i\colon X\cap H\hookrightarrow X
the corresponding closed inclusion.
Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
is
complex algebraically
(weakly) constructible
(respectively, complex algebraically constructible in case of a Dedekind domain
R
, if we want to use the dual perverse t-structure).
Then

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(U;R)\Rightarrow

R\Gamma(X,Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(U,j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(\{pt\};R)\>,

and

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(U;R)\Rightarrow

R\Gamma(X,X\cap H,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}):=R\Gamma(X,Rj_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma_{c}(U,j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(\{pt\};R)\>.

In particular:

(1)

The
relative homology

H_{k}(X,X\cap H;R)=H^{-k}(U;j^{*}{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet})=0\quad\text{for $k<{\mathrm{rHd}}(U,R)$.}

(2)

If
R
is a Dedekind domain, then the
relative cohomology

H^{k}(X,X\cap H;R)\simeq H^{k}_{c}(U;R_{U})=\begin{cases}0&\text{for $k<{\mathrm{rHd}}(U,R)$,}\\
\text{torsion-free}&\text{for $k={\mathrm{rHd}}(U,R)$.}\end{cases}

(3)

Suppose
X
is purely
n
-dimensional, and
H
is a
generic
hyperplane,
i.e.,
H
is transversal to a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
, so that
i^{*}IC_{X}[-1]\simeq IC_{X\cap H}
by Example
2.28
.
Then the
relative intersection cohomology

IH^{k}(X,X\cap H;R):=H^{k-n}_{c}(U;j^{*}IC_{X})=0\quad\text{ for $k<n$.}

Moreover,
IH^{n}(X,X\cap H;R)
is torsion-free in case
R
is a Dedekind domain.

Here, statements 1. and 2. show the role of the
rectified homological depth
for
the “weak Lefschetz theorem for singular spaces” as conjectured by Grothendieck.
Compare also with
[
56
, Theorem 3.4.1]
for the
corresponding homotopy result.

The “weak Lefschetz theorem for intersection homology”
3. is due to
[
46
, Theorem 7.1]
, and compare also with
[
47
, Theorem 6.10]
.
Here we only cite the following remark of Goresky-MacPherson from the beginning of

[
47
, Section 6.10]
: “…The following Lefschetz hyperplane theorem was
our original motivation for developing Morse theory on singular spaces …”.

The
weak Lefschetz theorem
of Example
3.61
can be generalized
in many different directions. Especially, it is enough to assume that
X
is
a
quasi-projective

algebraic subvariety
X=X^{\prime}\backslash A
, with
A\subset X^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{C}}P^{N}

closed subvarieties, if
H
is a
generic
hyperplane.
Just take a Whitney stratification of
X^{\prime}
such that
A
is a union of strata, and

H
is
transversal
to all strata. Then one can apply the following
base change isomorphisms
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Lemma 6.0.5]
).

Lemma 3.62
.

Let
A\subset X^{\prime}
be closed analytic subvarieties of the complex manifold
M
.
Fix a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X^{\prime}
, with
A
a union of strata.
Suppose
H
is closed complex submanifold of
M
, which is transversal to all strata,
and consider the following cartesian diagram of inclusions:

\begin{CD}X:=X^{\prime}\backslash A@<{j}<{}<U:=X\backslash H\\
@V{}V{k^{\prime}}V@V{}V{k}V\\
X^{\prime}@<{j^{\prime}}<{}<U^{\prime}:=X^{\prime}\backslash H\>.\end{CD}

Then for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{X}-wc}(X;R)
a weakly constructible complex with respect to the induced stratification
{\mathscr{S}}|_{X}
of
X
, one has natural isomorphisms:

(80)

j^{\prime}_{!}Rk_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq Rk^{\prime}_{*}j_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\quad\text{and}\quad Rj^{\prime}_{*}k_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq k^{\prime}_{!}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\>.

If
X^{\prime}
is also compact, then one gets for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{X}-wc}(X;R)
:

R\Gamma_{c}(X,Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq R\Gamma(X^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{!}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(U^{\prime},k_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\>,

and

R\Gamma(X,X\cap H,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(X^{\prime},Rk^{\prime}_{*}j_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma_{c}(U^{\prime},Rk_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\>.

Moreover,

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(U;R)\>\Rightarrow\>k_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(U^{\prime};R)\>,

and

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(U;R)\>\Rightarrow\>Rk_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(U^{\prime};R)\>.

Applying the Artin vanishing Theorem
3.59
to these complexes on the right hand side, one gets that the

weak Lefschetz theorem
of Example
3.61

remains true for
X=X^{\prime}\backslash A
a quasi-projective variety, with
A\subset X^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{C}}P^{N}
closed subvarieties, if
H
is a
generic
hyperplane as discussed above (with
U^{\prime}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}P^{N}\backslash H={\mathbb{C}}^{N}
affine).

We next discuss the counterpart of the Artin vanishing Theorem
3.59
in the complex analytic context for
q-complete
varieties in the following sense.

Definition 3.63
.

A complex variety
X
is called
q-complete
(
q\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
),
if there exists a

proper q-convex C
∞
-function

f\colon X\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
(with q-convexity in the sense of Definition
3.18
).
In particular, the
0
-complete
complex varieties are just the complex analytic
Stein spaces

(see, e.g.,
[
104
]
), and a closed complex subvariety of a q-complete variety is again
q-complete.

Then we need to consider a Morse approximation of
f
(as in
[
8
]
) with possibly infinitely many critical points,
and get by Theorem
3.19
the following result (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Corollary 6.1.2]
and compare also with
[
67
, Theorem 10.3.8]
).

Theorem 3.64

(Vanishing theorem for q-complete varieties)
.

Let
X
be a complex analytic variety with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
,

f\colon X\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
a proper q-convex function, and

K_{n}:=\{f\leq r_{n}\},\>U_{n}:=\{f<r_{n}\},\quad\text{ for \ $r_{n}\nearrow\infty$}

a sequence of regular values of
f
with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}
(
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
).
Then one gets for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-wc}(X;R)
a
{\mathscr{S}}
-weakly constructible complex and

q^{\prime}:=\min\,\{q,\dim X\}
:

(1)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\leq m}_{{\mathscr{S}}}(X;R)
, then

H^{k}(K_{n+1},K_{n};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq H^{k}(\{r_{n}\leq f\leq r_{n+1}\},\{f=r_{n}\};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0\quad\text{for $k>m+q^{\prime}$},

so that
H^{k}(K_{n+1};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\to H^{k}(K_{n};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is surjective
for
k\geq m+q^{\prime}
. In particular, the projective system

H^{k}(K_{n};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition for
k\geq m+q^{\prime}

so that

(81)

H^{k}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\lim_{\leftarrow}\>H^{k}(K_{n};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0\quad\text{for $k>m+q^{\prime}$.}

(2)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\geq m}_{{\mathscr{S}}}(X;R)
, then

(82)

H^{k}_{c}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\lim_{\rightarrow}\>H^{k}_{c}(U_{n};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0\quad\text{for $k<m-q^{\prime}$.}

(3)

If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{+}}D^{\geq m}_{{\mathscr{S}}}(X;R)
is constructible and
R
is a Dedekind domain, then

(83)

H^{m-q^{\prime}}_{c}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\lim_{\rightarrow}\>H^{m-q^{\prime}}_{c}(U_{n};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\quad\text{is torsion-free.}

Note that in the general complex analytic context one cannot expect any
finiteness or torsion-properties
for the corresponding cohomology groups.
If
X
is the Stein space given by an infinite discrete set
X={\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathscr{F}}
is a sheaf on
X
, then

H^{0}(X;{\mathscr{F}})=\prod_{x\in X}{\mathscr{F}}_{x}\quad\text{and}\quad H^{0}_{c}(X;{\mathscr{F}})=\bigoplus_{x\in X}{\mathscr{F}}_{x}\>.

So even if all stalks
{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
(
x\in X
) are finitely generated (torsion)
R
-modules, this does not need to be the case for

H^{k}(X;{\mathscr{F}})
or
H^{k}_{c}(X;{\mathscr{F}})
. See
[
112
]
for similar finiteness results on
q-convex and q-concave complex varieties

(using the same method of proof as in Theorem
3.64
).

Example 3.65
.

Let
X
be a q-complete variety of dimension
n=\dim X
(e.g., a Stein space for
q=0
), with
q^{\prime}:=\min\,\{q,n\}
. Then

(1)

The
homology

H_{k}(X;R)=H^{-k}_{c}(X;{\mathbb{D}}_{X}^{\bullet})=0
for
k>n+q^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
H_{n+q^{\prime}}(X;R)
is torsion-free in case
R
is a Dedekind domain.

(2)

The
cohomology

H^{k}(X;R)=0
for
k>n+q^{\prime}
.

(3)

Suppose
X
is purely n-dimensional.
Then the
intersection cohomology

IH^{k}(X;R):=H^{k-n}(X;IC_{X})=0\quad\text{ for $k>n+q^{\prime}$, }

and the
intersection homology

IH_{k}(X;R):=H^{n-k}_{c}(X;IC_{X})=0
for
k>n+q^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
IH_{n+q^{\prime}}(X;R)
is torsion-free in case
R
is a Dedekind domain.

The results in 1. and 2. are due to Hamm
[
54
,
55
]
, whereas 3. is due to Goresky-MacPherson
[
47
, Section 6.9]

(at least for Stein spaces). See
[
113
, Chapter 6]
for more general results and further references.

Before stating the relative versions of these vanishing results, let us recall the following.

Definition 3.66
.

A morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of complex algebraic varieties is called
affine
, if any point
y\in Y
has an open affine neighborhood
U\subset Y
such that

f^{-1}(U)
is affine. Similarly, a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of complex analytic varieties is called
q-complete
, resp.,
Stein
, if any point
y\in Y
has an open neighborhood
U\subset Y
such that

f^{-1}(U)
is q-complete, resp., Stein (
q\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
). So, by definition, a
Stein morphism
corresponds to a
0-complete morphism
.

Example 3.67
.

A finite morphism (e.g., a closed embedding) is an affine, resp., Stein morphism.
Similarly, if the closed embedding
i\colon X\hookrightarrow Y
is locally given by one algebraic, resp., analytic equation
X=\{f=0\}
,
then the inclusion of the open complement
j\colon U:=Y\backslash X\hookrightarrow Y
is an affine, resp., Stein morphism.
Also, an
affine algebraic
morphism is
Stein
when viewed as a morphism of the underlying analytic varieties.

Remark 3.68
.

Let us explain the crucial property of a q-complete (resp., affine) morphism
f\colon X\to Y
used for (co)stalk calculations in the following Theorems.
In the analytic context consider a local embedding
(Y,y)\hookrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)
and an open neighborhood
U\subset Y
of
y
such that

f^{-1}(U)
is q-complete, with
g\colon f^{-1}(U)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
a proper q-convex function. Consider the strongly plurisubharmonic distance function

r\colon{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
with
r(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\cdot\bar{z}_{i}
. Then for any small
r_{0}>0
the function

(84)

g^{\prime}:=g+\frac{1}{r-r_{0}}\circ f:\>f^{-1}(U\cap\{r<r_{0}\})\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}\quad\text{is also proper and q-convex}

(see, e.g.,
[
113
, p.429]
and
[
123
, Proposition 2.2, Proposition 2.4]
).
Therefore one can apply the
vanishing Theorem
3.64
to
f^{-1}(U\cap\{r<r_{0}\})
.

For an affine morphism
f\colon X\to Y
, one takes a global affine embedding
(U,y)\hookrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)
of an open affine neighborhood
U\subset Y
of
y
such that

f^{-1}(U)
is also affine with
g\colon f^{-1}(U)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\geq 0}
a proper strongly plurisubharmonic and semialgebraic function.
Then
g^{\prime}
as before is a
proper strongly plurisubharmonic and semialgebraic function
on the
open semi-algebraic
subset

f^{-1}(U\cap\{r<r_{0}\})\subset X
, so that one can use the Artin vanishing Theorem in the version of Remark
3.60
.

Let us now state the following
Artin-Gothendieck type
result in the complex algebraic context (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Theorem 6.0.4]
).

Theorem 3.69

(Algebraic Artin-Grothendieck type Theorem)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be an affine morphism of complex algebraic varieties,
with
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
a complex algebraically (weakly) constructible sheaf complex
(respectively, complex algebraically constructible sheaf complex in case of a Dedekind domain
R
, if we want to use the dual perverse t-structure).
Then:

(1)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X;R)\Rightarrow Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(Y;R)
,

(2)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X;R)\Rightarrow Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(Y;R)
.

Compare with
[
6
, Section 4]
, especially

[
6
, Theorem 4.1.1]
,
for the corresponding relative counterpart for an affine morphism
in the context
of the
perverse t-structure
in
l
-adic
cohomology. In the case
Y=\{pt\}
a point space, Theorem
3.69
just reduces to Theorem
3.59
.

In the complex analytic context one has in addition to assume that the corresponding direct image complexes are again (weakly) constructible
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Corollary 6.0.8]
and
[
67
, Proposition 10.3.17]
for the case of a Stein map).

Theorem 3.70

(Analytic Artin-Grothendieck type Theorem)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a q-complete morphism of complex analytic varieties (e.g., a Stein map for
q=0
),
with
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(X;R)
a (weakly) constructible sheaf complex.
Then:

(1)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\leq n}(X;R)
and
Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(Y;R)\Rightarrow Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\leq n+q}(Y;R)
,

(2)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\geq n}(X;R)
and
Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(Y;R)\Rightarrow Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\geq n-q}(Y;R)
.

(3)

Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
is constructible with
R
a Dedekind domain. then

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{+}}D^{\geq n}(X;R)
and
Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(Y;R)\Rightarrow Rf_{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{+}}D^{\geq n-q}(Y;R)
.

In the case
Y=\{pt\}
a point space, Theorem
3.70
corresponds to Theorem
3.64
.

Let us finish this section with the following:

Example 3.71

(Relative weak Lefschetz theorem for singular spaces)
.

Let
V\to Y
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) vector bundle, and
W\hookrightarrow V
be a subvector bundle with
rank\;V=rank\;W+1
.
Let
i\colon{\mathbb{P}}(W)\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}(V)
the closed inclusion of the associated projective bundles, with open complement

j\colon U:={\mathbb{P}}(V)\backslash{\mathbb{P}}(W)\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}(V)
. Then the projection
\pi\colon U\to Y
is an affine (resp., Stein) morphism.

Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}({\mathbb{P}}(V);R)
is (weakly) constructible
(respectively, constructible in case of a Dedekind domain
R
, if we want to use the dual perverse t-structure), so that

R\pi_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},\>R\pi_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{(w)c}(Y;R)\>.

Then

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\leq n}(U;R)\Rightarrow R\pi_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p}D^{\leq n}(Y;R)\>,

and

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(U;R)\Rightarrow R\pi_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\,^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(Y;R)\>.

4.
Nearby and vanishing cycles, applications

In this section we recall the construction of Deligne’s nearby and vanishing cycle functors (
[
52
]
), and indicate their relation with perverse sheaves. We continue to assume that the base ring
R
is commutative and noetherian, of finite global dimension, and we work with (weakly) constructible complexes of sheaves of
R
-modules in the complex algebraic (or analytic) context.

4.1.
Construction

Let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a morphism from a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety
X
to
{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
X_{0}=f^{-1}(0)
be the central fiber, with inclusion map
i\colon X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
. Let
X^{*}:=X\setminus X_{0}
and let
f^{*}\colon X^{*}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
be the induced morphism to the punctured affine line. Consider the following cartesian diagram:

(85)

\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces X^{*}}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\widetilde{X^{*}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{\pi}}
\textstyle{\{0\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\>,}
\scriptstyle{\pi}

where
\pi\colon\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is the infinite cyclic (universal) cover of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
given by
z\mapsto\exp(2\pi iz)
. Then
\widehat{\pi}\colon\widetilde{X^{*}}\to X^{*}
is an infinite cyclic cover with deck group
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
. Note that
\pi
is only a holomorphic but not an algebraic map, so that this fiber square only exists in the complex analytic category, even if we start with a morphism
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
in the complex algebraic context.

Definition 4.1
.

The
nearby cycle functor of
f
assigns to a bounded complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
the complex on
X_{0}
defined by

(86)

\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}:=i^{*}R(j\circ\widehat{\pi})_{*}(j\circ\widehat{\pi})^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq i^{*}Rj_{*}R\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X_{0};R)\>.

Remark 4.2
.

By definition,
\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
depends only on the restriction
j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
to
X^{*}
. In the complex analytic context, it would have been enough for the definition of the nearby cycles to start with a holomorphic map
f\colon X\to D\subset{\mathbb{C}}
to a small open disc
D
around zero in the complex plane, with
\pi\colon\widetilde{D^{*}}\to D^{*}
the induced infinite cyclic (universal) cover of
D^{*}
.

Note that
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
acts naturally on
R\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}
, with an induced action on the nearby cycles
\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
.
In our complex context we choose the generator of
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
fitting with the complex orientation of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and call the induced action

h=h_{f}\colon\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\to\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

the
monodromy automorphism
of the nearby cycles. The adjunction morphism

id\to R(j\circ\widehat{\pi})_{*}(j\circ\widehat{\pi})^{*}

induces the
specialization map

sp\colon i^{*}\to\psi_{f}
commuting with the monodromy automorphism
h
acting trivially on
i^{*}
(i.e., acting as the identity on
i^{*}
). Let us now explain the use of the base change induced by the universal cover
\pi\colon\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{*}

in the definitions above in a simple but important example.

Example 4.3

(Nearby cycle functor for the identity map)
.

Let
f=id
be the identity map of
{\mathbb{C}}
, with
i\colon\{0\}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
the inclusion of the point zero. Similarly, let
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow D_{r}:=\{|z|<r\}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
, for
0<|x|<r\leq\infty
, be the inclusion of a (nearby) point
x\neq 0
in a (small) open disc of radius
r
around zero.
Finally consider a complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-wc}({\mathbb{C}};R)
which is weakly constructible with respect to the Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
{\mathbb{C}}
given by the two strata
S=\{0\}
and
S^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}^{*}
.

Then the cohomology sheaves
{\mathscr{H}}^{k}(j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
are only
locally constant
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, but their pullbacks

{\mathscr{H}}^{k}(\widehat{\pi}^{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
to
{\mathbb{C}}\simeq\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
(or their restrictions to
\widetilde{D_{r}^{*}}
) are locally constant and therefore

constant
(for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
)
since
{\mathbb{C}}\simeq\widetilde{{\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
(or
\widetilde{D_{r}^{*}}
) is convex and
contractible
. Then

\begin{CD}\psi_{id}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@<{\sim}<{}<R\Gamma(D^{*}_{r};R\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(\widetilde{D_{r}^{*}};\widehat{\pi}^{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\\
@V{}V{\wr}V\\
i^{*}_{\tilde{x}}\widehat{\pi}^{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq i^{*}_{x}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\end{CD}

for
i_{\tilde{x}}\colon\{\tilde{x}\}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{D_{r}^{*}}
the inclusion of a point
\tilde{x}
with
\pi(\tilde{x})=x
. And the
monodromy

h
on

H^{k}(\psi_{id}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}))
gets identified with the
monodromy of the local system

{\mathscr{H}}^{k}(j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
acting on
i^{*}_{x}{\mathscr{H}}^{k}(j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
(for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
). Finally the
specialization
map is given by

\begin{CD}sp\colon\;i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@<{\sim}<{}<R\Gamma(D_{r};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})@>{}>{}>i^{*}_{x}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq\psi_{id}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\>.\end{CD}

Definition 4.4
.

In the context of the diagram (
85
),
the
vanishing cycles
of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
, denoted
\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
, is the bounded complex on
X_{0}
defined by taking ‘‘the’’ cone of
the
specialization map

sp\colon i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\to\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
.
In particular, one gets a unique distinguished triangle

(87)

i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{{sp}}{\longrightarrow}\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{can}{\longrightarrow}\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{[1]}{\longrightarrow}

in
D^{b}(X_{0};R)
.

Remark 4.5
.

Note that cones are not functorial, but in the above construction one can for example work with (a suitable truncation of) the canonical flabby resolution to get
\varphi_{f}
as a functor (see
[
67
, Chapter 8]
or
[
113
, pp. 25-26]
for more details). The vanishing cycle functor also comes equipped with a
monodromy automorphism, denoted also by
h
, so that
h
induces an automorphism of the triangle (
87
).

Example 4.6
.

Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is supported on
X_{0}
, i.e.,
j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq 0
. Then
\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq 0
and

(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\simeq i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
, with a trivial monodromy action
h
.

Example 4.7

(Vanishing cycle functor for the identity map)
.

Consider the context of the Example
4.3
. Then for any point
0\neq x\in D_{r}
in a small open disc
D_{r}
around zero:

(\varphi_{id}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\simeq R\Gamma(D_{r},\{x\};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\bigl(R\Gamma_{\{l\geq 0\}}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\bigr)_{0}=:LMD({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},l,0)

for any
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear map
l\colon{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with
l(x)<0
. In particular the following properties are equivalent:

(1)

sp\colon i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\to\psi_{id}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is an isomorphism,

(2)

\varphi_{id}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\simeq 0
,

(3)

all cohomology sheaves
{\mathscr{H}}^{k}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
are locally constant on
{\mathbb{C}}
or
D_{r}
(
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
),

(4)

the normal Morse datum
NMD({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},\{0\})\simeq LMD({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},l,0)\simeq 0
.

Next we explain why the nearby and vanishing cycle functors preserve (weak) constructibility.
Consider a complex algebraic (or analytic) morphism
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
as in diagram (
85
).
Assume
X
is endowed with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
such that
X_{0}=\{f=0\}
is a union of strata, with induced stratification
{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
.
Similarly for the induced stratification
{\mathscr{S}}|_{X^{*}}
of the open complement
X^{*}=\{f\neq 0\}
.
But
\widehat{\pi}\colon\widetilde{X^{*}}\to X^{*}
is an infinite cyclic covering with deck group
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
, so that

\widetilde{X^{*}}
gets an induced complex analytic Whitney stratification
\widetilde{{\mathscr{S}}}
, making
\widehat{\pi}\colon\widetilde{X^{*}}\to X^{*}
a
stratified map

such that, for any stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
,
\widehat{\pi}\colon\widehat{\pi}^{-1}(S)\to S
is also an infinite cyclic covering with deck group

\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e., a locally trivial fibration with fiber
{\mathbb{Z}}
. But this implies by induction on
\dim X^{*}
the following (see, e.g.
[
113
, Corollary 4.2.1(4)]
).

Lemma 4.8
.

R\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}
maps
D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}|_{X^{*}}-wc}(X^{*};R)
to itself.

Together with Example
2.38
this implies (also in the complex algebraic context) the following important fact.

Corollary 4.9
.

The nearby and vanishing cycle functors
\psi_{f},\varphi_{f}
induce

\psi_{f},\;\varphi_{f}\colon\>D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)wc}(X;R)\to D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}-)wc}(X_{0};R)\>,

i.e., they preserve weak constructibility (with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}
and
{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
).

But note that
R\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}

does not preserve

D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{X^{*}}-)c}(X^{*};R)
, i.e., constructibility.
Let
g
be a generator of
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
, given by the complex
orientation of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, which we interpret now as an
automorphism
of

\widetilde{X^{*}}
.
Let
{\mathscr{G}}
be a sheaf on
X^{*}
. We claim that the following sequence of sheaves
on
X^{*}
is
exact
:

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>{\mathscr{G}}@>{ad_{\widehat{\pi}}}>{}>\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathscr{G}}@>{g^{*}-id}>{}>\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathscr{G}}@>{}>{}>0\>.\end{CD}

Take a ball
B
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
so that the restriction of the
universal covering map of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
to
B
is
isomorphic to the projection
B\times{\mathbb{Z}}\to B
, with
g
corresponding
to the translation
{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}},i\mapsto i+1
.
If we take an open subset
V\subset f^{-1}(B)
, then
\widehat{\pi}\colon\widehat{\pi}^{-1}(V)\to V

is isomorphic to the projection
V\times{\mathbb{Z}}\to V
, with
g
acting as
before on the second factor. Then

\Gamma(V,\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathscr{G}})\simeq\prod\nolimits_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\;\Gamma(V,{\mathscr{G}})

such that
g^{*}
acts by the
permutation

i\mapsto i+1
.
Moreover the adjunction map corresponds to the diagonal embedding

\Gamma(V,{\mathscr{G}})\to\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\;\Gamma(V,{\mathscr{G}})
.
Then the sequence

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\Gamma(V,{\mathscr{G}})@>{diag}>{}>\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\;\Gamma(V,{\mathscr{G}})@>{g^{*}-id}>{}>\prod_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\;\Gamma(V,{\mathscr{G}})@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

is exact. Since each point of
X^{*}=\{f\neq 0\}
has a fundamental system
of open neighborhoods
V
as before, this implies our claim.

By using a flabby resolution we therefore get the distinguished
triangles (see, e.g.,
[
113
, (5.88) on p.369]
)

\begin{CD}{\mathscr{G}}@>{ad_{\widehat{\pi}}}>{}>R\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathscr{G}}@>{g^{*}-id}>{}>R\widehat{\pi}_{*}\widehat{\pi}^{*}{\mathscr{G}}@>{[1]}>{}>\end{CD}

for any
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X^{*};R)
, and

(88)

\begin{CD}i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{ad_{\widehat{\pi}}}>{}>\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{h_{f}-id}>{}>\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{[1]}>{}>\end{CD}

for any
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
.

Consider also the distinguished triangle

(89)

\begin{CD}i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{ad_{j}}>{}>i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{}>{}>i^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[1]@>{[1]}>{}>\>.\end{CD}

Since the map
can\colon i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\to\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
factorizes as

\begin{CD}can\colon i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{ad_{j}}>{}>i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{ad_{\widehat{\pi}}}>{}>\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\>,\end{CD}

we get from the distinguished triangles (
89
),
(
88
), (
87
) and the

octahedral axiom
a distinguished triangle

(90)

\begin{CD}i^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[1]@>{}>{}>\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{var}>{}>\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{[1]}>{}>\end{CD}

for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
.
Here the variation morphism

var\colon\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\to\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

can be defined by the cone of the pair of morphisms
(applied to a flabby resolution):

(0,h-id)\colon[i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\to\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}]\longrightarrow[0\to\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}]\>,

with
h=h_{f}
the monodromy automorphism, so that

(91)

can\circ var=h-id\quad\text{and}\quad var\circ can=h-id\>.

Remark 4.10
.

Note that the
variation morphism

var
depends of the choice of a generator
g\in\pi_{1}({\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e., on the choice of an orientation for

{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. Moreover there are then two choices for
var
in the literature, using a different sign convention so that

can\circ var=\pm(h-id)=\mp(id-h)\quad\text{and}\quad var\circ can=\pm(h-id)=\mp(id-h)\>.

For example, our choice here only fits with
-var
as used in
[
67
, Equation (8.6.8)]
and
[
113
, Equation (5.90)]
.

Let us now explain an important description of the
(co)stalks of the nearby cycles
in terms of
local Milnor fibers
.
Consider a complex algebraic (or analytic) morphism
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
as in diagram (
85
).
Assume
X
is endowed with a Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
such that
X_{0}=\{f=0\}
is a union of strata, with induced stratification
{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
.
By a classical theorem of Hironaka
[
57
, Corollary 1 to Theorem 2, p.248]
(and see also
[
76
, Corollary 1.3.5.1]
),
one can always refine a given stratification so that it satisfies the

a_{f}
-condition (
69
). In fact, by a more recent result
[
14
, Theorem 4.2.1]
, this
a_{f}
-condition is true for the given Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, with
X_{0}
a union of strata.

Note that the
a_{f}
-condition is needed to have a
local Milnor fibration
at a given point
x\in X_{0}
, with
Milnor fiber

(M_{f,x},\partial M_{f,x}):=(X\cap\{f=w\}\cap\{r\leq\epsilon\},X\cap\{f=w\}\cap\{r=\epsilon\})

\text{and}\quad\mathring{M}_{f,x}:=M_{f,x}\backslash\partial M_{f,x}

for
0<|w|\ll\epsilon

and a general holomorphic function germ

f\colon(X,x)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
on a singular complex analytic variety
X
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 1.3.3]
and
[
72
]
).
Here we consider a local embedding
(X,x)\hookrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)
with
r(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\cdot\bar{z}_{i}
the distance to
x=0
.
By the
curve selection lemma
one can assume that (locally near
x\in X_{0}
)
w
is a stratified regular value, i.e.,
\{f=w\}
is transversal to
{\mathscr{S}}
for

0<|w|
small enough. Then by the
a_{f}
-condition, and for
0<|w|\ll\epsilon
small enough,
r=\epsilon
is a stratified regular value of
r
with respect to
the induced Whitney stratification of
X\cap\{f=w\}
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 1.1.3]
).

Then the following local calculation is a direct consequence of the definition of the
nearby cycle functor
and the existence of such a
local Milnor fibration

(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 5.4.2]
).

Proposition 4.11
.

For every
x\in X_{0}
, with
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow X_{0}=\{f=0\}
the inclusion, there are isomorphisms:

(92)

i_{x}^{*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(M_{f,x},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(\mathring{M}_{f,x},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

compatible with the corresponding monodromy actions,
and

(93)

i_{x}^{!}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(M_{f,x},\partial M_{f,x},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma_{c}(\mathring{M}_{f,x},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

for any
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(X;R)
.

By Proposition
3.21
and the triangle (
87
) we get the following.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
T\subset D^{b}(\{pt\};R)
be a fixed “null system”, i.e., a full triangulated subcategory stable by isomorphisms.
If
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}-)T-stalk}(X;R)
, then also

\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},\;\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}-)T-stalk}(X;R)\>.

In particular,
\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
and
\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
are constructible for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
constructible.

Remark 4.13
.

By taking
T=D^{b}_{c,\chi=0}(\{pt\};R)
, we get the nearby and vanishing cycles for constructible functions:

\psi_{f},\;\varphi_{f}\colon\>CF_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X)\to CF_{({\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}})}(X_{0})\>,

with

\psi_{f}(\alpha)(x):=\int_{M_{f,x}}\alpha d\chi\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi_{f}(\alpha)(x):=\int_{M_{f,x}}\alpha d\chi-\alpha(x)

for
\alpha\in CF_{({\mathscr{S}})}(X)
and
x\in X_{0}=\{f=0\}
.

Using Proposition
4.11
and the distinguished triangle (
87
), one gets the following (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Lemma 5.4.1, Example 5.4.1]
).

Corollary 4.14
.

For every
x\in X_{0}=\{f=0\}
, with
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\hookrightarrow X_{0}=\{f=0\}
the inclusion, there are isomorphisms:

\displaystyle i_{x}^{*}(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\simeq R\Gamma(\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x},\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x}\cap\{f=w\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

(94)

\displaystyle\simeq\bigl(R\Gamma_{\{Re(f)\geq 0\}}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\bigr)_{x}=:LMD({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},Re(f),x)

for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(X;R)
and
0<|w|\ll\epsilon
small enough.
Here
\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x}=X\cap\{r<\epsilon\}
is the intersection of
X
with a small open
\epsilon
-ball
around
x\in X
.

Example 4.15
.

As a special case of (
4.14
), let
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}=R_{X}
be the constant sheaf on
X
. Since
\mathring{B}_{\epsilon,x}\cap X_{0}
is contractible, one gets

\displaystyle{\mathscr{H}}^{k}(\varphi_{f}R_{X})_{x}\simeq\widetilde{H}^{k}(M_{f,x};R)

is the
reduced cohomology
of the Milnor fiber
M_{f,x}
of
f
at
x
.
If, moreover,
X
is smooth, then Milnor fibers at smooth points of
X_{0}
are contractible, so the above calculation yields the inclusion:

\mathrm{supp}(\varphi_{f}R_{X}):=\bigcup_{k}\mathrm{supp}{\mathscr{H}}^{k}(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\subseteq\mathrm{Sing}(X_{0}).

A more general estimation of the support of vanishing cycles is provided by the following result (see, e.g.,
[
86
]
or
[
113
, Remark 4.2.4]
).

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety with a given Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
, and let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a morphism
with
X_{0}=\{f=0\}
. For every
{\mathscr{S}}
-weakly constructible complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
on
X
and every integer
k
, one has the inclusion

(95)

\mathrm{supp}{\mathscr{H}}^{k}(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\subseteq X_{0}\cap\mathrm{Sing}_{{\mathscr{S}}}(f),

where

\mathrm{Sing}_{{\mathscr{S}}}(f):=\bigcup_{S\in{\mathscr{S}}}\mathrm{Sing}(f|_{S})

is the stratified singular set of
f
with respect to the stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
.

Example 4.17

(Isolated stratified critical point)
.

In the context of Proposition
4.16
, assume that
x\in X_{0}=\{f=0\}
is an
isolated
stratified critical point of
f
.
Then

i_{x}^{!}(\varphi{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\simeq i_{x}^{*}(\varphi{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\simeq\bigl(R\Gamma_{\{Re(f)\geq 0\}}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\bigr)_{x}=:LMD({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},Re(f),x)

for
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\to X_{0}
the point inclusion. Applying
i_{x}^{*}
to the distinguished triangle (
87
), one gets

\begin{CD}i_{x}^{*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]@>{can}>{}>i_{x}^{*}(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]@>{}>{}>i_{x}^{*}(i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})@>{[1]}>{}>\>,\end{CD}

with
i_{x}^{*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(M_{f,x},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
for a local Milnor fiber
M_{f,x}
as in (
92
).

Applying
i_{x}^{!}
to the distinguished triangle (
90
), one gets

\begin{CD}i_{x}^{!}(i^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})@>{}>{}>i_{x}^{*}(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]@>{var}>{}>i_{x}^{!}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]@>{[1]}>{}>\>,\end{CD}

with
i_{x}^{!}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(M_{f,x},\partial M_{f,x},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
for a local Milnor fiber
(M_{f,x},\partial M_{f,x})
as in (
93
).

Remark 4.18
.

Consider a local embedding
(X,x)\hookrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}^{n},x)
, with
N
a
normal slice
to
x\in S
for a stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
.
Assume
g\colon({\mathbb{C}}^{N},x)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
is a holomorphic function germ such that the covector
dg_{x}
is
non-degenerate
with respect to
{\mathscr{S}}
.
Then
x
is an isolated stratified critical point of
g|_{N}
with respect to the induced Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}|_{N}
of
X\cap N
, so that
Example
4.17
gives for this case the distinguished triangles from Proposition
3.14
.

The nearby and vanishing cycle functors have the following
base change properties
.
Consider a cartesian diagram of morphism

(96)

\begin{CD}Y_{0}@>{k^{\prime}}>{}>X_{0}@>{}>{}>\{0\}\\
@V{i^{\prime}}V{}V@V{}V{i}V@V{}V{}V\\
Y@>{k}>{}>X@>{f}>{}>{\mathbb{C}}\>,\end{CD}

with
f^{\prime}:=f\circ k
. Then one has the following
base change isomorphisms
(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Remark 4.3.7, Lemma 4.3.4]
).

Proposition 4.19

(Base change isomorphisms for nearby and vanishing cycles)
.

The following base change isomorphisms commute with the maps
can
and
var
:

(1)

Assume
k\colon Y\to X
is proper. Then

Rk^{\prime}_{*}(\psi_{f^{\prime}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\psi_{f}(Rk_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\quad\text{and}\quad Rk^{\prime}_{*}(\varphi_{f^{\prime}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\varphi_{f}(Rk_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

for all
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(Y;R)
.

(2)

Assume
k\colon Y\to X
is smooth. Then

k^{\prime*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\psi_{f^{\prime}}(k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\quad\text{and}\quad k^{\prime*}(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\varphi_{f^{\prime}}(k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

for all
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
.

(3)

Assume
X\hookrightarrow M
is a closed subvariety of the complex algebraic (or analytic) manifold
M
, with
{\mathscr{S}}
a Whitney stratification of
X
.
Let
N\hookrightarrow M
be a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) submanifold which is transversal to
{\mathscr{S}}
(i.e., transversal to all strata
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
),
with
k\colon Y:=X\cap N\hookrightarrow X
the induced inclusion. Then

k^{\prime*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\psi_{f^{\prime}}(k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\quad\text{and}\quad k^{\prime*}(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\varphi_{f^{\prime}}(k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

for all
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-wc}(X;R)
.

Then one gets by
proper base change
and the Examples

4.3
and
4.7
the following (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 1.1.1]
).

Example 4.20
.

Let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a
proper
morphism, with
X_{0}=\{f=0\}
. Then

(97)

R\Gamma(X_{0},\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\psi_{id}(Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq i_{x}^{*}(Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(\{f=x\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

and

\displaystyle R\Gamma(X_{0},\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\simeq\varphi_{id}(Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\quad\quad

(98)

\displaystyle\simeq R\Gamma(D_{r},\{x\},Rf_{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(\{|f|<r,\{f=x\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(X;R)
and
a point
0\neq x\in D_{r}
in a small open disc
D_{r}
around zero. In particular, it follows from (
87
) and (
97
) that for any
0\neq x\in D_{r}
as above, one has for
f
proper the following
specialization sequence
:

(99)

\cdots\to H^{k}(X_{0};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\to H^{k}(\{f=x\};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\to H^{k}(X_{0};\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\to H^{k+1}(X_{0};{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\cdots

In the case
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}=R_{X}
and by analogy with the local situation, the groups
H^{*}(X_{0};\varphi_{f}R_{X})
are usually referred to as the the
vanishing cohomology of
f
(see, e.g.,
[
95
]
).

4.2.
Relation with perverse sheaves and duality

Let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a morphism of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties.
The behavior of the nearby and vanishing cycle functors with regard to Verdier duality is described by the following result (for instance, see
[
89
, Theorem 3.1, Corollary 3.2]
).

Theorem 4.21
.

The shifted functors
\psi_{f}[-1]
and
\varphi_{f}[-1]
commute with the
Verdier duality functor
{\mathscr{D}}
up to natural isomorphisms.

Note that this duality result also fits with the following behavior of the nearby and vanishing cycle functors with regard to the (dual) perverse t-structure
(but this is not used in its proof, see, e.g.,
[
113
, Theorem 6.0.2]
).

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a morphism of complex algebraic (or analytic) varieties, with
X^{*}:=\{f\neq 0\}
.
Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R))
is weakly constructible (resp.,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is constructible with
R
a Dedekind domain, in case we want to use the dual perverse t-structure). Then we have:

(1)

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X^{*};R)\>\Rightarrow\>(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X_{0};R)
.

(2)

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X^{*};R)\>\Rightarrow\>(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X_{0};R)
.

(3)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X;R)\>\Rightarrow\>(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X_{0};R)
.

(4)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X;R)\>\Rightarrow\>(\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X_{0};R)
.

Proof.

Note that the result (3.) resp., (4.) for the
vanishing cycle functor
follows directly form
the corresponding result (1.) resp., (2.) for the nearby cycle functor, if one uses the distinguished
triangle (
87
) or (
90
).

The argument for the nearby cycles is similar
to the proof of Theorem
3.19
. But this time we use the
corresponding description of Proposition
4.11
for the
(co)stalk
of the nearby cycle functor
.
Choose a complex algebraic (or analytic) Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}

of
X
with
X_{0}=\{f=0\}
and
X^{*}=\{f\neq 0\}

a union of strata, which
satisfies the
a_{f}
-condition
of Thom (
69
).
Then we already know, by Corollary
4.9
, that
\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is (weakly) constructible with respect to the induced Whitney stratification

{\mathscr{S}}|_{X_{0}}
of
X_{0}
.

Consider a point
x\in S
for a stratum
S\subset\{f=0\}
of dimension
s
.
First we assume that
S=\{x\}
is a point stratum. By Proposition
4.11
we get

i_{x}^{*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(X\cap\{r\leq\delta,f=w\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\>,

and

i_{x}^{!}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(X\cap\{r\leq\delta,f=w\},X\cap\{r=\delta,f=w\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

for
0<|w|\ll\delta\ll 1
, with
i_{x}\colon\{x\}\to\{f=0\}
the inclusion
and

r(z):=\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\>z_{i}\bar{z}_{i}\quad\text{in a local embedding}\quad(X,x)\hookrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)\>.

Then
L:=\{f=w\}
is
transversal
to
{\mathscr{S}}
near
x

for
0<|w|\ll 1
(by the curve selection lemma). So we get by Proposition
2.27
:

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}|_{L}[-1]\in\;^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(L;R)\quad\text{or}\quad{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}|_{L}[-1]\in\;^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(L;R)\>.

Moreover,
for
0<|w|\ll\delta\ll 1
,
\delta
is a regular value of
r
with respect to

{\mathscr{S}}|_{L}
. This follows from the
a_{f}
-condition
.
If we apply Theorem
3.19
with
q=0
to
r
(or
-r
),
then we get by the (co)stalk formulae above:

i_{x}^{*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\in\;^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(\{pt\};R)\quad\text{or}\quad i_{x}^{!}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\in\;^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(\{pt\};R)\>.

This proves our claim for a point stratum.

We reduce the general case to the first case by
taking a
complex analytic normal slice

N
at
x
(in some local embedding), with
\hbox{\rm codim}\,N=\dim S=s
.
Consider the cartesian diagram

\begin{CD}\{x\}=N\cap S@>{\kappa_{x}}>{}>N\cap\{f=0\}@>{i^{\prime}}>{}>N\cap X\\
@V{}V{k_{x}}V@V{}V{k^{\prime}}V@V{}V{k}V\\
S@>{i_{S}}>{}>\{f=0\}@>{i}>{}>X\>.\end{CD}

Then we have

k_{x}^{*}i_{S}^{*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\kappa_{x}^{*}k^{\prime*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\>.

Since
\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is weakly constructible with respect to
the induced stratification of
\{f=0\}
, we get by the
base change property
(
10
):

k_{x}^{*}i_{S}^{!}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\kappa_{x}^{!}k^{\prime*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\>.

But we also have by Proposition
4.19
the
base change isomorphism

k^{\prime*}(\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq\psi_{f^{\prime}}(k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\>,\quad\text{with $f^{\prime}:=f\circ k$.}

Then the claim follows from the first case
for
f^{\prime},\,X^{\prime}:=N\cap X
and
k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
, since

N
is transversal to
{\mathscr{S}}
near
x
, with
\hbox{\rm codim}\,N=\dim S=s
,
so that by Proposition
2.27
:

k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\;^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n-s}(X^{\prime};R)\quad\text{or}\quad k^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\;^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n-s}(X^{\prime};R)\>.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.23
.

We get in particular
that the (shifted)
nearby and vanishing cycle functors

{{}^{p}}\psi_{f}:=\psi_{f}[-1]
and
{{}^{p}}\varphi_{f}:=\varphi_{f}[-1]
are
t-exact
functors with respect to the
perverse t-structure
.

This is a result of Gabber in the algebraic
context for
l
-adic cohomology (unpublished, but compare with

[
6
, Proposition 4.4.2]
,
[
16
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
61
, Corollary 4.5, 4.6]
). In the complex analytic
context it is due to Goresky-MacPherson
[
45
, Theorem 6.5]
, and

[
47
, p.222, Corollary 6.13.6, p.224 6.A.5]
.
For another proof of
this classical case see
[
67
, Corollary 10.3.11, 10.3.13]
.

But it does not seem to be well known that the result also applies for
R
a Dedekind domain to the
dual t-structure
.
In particular, the functors
{{}^{p}}\psi_{f}=\psi_{f}[-1]
and
{{}^{p}}\varphi_{f}=\varphi_{f}[-1]
preserve then

strongly perverse
sheaves.

Example 4.24
.

If
X
is a pure-dimensional complex algebraic (or analytic) variety satisfying
{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=\dim X
for
R
a Dedekind domain, then Proposition
2.54
and the above Remark yield that
{{}^{p}}\psi_{f}R_{X}[\dim X]
and
{{}^{p}}\varphi_{f}R_{X}[\dim X]
are strongly perverse sheaves on
X_{0}
. Therefore
these perverse sheaves have torsion-free costalks in the lowest possible degree (cf. Corollary
2.55
).

Example 4.25
.

Let
f\colon(X,0)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
be a nonconstant holomorphic function germ defined on a pure
(n+1)
-dimensional complex singularity germ contained in some ambient
({\mathbb{C}}^{N},0)
.
Denote by
M_{f,0}
the
Milnor fiber of the singularity at the origin in
X_{0}=f^{-1}(0)
. Let
\Sigma:=\mathrm{Sing}_{{\mathscr{S}}}(f)
be the stratified singular locus of
f
with respect to a fixed Whitney stratification
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
, and set
r:=\dim_{0}\Sigma
.
Let
R
be a Dedekind domain and assume that
{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)=n+1
. The support condition for the perverse sheaf
{{}^{p}}\varphi_{f}R_{X}[n+1]
(which is supported on
\Sigma
) yields that the only possibly non-trivial
reduced cohomology
\widetilde{H}^{k}(M_{f,0};R)
of
M_{f,0}
is concentrated in degrees
n-r\leq k\leq n
. Moreover, as shown, e.g., in
[
96
, Theorem 3.4(d)]
or
[
113
, Example 6.0.12]
, the lowest (possibly) non-trivial module

\widetilde{H}^{n-r}(M_{f,0};R)
is torsion-free.

For more applications of the fact that vanishing and nearby cycle functors preserve strongly perverse sheaves see
[
113
]
, e.g.,
[
113
, Example 6.0.14]
for applications to
local Lefschetz and vanishing theorems
, as well as
[
95
]
(for the study of vanishing cohomology of complex projective hypersurfaces) and
[
96
]
(for understanding the Milnor fiber cohomology).

Using Theorem
4.22
and Corollary
3.25
, together with Example
4.17
and Remark
4.18
, one gets the following characterization (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Corollary 6.0.7]
).

Corollary 4.26
.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) variety.
Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is weakly constructible (resp.,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is constructible with
R
a Dedekind domain, in case we want to use the dual perverse t-structure).
Then we have for

“
?
given by
\,\leq
” or “
?
given by
\,\geq
”:

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\,?\,n}(X;R)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bigl((\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})[-1]\bigr)_{x}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\,?\,n}(\{pt\};R)

for all holomorphic function germs
f\colon(X,x)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
such that
x
is
isolated in the support of
\varphi_{f}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
.

As an application we get once more the
effectivity of characteristic cycles of perverse sheaves
, this time via
vanishing cycles
.
Assume
X
is a complex manifold and
R
is a field. Let
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{{\mathscr{S}}-c}(X;R)
be a constructible complex with respect to some Whitney stratification

{\mathscr{S}}
. Consider the characteristic cycle of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
, i.e.,

CC({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=\sum_{S\in{\mathscr{S}}}m(S)\cdot\overline{T_{S}^{*}X},

with multiplicities
m(S)
given as in Definition
3.34
in terms of the normal Morse data of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
.
Here, we recall the calculation of
CC({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
in terms of vanishing cycles.

For a stratum
S\in{\mathscr{S}}
, let
x\in S
be a point, and let
g\colon(X,x)\to({\mathbb{C}},0)
be a holomorphic function germ at
x
such that
dg_{x}
is a non-degenerate covector and
x
is a complex Morse critical point of
g|_{S}
(i.e.,
dg(X)
intersects
T_{S}^{*}X
transversally at
dg_{x}
). Then, as in Example
3.37
,
Re(g)|_{S}
has a classical Morse critical point at
x
of Morse index equal to
\dim S
, and the multiplicities
m(S)
of
CC({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
can be computed from Theorem
3.12
and Corollary
4.14
:

(100)

m(S)=\chi(({{}^{p}\varphi}_{g}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})_{x}).

Then we get by Example
4.17
and Corollary
4.26
:

Corollary 4.27
.

If, in the above notations,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is a perverse sheaf, then its characteristic cycle is effective, i.e.,
m(S)\geq 0
for all strata
S
in
{\mathscr{S}}
.

Let us come back to the general context for finishing this section with the following applications of Theorem
4.22

(see, e.g.,
[
113
, Proposition 6.0.2]
).

Proposition 4.28
.

Let
i\colon Y\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion
of a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subset, with
j\colon U:=X\backslash Y\hookrightarrow X
the
inclusion of the open complement. Suppose that
Y
can locally be
described (at each point
x\in Y
) as the common zero-set of at most

k
algebraic (or holomorphic) functions on
X
(
k\geq 1
).

Assume
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}(X;R)
is weakly constructible (resp.,
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is constructible with
R
a Dedekind domain,
in case we want to use the dual perverse t-structure).
Then one has:

(1)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X;R)\Rightarrow i^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[k]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(Y;R)
.

(2)

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X;R)\Rightarrow i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[-k]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(Y;R)
.

(3)

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(U;R)\Rightarrow i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[k-1]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(Y;R)
and

Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[k-1]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\leq n}(X;R)\>.

(4)

j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(U;R)\Rightarrow i^{!}Rj_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[-(k-1)]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(Y;R)
and

Rj_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}[-(k-1)]\in\>^{p^{(+)}}D^{\geq n}(X;R)\>.

These are local results,
so that we can assume that
Y
is the common zero-set of
k^{\prime}
algebraic (or holomorphic)
functions (with
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
). Then the claim for (1.) or (2.) follows by induction
from the case
k^{\prime}=1
, which is a direct application of Theorem
4.22

and the distinguished triangle (
87
) or (
90
).
Finally (3.) or (4.) is, by
i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}\simeq i^{!}Rj_{!}j^{*}[1]
(see, e.g.,
[
6
, (1.4.6.4)]
),
a special case of (1.) or (2.).

Example 4.29

(Purity)
.

Let
i\colon Y\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion
of a closed complex algebraic (or analytic) subset,
which can locally be described as the common zero-set of at most

k
algebraic (or holomorphic) functions on
X
. Then
i^{!}{\mathbb{D}}_{X}\simeq{\mathbb{D}}_{Y}
implies by
Proposition
4.28
the following estimate of the

rectified homological depth
:

(101)

{\mathrm{rHd}}(X,R)\geq n\>\Rightarrow\>{\mathrm{rHd}}(Y,R\geq n-k\>.

Especially for
X
smooth of pure dimension
n
, we get (using also
i^{*}R_{X}=R_{Y}
)

{\mathrm{rHd}}(Y,R)\geq n-k=\dim Y\geq{\mathrm{rHd}}(Y,R)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{Y}[\dim Y]\in\>^{p}D^{\geq 0}(Y;R)\>,

if
Y
is locally a
set-theoretical complete intersection
of codimension
k

in
X
. In particular

{\mathrm{rHd}}(Y,R)=\dim Y\quad\text{and}\quad\text{$R_{Y}[\dim Y]$ is a perverse sheaf}

for
Y
a
pure-dimensional local complete intersection
.

Compare also with
[
56
, Theorem 3.2.1, Corollary.3.2.2]
for the corresponding
homotopy results.

4.3.
Thom-Sebastiani for vanishing cycles

In this section, we state a Thom-Sebastiani result for vanishing cycles, generalizing
[
122
]
to functions defined on singular ambient spaces, with arbitrary critical loci, and with arbitrary weakly constructible sheaf coefficients. For complete details, see
[
87
]
and also
[
113
, Corollary 1.3.4]
.

Let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
g\colon Y\to{\mathbb{C}}
be complex algebraic (or analytic) functions. Let
pr_{1}
and
pr_{2}
denote the projections of
X\times Y
onto
X
and
Y
, respectively. Consider the function

f\boxtimes g:=f\circ pr_{1}+g\circ pr_{2}\colon X\times Y\to{\mathbb{C}}.

The goal is to express the vanishing cycle functor
\varphi_{f\boxtimes g}
in terms of the corresponding functors
\varphi_{f}
and
\varphi_{g}
for
f
and, resp.,
g
.

We let
V(f)=\{f=0\}
, and similarly for
V(g)
and
V(f\boxtimes g)
. Denote by

\ell

the inclusion of
V(f)\times V(g)
into
V(f\boxtimes g)
. With these notations, one has the following result.

Theorem 4.30
.

For
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(X;R)
and
{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{wc}(Y;R)
, there is a natural isomorphism

(102)

\ell^{*}{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f\boxtimes g}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})\simeq{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{g}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}

commuting with the corresponding monodromies.

Moreover, if
p=(x,y)\in X\times Y
is such that
f(x)=0
and
g(y)=0
, then, in an open neighborhood of
p
, the complex
{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f\boxtimes g}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})
has support contained in
V(f)\times V(g)
, and, in every open set in which such a containment holds, there are natural isomorphisms

(103)

{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f\boxtimes g}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})\simeq\ell_{!}({{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{g}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet})\simeq\ell_{*}({{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{L}{\boxtimes}{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{g}{\mathscr{G}}^{\bullet}).

Corollary 4.31
.

In the notations of the above theorem and with integer coefficients, there is an isomorphism

(104)

\widetilde{H}^{i-1}(M_{f\boxtimes g,p})\cong\bigoplus_{a+b=i}\left(\widetilde{H}^{a-1}(M_{f,pr_{1}(p)})\otimes\widetilde{H}^{b-1}(M_{g,pr_{2}(p)})\right)\\
\oplus\bigoplus_{c+d=i+1}{\rm{Tor}}\left(\widetilde{H}^{c-1}(M_{f,pr_{1}(p)}),\widetilde{H}^{d-1}(M_{g,pr_{2}(p)})\right),

where
M_{f,x}
denotes as usual the Milnor fiber of a function
f
at
x
, and similarly for
M_{g,y}
.

Remark 4.32
.

Note that the Thom-Sebastiani Theorem
4.30

implies directly the
multiplicativity of normal Morse data
in terms of vanishing cycles with respect to
external products
as mentioned in
Example
3.17
.

Example 4.33

(Brieskorn singularities and intersection cohomology)
.

For
i=1,\ldots,n
, consider a
{\mathbb{C}}
-local system
{\mathscr{L}}_{i}
of rank
r_{i}
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, with monodromy automorphism
h_{i}
, and denote the corresponding intersection cohomology complex on
{\mathbb{C}}
by
IC_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathscr{L}}_{i})
. The complex
IC_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathscr{L}}_{i})
agrees with
{\mathscr{L}}_{i}[1]
on
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, and has stalk cohomology at the origin concentrated in degree
-1
, where it is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Ker}~(id-h_{i})
.
For positive integers
a_{i}
, consider the functions
f_{i}(x)=x^{a_{i}}
