on
{\mathbb{C}}
. The complex
{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f_{i}}IC_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathscr{L}}_{i})
is a perverse sheaf supported only at
0
; therefore,
{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{f_{i}}IC_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathscr{L}}_{i})
is non-zero only in degree zero, where it has dimension
a_{i}r_{i}-\dim\mathrm{Ker}~(id-h_{i})
.

Next, consider the
{\mathbb{C}}
-local system
{\mathscr{L}}_{1}{\boxtimes}\cdots{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{L}}_{n}
on
({\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{n}
with monodromy automorphism
h:=\boxtimes_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}
, and note that, as in Example
2.31
,

IC_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathscr{L}}_{1})\overset{L}{\boxtimes}\cdots\overset{L}{\boxtimes}IC_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathscr{L}}_{n})\simeq IC_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}({\mathscr{L}}_{1}{\boxtimes}\cdots{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{L}}_{n}).

The perverse sheaf

{{{}^{p}}\varphi}_{x_{1}^{a_{1}}+\cdots+x_{n}^{a_{n}}}IC_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}({\mathscr{L}}_{1}{\boxtimes}\cdots{\boxtimes}{\mathscr{L}}_{n})

is supported only at the origin, and hence is concentrated only in degree zero. In degree zero, it can be seen by iterating the Thom-Sebastiani isomorphism that it has dimension equal to

\prod_{i}\left(a_{i}r_{i}-\dim\mathrm{Ker}~(id-h_{i})\right).

In the special case when
r_{i}=1
and
h_{i}=1
for all
i
, the above calculation recovers the classical result stating that the Milnor number of the isolated singularity at the origin of
x_{1}^{a_{1}}+\cdots+x_{n}^{a_{n}}=0
is
\prod_{i}(a_{i}-1)
.

4.4.
Gluing perverse sheaves via vanishing cycles

Assume
R={\mathbb{C}}
(or, more generally,
R
is an algebraically closed field; but only the case
R={\mathbb{C}}
also nicely fits with the corresponding theory of (regular) holonomic D-modules). Let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a complex algebraic (or analytic) morphism with corresponding nearby and vanishing cycle functors
\psi_{f}
,
\varphi_{f}
. Recall that these two functors come equipped with monodromy automorphisms, both of which are denoted here by
h
.
For
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;{\mathbb{C}})
, the morphism

can\colon\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

of (
87
) is called the
canonical morphism
, and it is compatible with monodromy. Similarly for the
variation morphism

var\colon\varphi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

of (
90
).
In the above notations we also have

(105)

can\circ var=h-id\quad\text{and}\quad var\circ can=h-id\>.

The monodromy automorphisms acting on the nearby and vanishing cycle functors have Jordan decompositions

h=h_{u}\circ h_{s}=h_{s}\circ h_{u},

where
h_{s}
is
semi-simple
(and locally of finite order) and
h_{u}
is
unipotent
. For any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;{\mathbb{C}})
, denote by

\psi_{f,\lambda}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
the generalized
\lambda
-eigenspace for
h
, and similarly for
\varphi_{f,\lambda}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
. By the definition of vanishing cycles, the canonical morphism
can
induces morphisms

can\colon\psi_{f,\lambda}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\longrightarrow\varphi_{f,\lambda}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},

which (since the monodromy acts trivially on
i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
) are isomorphisms for
\lambda\neq 1
, and there is a distinguished triangle

(106)

i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{{sp}}{\longrightarrow}\psi_{f,1}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{can}{\longrightarrow}\varphi_{f,1}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{[1]}{\longrightarrow}\ .

There are decompositions

(107)

\psi_{f}=\psi_{f,1}\oplus\psi_{f,\neq 1}\ \ {\rm and}\ \ \varphi_{f}=\varphi_{f,1}\oplus\varphi_{f,\neq 1}

so that
h_{s}=1
on
\psi_{f,1}
and
\varphi_{f,1}
, and
h_{s}
has no
1
-eigenspace on
\psi_{f,\neq 1}
and
\varphi_{f,\neq 1}
. Moreover,
can\colon\psi_{f,\neq 1}\to\varphi_{f,\neq 1}
and
var\colon\varphi_{f,\neq 1}\to\psi_{f,\neq 1}
are isomorphisms.

The canonical and variation morphisms play an important role in the following
gluing
of perverse sheaves.

Let
X
be a complex algebraic variety, with
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion of a closed algebraic subvariety, and
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion
of the open complement
U:=X\backslash Z
. A natural question to address is if one can “glue” the categories
Perv(Z)
and
Perv(U)
to recover the category
Perv(X)
of perverse sheaves on
X
. We discuss here only the case when
Z
is a hypersurface, but see also
[
128
]
for a more general setup.
The gluing procedure, due to Beilinson
[
7
]
and Deligne-Verdier
[
128
]
, establishes an equivalence of categories between perverse sheaves on the algebraic variety
X
and a pair of perverse sheaves, one on
Z
, the other on
U
, together with a gluing datum.
A similar method was used by M. Saito for constructing his mixed Hodge modules
[
110
]
.

As a warm-up situation, consider
X={\mathbb{C}}
with coordinate function
z
,
Z=\{0\}
and
U={\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. Let
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
be a
{\mathbb{C}}
-perverse sheaf on
X
. One can form the diagram

{{}^{p}\psi}_{z}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\overset{can}{\underset{var}{\rightleftarrows}}{{}^{p}\varphi}_{z}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}

whose objects are perverse sheaves on
Z=\{0\}
, i.e., complex vector spaces. This leads to the following elementary description of the category of perverse sheaves on
{\mathbb{C}}
, cf.
[
128
]
.

Proposition 4.34
.

The category of perverse sheaves on
{\mathbb{C}}
which are locally constant on
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
is equivalent to the category of quivers (that is, diagrams of vector spaces) of the form

{\psi}\overset{c}{\underset{v}{\rightleftarrows}}\varphi

with
\psi,\varphi
finite dimensional vector spaces, and
id+c\circ v
,
id+v\circ c
invertible.

Example 4.35
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}
be a local system on
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with stalk
V
and monodromy
h\colon V\to V
. The perverse sheaf
j_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]
(e.g., see Example
2.47
) corresponds to

V\overset{c}{\underset{v}{\rightleftarrows}}V/{\mathrm{Ker}~(h-id)},

where
c
is the projection and
v
is induced by
h-id
. Thus a quiver

{\psi}\overset{c}{\underset{v}{\rightleftarrows}}\varphi

with
c
surjective arises from
j_{*}{\mathscr{L}}[1]
, where
{\mathscr{L}}_{1}=\psi
is the stalk of
{\mathscr{L}}
and
h=id+v\circ c
.

More generally, let
f
be a regular function on a smooth algebraic variety
X
, with
Z=f^{-1}(0)
and
U=X\setminus Z
. Let
Perv(U,Z)_{gl}
be the category whose objects are
({\mathscr{A}}^{\bullet},{\mathscr{B}}^{\bullet},c,v)
, with
{\mathscr{A}}^{\bullet}\in Perv(U)
,
{\mathscr{B}}^{\bullet}\in Perv(Z)
,
c\in{\rm{Hom}}({{}^{p}\psi}_{f,1}{\mathscr{A}}^{\bullet},{\mathscr{B}}^{\bullet})
,
v\in{\rm{Hom}}({\mathscr{B}}^{\bullet},{{}^{p}\psi}_{f,1}{\mathscr{A}}^{\bullet})
, and so that
id+v\circ c
is invertible. Then one has the following.

Theorem 4.36

(Beilinson
[
7
]
, Deligne-Verdier
[
128
]
)
.

There is an equivalence of categories

Perv(X)\simeq Perv(U,Z)_{gl}

defined by:

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\mapsto({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}|_{U},{{}^{p}\varphi}_{f,1}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet},can,var).

See also
[
119
, Section 3.3, 3.4]
for some other examples of quiver descriptions of perverse sheaves, especially also for the corresponding description of Verdier duality on the quiver side.

4.5.
Other applications

There are many more applications of the nearby and vanishing cycles functors than we can mention in an expository paper, e.g., see
[
31
]
,
[
113
]
,
[
67
]
,
[
93
]
and the references therein. Let us only mention here that vanishing cycles play an important role in the theory of characteristic classes for singular hypersurfaces (e.g., see
[
19
]
,
[
99
]
,
[
100
]
,
[
116
]
), which have recently seen applications in birational geometry (cf.
[
100
]
), and they have also found applications to concrete optimization problems in applied algebraic geometry and algebraic statistics (e.g., see
[
97
]
,
[
98
]
and the survey
[
94
]
).

We finish this section with an application to the calculus of
Grothendieck groups of constructible sheaves and their relation to the theory of constructible functions (see, e.g.,
[
113
, Section 6.0.6]
for a more general version).

Let
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be an algebraic (or analytic) morphism with

i\colon X_{0}:=\{f=0\}\hookrightarrow X
and
j\colon U:=\{f\neq 0\}\hookrightarrow X
the inclusions.
Then one has the distinguished triangle (
88
)

\begin{CD}i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{}>{}>\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{h_{f}-id}>{}>\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{[1]}>{}>\end{CD}

for any
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
. But then also
\psi_{f}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X_{0};R)
by
Corollary
4.9
and Corollary
4.12
, so that

(108)

[i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}]=\>0\>\in K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(X_{0};R))

for the corresponding class in the
Grothendieck group of constructible sheaf complexes on
X_{0}
.

Let more generally
X_{0}:=\{f_{i}=0,\>i=1,..,k+1\}
be the common zero-set of
the algebraic (or holomorphic) functions
f_{i}\colon X\to{\mathbb{C}}
(
i=1,\dots,k+1
).
Let
j\colon U:=X\backslash X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
be again the inclusion.
Then the equality (
108
) is also true. This follows by induction from
a
Mayer-Vietoris triangle
(see, e.g.,
[
67
, p.114, (2.6.28)]
,
with
R\Gamma_{U}(\cdot)\simeq Rj_{*}j^{*}
) associated to the
open covering
\{U_{1},U_{2}\}
of
U
, with

U_{1}:=\{f_{k+1}\neq 0\}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{2}:=X\backslash\{f_{i}=0,\>i=1,\dots,k\}\>.

Note that

U_{1}\cap U_{2}=X\backslash\{f_{i}\cdot f_{k+1}=0,\>i=1,\dots,k\}

is the complement of the
common zero-set of
k
algebraic (or holomorphic) functions.

Let
i\colon X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion. By the distinguished triangle

\begin{CD}Rj_{!}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{}>{}>Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{}>{}>i_{*}i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{[1]}>{}>\end{CD}

we get

(109)

Rj_{!}j^{*}=Rj_{*}j^{*}:\>K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(X;R))\to K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(X;R))\>.

Similarly, the distinguished triangle

\begin{CD}i^{!}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{}>{}>i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{}>{}>i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}@>{[1]}>{}>\end{CD}

implies

(110)

i^{!}=i^{*}:\>K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(X;R))\to K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(X_{0};R))\>.

Using again a

Mayer-Vietoris triangle
(see, e.g.,
[
67
, p.114, (2.6.28)]
,
with
R\Gamma_{U}(\cdot)\simeq Rj_{*}j^{*}
) associated to an
open affine covering, these equalities (
109
) and (
110
) are then available in the general complex algebraic context.
Consider in addition a proper morphism
h\colon X\to Y
. Then
Rh_{!}=Rh_{*}
maps
D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
into
D^{b}_{c}(X;R)

and we get the following equality for the induced maps on the level of
Grothendieck groups:

(111)

R(h\circ j)_{!}j^{*}=R(h\circ j)_{*}j^{*}:\>K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(X;R))\to K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(Y;R))

in the complex algebraic context.
Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of complex algebraic varieties.
By a theorem of Nagata (see, e.g.,
[
82
,
23
]
)
this can be (partially) compactified to
f=\bar{f}\circ j
, with
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\bar{X}

an open inclusion and
\bar{f}\colon\bar{X}\to Y
a proper morphism of
complex algebraic varieties.
Then the functors
Rf_{!},Rf_{*}
preserve algebraically constructible complexes
and one gets the equality (compare also with
[
70
, p.210]
and
[
129
]
):

(112)

Rf_{!}=Rf_{*}:\>K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(X;R))\to K_{0}(D^{b}_{c}(Y;R))\>,

which also implies for the induced group homomorphisms of complex algebraically constructible functions
the equality

(113)

f_{!}=f_{*}:\;CF(X)\to CF(X)\>.

Let us now consider the complex analytic context, with

CF(X)
the abelian group of
complex analytically constructible functions on
X
.

Example 4.37
.

Let
i\colon X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion
of a closed complex analytic subset, with
j\colon U:=X\backslash X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
the
inclusion of the open complement. Fix also a proper holomorphic map
f\colon X\to Y
of complex analytic varieties. Then we get the following equalities
for the corresponding group homomorphisms on the level of
constructible functions:

(1)

j_{!}j^{*}=j_{*}j^{*}:\;CF(X)\to CF(X)
,
especially
i^{*}j_{*}j^{*}=0
.

(2)

i^{!}=i^{*}:\;CF(X)\to CF(X_{0})
.

(3)

(f\circ j)_{!}j^{*}=(f\circ j)_{*}j^{*}:\;CF(X)\to CF(Y)
.

Note that (1.) implies (3.). Moreover, (1.) and (2.) are local results so that we can assume
that
X_{0}
is defined by finitely many holomorphic functions.
Then they follow from the corresponding result above on the level of
Grothendieck groups of constructible sheaf complexes.

A typically application is the following generalization of a classical result of
Sullivan for
R
a field and
X_{0}
compact so that the constant map
f\colon X_{0}\to pt
is proper. Then

(114)

\chi(X_{0},i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=f_{*}i^{*}j_{*}j^{*}(\chi_{stalk}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}))=0

for any
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(X;R)
.

Remark 4.38
.

R\Gamma(X_{0},i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
calculates the
global
link cohomology
of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
as defined in
[
38
]
.
For example, for
X_{0}=\{x\}
a point, one has
(for
0<\epsilon\ll 1
):

R\Gamma(X_{0},i^{*}Rj_{*}j^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\simeq R\Gamma(X\cap\{||z||=\epsilon\},{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\;,

with
||\cdot||
defined by some local embedding
(X,x)\hookrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}^{n},0)
. So, for
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}:=R_{X}
the constant sheaf, we get that
the
link

X\cap\{||z||=\epsilon\}\quad\text{of $X$ in $x$}

has a
vanishing Euler characteristic
.
For a proof of this classical result
of Sullivan
[
124
]
, based on the
Milnor fibration theorem
, compare with

[
18
, Proposition 4.1]
.

5.
Intersection cohomology, the decomposition theorem, applications

In this section, we overview properties of the intersection cohomology groups of complex algebraic varieties, which generalize the corresponding features of the cohomology groups of smooth varieties. These properties, consisting of Poincaré duality, Lefschetz theorems and the decomposition theorem, are collectively termed the
Kähler package
for intersection cohomology.
In this section we work over
R={\mathbb{Q}}
.

Recall the following.

Definition 5.1
.

The (compactly supported)
intersection cohomology groups
of a pure complex
n
-dimensional complex algebraic variety
X
are defined as:

IH^{k}(X;R):={H}^{k-n}(X;IC_{X})\ ,\ \ IH_{c}^{k}(X;R):={H}_{c}^{k-n}(X;IC_{X}).

First note that Poincaré duality for the intersection cohomology groups of
X
is an immediate corollary of the self-duality of
IC_{X}
(cf. Proposition
2.48
). Specifically, if
X
is a pure
n
-dimensional complex algebraic variety, there is an isomorphism

(115)

IH^{2n-k}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq IH^{k}_{c}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})^{\vee},

for any integer
k
.

Note also that if
X
is a pure
n
-dimensional complex algebraic variety, there exists a natural morphism

(116)

\alpha_{X}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{X}[n]\longrightarrow IC_{X},

extending the natural quasi-isomorphism on the smooth locus of
X
. Moreover,
\alpha_{X}
becomes a quasi-isomorphism is
X
is assumed to be a rational homology manifold (see, e.g.,
[
92
, Theorem 6.6.3]
).
Applying the hypercohomology functor to (
116
) yields a morphism

H^{k}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\longrightarrow IH^{k}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}),

which is an isomorphism if
X
is a rational homology manifold.

5.1.
Lefschetz type results for intersection cohomology

Lefschetz type results for intersection cohomology can be derived from sheaf theoretic statements about perverse sheaves.
We begin with the following immediate consequence of the Artin vanishing Theorem
3.59
for perverse sheaves:

Theorem 5.2

(Weak Lefschetz Theorem for Perverse Sheaves)
.

If
X
is a complex projective variety and
i\colon D\hookrightarrow X
denotes the inclusion of a hyperplane section, then for every
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}\in Perv(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
the restriction map

H^{k}(X;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\longrightarrow H^{k}(D;i^{*}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})

is an isomorphism for
k<-1
and is injective for
k=-1
.

Example 5.3
.

Let
X\subset{\mathbb{C}}P^{n+1}
be a complex projective hypersurface, with
i\colon D\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion of a hyperplane section. By applying Theorem
5.2
to the perverse sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}:={{\mathbb{Q}}}_{X}[n]
on
X
, one gets isomorphisms

H^{k}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\longrightarrow H^{k}(D;{\mathbb{Q}})

for all
k<n-1
and a monomorphism for
k=n-1
.

Theorem
5.2
has the following important consequence (see also Example
3.61
).

Corollary 5.4

(Lefschetz hyperplane section theorem for intersection cohomology)
.

Let
X^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}{P}^{N}
be a pure
n
-dimensional closed algebraic subvariety with a Whitney stratification
\mathscr{S}
. Let
H\subset\mathbb{C}{P}^{N}
be a generic hyperplane (i.e., transversal to all strata of

\mathscr{S}
), with
i\colon D:=X\cap H\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion of the corresponding hyperplane section. Then the natural homomorphism

IH^{k}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\longrightarrow IH^{k}(D;{\mathbb{Q}})

is an isomorphism for
0\leq k\leq n-2
and a monomorphism for
k=n-1
.

(Indeed, by transversality, one gets that
i^{*}IC_{X}\simeq IC_{D}[1]
, e.g., see Example
2.28
.)

The Hard Lefschetz theorem for intersection cohomology can also be deduced from a more general sheaf-theoretic statement. Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a projective morphism and let
L\in H^{2}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
be the first Chern class of an
f
-ample line bundle on
X
. Then
L
corresponds to a map of complexes
L\colon{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{X}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{X}[2]
, which, after tensoring with
IC_{X}
yields a map

L\colon IC_{X}\longrightarrow IC_{X}[2].

This induces
L\colon Rf_{*}IC_{X}\to Rf_{*}IC_{X}[2]
, and after applying perverse cohomology one gets a map of perverse sheaves on
Y
:

L\colon{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}(Rf_{*}IC_{X})\longrightarrow{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i+2}(Rf_{*}IC_{X}).

Iterating, one gets maps of perverse sheaves

L^{i}\colon{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{-i}(Rf_{*}IC_{X})\longrightarrow{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}(Rf_{*}IC_{X})

for every
i\geq 0
.
Then one has the following result, proved initially by positive characteristic methods
[
6
, Theorem 6.2.10]
(see also
[
109
,
110
]
, or the more geometric approach of
[
24
]
):

Theorem 5.5

(Relative Hard Lefschetz)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a projective morphism of complex algebraic varieties with
X
pure-dimensional, and let
L\in H^{2}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
be the first Chern class of an
f
-ample line bundle on
X
. For every
i>0
, one has isomorphisms of perverse sheaves

L^{i}\colon{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{-i}(Rf_{*}IC_{X})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}(Rf_{*}IC_{X}).

By taking
f
in Theorem
5.5
to be the constant map
f\colon X\to point
, one obtains as a consequence the Hard Lefschetz theorem for intersection cohomology groups:

Corollary 5.6

(Hard Lefschetz theorem for intersection cohomology)
.

Let
X
be a complex projective variety of pure complex dimension
n
, with
L\in H^{2}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
the first Chern class of an ample line bundle on
X
. Then there are isomorphisms

(117)

L^{i}\colon IH^{n-i}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}IH^{n+i}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})

for every integer
i>0
, induced by the cup product by
L^{i}
. In particular, the intersection cohomology Betti numbers of
X
are unimodal, i.e.,
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}IH^{i-2}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\leq\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}IH^{i}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
for all
i\leq n/2
.

5.2.
The decomposition theorem and immediate applications

A great deal of information about intersection cohomology groups can be derived from the
BBD decomposition theorem

[
6
, Theorem 6.2.5]
, one of the most important results in the theory of perverse sheaves. It was conjectured by S. Gelfand and R. MacPherson, and proved soon after by Beilinson, Bernstein, Deligne and Gabber by reduction to positive characteristic. The proof given in
[
6
]
ultimately rests on Deligne’s proof of the Weil conjectures. Different proofs were given later on by M. Saito (as a consequence of his theory of mixed Hodge modules
[
109
]
) and, more recently, by de Cataldo and Migliorini
[
24
]
(involving only classical Hodge theory). A more general decomposition theorem (for semi-simple perverse sheaves) has been obtained by Mochizuki
[
102
]
,
[
103
]
(with substantial contributions of Sabbah
[
108
]
), in relation to a conjecture of Kashiwara
[
66
]
; see also
[
26
]
.
For more topological versions of the decomposition theorem for self-dual perverse sheaves on the level of Witt and cobordism groups, see
[
21
,
119
]
.
In what follows, we explain the statement of the decomposition theorem, together with a few applications.

In its initial form of
[
6
]
, the decomposition theorem calculates the derived pushforward of an
IC
-complex under a proper algebraic map. For simplicity, in this section we assume that all varieties are
irreducible
.

Recall that every algebraic map
f\colon X\to Y
of complex algebraic varieties can be
stratified
, i.e., there exist algebraic Whitney stratifications
{\mathscr{S}}
of
X
and
{\mathscr{T}}
of
Y
such that, given any connected component
T
of a
{\mathscr{T}}
-stratum on
Y
one has the following properties:

(a)

f^{-1}(T)
is a union of connected components of strata of
{\mathscr{S}}
, each of which is mapped submersively to
T
by
f
;

(b)

For every point
y\in T
, there is an Euclidean neighborhood
U
of
y
in
T
and a stratum-preserving homeomorphism
h\colon U\times f^{-1}(y)\to f^{-1}(U)
such that
f|_{f^{-1}(U)}\circ h
is the projection to
U
.

Property
(b)
is just
Thom’s isotopy lemma
: for every stratum
T
in
Y
, the restriction of
f
over
T
, i.e.,
f|_{f^{-1}(T)}\colon f^{-1}(T)\to T
, is a topologically locally trivial fibration.

Theorem 5.7

(BBD decomposition theorem
[
6
]
)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a proper map of complex
algebraic varieties. Then:

(i)

(Decomposition) There
is a (non-canonical) isomorphism in
D^{b}_{c}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})
:

(118)

Rf_{\ast}IC_{X}\simeq\bigoplus_{i}\ ^{p}{\mathscr{H}}^{i}(Rf_{\ast}IC_{X})[-i].

(ii)

(Semi-simplicity) Each
{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}^{i}(Rf_{\ast}IC_{X})
is a semi-simple object in
Perv(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})
, i.e., if
{\mathscr{T}}
is the set of connected
components of strata of
Y
in a stratification of
f
, there is a
canonical isomorphism of perverse sheaves on
Y
:

(119)

{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{i}(Rf_{*}IC_{X})\simeq\bigoplus_{T\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ IC_{\overline{T}}({\mathscr{L}}_{i,T})

where the local systems

{\mathscr{L}}_{i,T}

are semi-simple.

Remark 5.8
.

If
f
is a projective submersion of smooth complex algebraic varieties, Theorem
5.7
reduces to Deligne’s decomposition theorem, see
[
28
]
and
[
29
, Theorem 4.2.6]
.

Standard facts in algebraic geometry (e.g., resolution of singularities and Chow’s lemma) reduce the proof of the BBD decomposition theorem (Theorem
5.7
) to the case when
f\colon X\to Y
is a projective morphism, with
X
a smooth variety.
If the morphism
f
is projective, then (
118
) is a formal consequence of the relative Hard Lefschetz theorem (cf.
[
30
]
). So the heart of the BBD decomposition theorem consists of the semi-simplicity statement. After the above-mentioned reductions for
f
, the proof given in
[
24
]
is by induction on the pair of indices
(\dim Y,r(f))
, where
r(f)
is the
degree of semi-smallness
of
f
. The problem is then reduced to the case
r(f)=0
, i.e., that of a
semi-small
map, when
Rf_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{X}[\dim X]\simeq{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{0}(f_{*}{\mathbb{Q}}_{X}[n])
is perverse on
Y
. This case is handled via the non-degeneracy of a certain refined intersection pairing associated to the fibers over the most singular points of
f
.

One of the first consequences of the BBD decomposition theorem is that it gives a splitting of
IH^{*}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
in terms of twisted intersection cohomology groups of closures of strata in
Y
, namely:

Corollary 5.9
.

Under the assumptions and notations of Theorem
5.7
, there is a splitting

(120)

IH^{j}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\bigoplus_{T\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ IH^{j-\dim X+\dim T-i}({\overline{T}};{\mathscr{L}}_{i,T}),

for every
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

By applying Theorem
5.7
to the case of a resolution of singularities one gets the following.

Corollary 5.10
.

The intersection cohomology of a complex algebraic variety is a direct summand of the cohomology of a resolution of singularities.

More generally, one has the following nice application of the BBDG decomposition theorem (e.g., see
[
27
, Section 4.5]
) or
[
92
, Theorem 9.3.37]
):

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a proper map of complex algebraic varieties, and let
Y^{\prime}:=f(X)
be the image of
f
. Denote by
d=\dim X-\dim Y^{\prime}
the relative dimension of
f
. Then
IC_{Y^{\prime}}[d]
is a direct summand of
Rf_{*}IC_{X}
. In particular,
IH^{j}(Y^{\prime};{\mathbb{Q}})
is a direct summand of
IH^{j}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})
for every integer
j
.

An important application of the decomposition statement (
118
) for
f\colon X\to Y
is the
E_{2}
-degeneration of the corresponding
perverse Leray spectral sequence
:

E_{2}^{i,j}=H^{i}(Y;{{}^{p}{\mathscr{H}}}^{j}(Rf_{*}IC_{X}))\Longrightarrow H^{i+j}(Y;Rf_{*}IC_{X})=IH^{i+j+\dim X}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}).

This can be used to prove the following singular version of the classical global invariant cycle theorem for smooth projective maps, as well as a local version of it (see
[
6
, Corollary 6.2.8, Corollary 6.2.9]
):

Theorem 5.12

(Global and local invariant cycle theorems)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a proper map of complex algebraic varieties. Let
U\subseteq Y
be a Zariski-open subset on which the sheaf
R^{i}f_{*}IC_{X}
is a local system. Then the following assertions hold:

(a)

(Global) The natural restriction map

IH^{i}(X;{\mathbb{Q}})\longrightarrow H^{0}(U;R^{i}f_{*}IC_{X})

is surjective.

(b)

(Local) Let
u\in U
and
B_{u}\subset U
be the intersection with a sufficiently small Euclidean ball (chosen with respect to a local embedding of
(Y,u)
into a manifold) centered at
u
. Then the natural restriction/retraction map

H^{i}(f^{-1}(u);IC_{X})\simeq H^{i}(f^{-1}(B_{u});IC_{X})\longrightarrow H^{0}(B_{u};R^{i}f_{*}IC_{X})

is surjective.

5.3.
A recent application of the Kähler package for intersection cohomology

In this section, we mention briefly a recent combinatorial application of the Kähler package for intersection cohomology. For more applications, the interested reader may consult
[
25
]
,
[
92
]
and the references therein.

Let
E=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\}
be a spanning subset of a
d
-dimensional complex vector space
V
, and let
w_{i}(E)
be the number of
i
-dimensional subspaces spanned by subsets of
E
.
In
1974
, Dowling and Wilson
[
32
,
33
]
proposed the following conjecture (which is a special case of a more general conjecture for matroids; see
[
12
]
for more recent developments):

Conjecture 5.13

(Dowling–Wilson top-heavy conjecture)
.

For all
i<d/2
one has:

(121)

w_{i}(E)\leq w_{d-i}(E).

Remark 5.14
.

If
d=3
, de Bruijn–Erdös showed that
w_{1}(E)\leq w_{2}(E)
. More generally, Motzkin showed that
w_{1}(E)\leq w_{d-1}(E)
.

Another conjecture concerning the numbers
w_{i}(E)
was proposed by Rota
[
105
,
106
]
in
1971
, and it can be formulated as follows:

Conjecture 5.15

(Rota’s unimodal conjecture)
.

There is some
j
so that

(122)

w_{0}(E)\leq\cdots\leq w_{j-1}(E)\leq w_{j}(E)\geq w_{j+1}(E)\geq\cdots\geq w_{d}(E).

Huh-Wang
[
59
]
used the Kähler package on intersection cohomology groups to prove the Dowling-Wilson top-heavy conjecture, and the unimodality of the “lower half” of the sequence
\{w_{i}(E)\}
. Specifically, they showed the following:

Theorem 5.16

(Huh-Wang)
.

For all
i<d/2
, the following properties hold:

(a)

(top heavy)
w_{i}(E)\leq w_{d-i}(E).

(b)

(unimodality)
w_{i}(E)\leq w_{i+1}(E).

Sketch of proof.

The key step in the proof is to show that the numbers
w_{i}(E)
are realized geometrically, i.e.,
there exists a complex
d
-dimensional projective variety
Y
such that for every
0\leq i\leq d
one has:

H^{2i+1}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})=0
and
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H^{2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})=w_{i}(E).

To define the variety
Y
one first uses
E=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\}
to construct a map
i_{E}\colon V^{\vee}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
by regarding each
v_{i}\in E
as a linear map on the dual vector space
V^{\vee}
. Precomposing
i_{E}
with the open inclusion
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\hookrightarrow({\mathbb{C}}P^{1})^{n}
yields a map

f\colon V^{\vee}\to({\mathbb{C}}P^{1})^{n}.

Set

Y:=\overline{\mathrm{Im~}(f)}\subset({\mathbb{C}}P^{1})^{n}.

Ardila-Boocher
[
5
]
showed that the variety
Y
has an algebraic cell decomposition (i.e., it is paved by complex affine spaces), the number of
{\mathbb{C}}^{i}
’s appearing in the decomposition of
Y
being exactly
w_{i}(E)
. However, the space
Y
is in this case highly singular, so its rational cohomology does not satisfy the Kähler package. Instead, one needs to use the corresponding intersection cohomology results.

Next, note that for any complex projective variety
Y
one has that

\mathrm{Ker}~\left(H^{i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\overset{\alpha}{\to}IH^{i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\right)=W_{\leq i-1}H^{i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})

is the subspace of
H^{i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})
consisting of classes of Deligne weight
\leq i-1
(e.g., see
[
130
, Theorem 9.2]
). Since the complex projective variety
Y
constructed above has an algebraic cell decomposition, its cohomology
H^{i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})
is pure of weight
i
. (This follows easily by induction using the fact that
H^{i}_{c}(\mathbb{C}^{n};\mathbb{Q})=0
for
i\neq 2n
and
H^{2n}_{c}(\mathbb{C}^{n};\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{C}
is pure of weight
2n
.)
Hence, the natural map

\alpha\colon H^{*}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\to IH^{*}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})

is injective in any degree
i
.

For
i<d/2
, consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{H^{2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\smallsmile L^{d-2i}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{IH^{2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\smallsmile L^{d-2i}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{H^{2d-2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{IH^{2d-2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})}

where the right-hand vertical arrow is the Hard Lefschetz isomorphism for the intersection cohomology groups of
Y
(see Corollary
5.6
). Since the maps labelled by
\alpha
are injective, it follows that

H^{2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\xrightarrow{\smallsmile L^{d-2i}}H^{2d-2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})

is injective as well. In particular,

w_{i}(E)=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H^{2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\leq\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H^{2d-2i}(Y;{\mathbb{Q}})=w_{d-i}(E)

for every
i<d/2
, thus proving part
(a)
of the theorem.
Part
(b)
follows similarly, by using the unimodality of the intersection cohomology Betti numbers (cf. Corollary
5.6
).
∎

6.
Perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties

In this section, we survey recent developments in the study of perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties, with concrete applications in the study of homotopy types of complex algebraic manifolds (formulated in terms of their cohomology jump loci), as well as new topological characterizations of semi-abelian varieties. We begin by introducing and recalling the main ingredients needed to formulate our results. For complete details, the interested reader may consult
[
77
,
80
,
79
]
.

6.1.
Cohomology jump loci

Let
X
be a connected CW complex of finite type (e.g., a complex quasi-projective variety) with positive first Betti number, i.e.,
b_{1}(X)>0
. The
character variety

\Char(X)
of
X
is the identity component of the moduli space of rank-one
{\mathbb{C}}
-local systems on
X
, i.e.,

\Char(X):={\rm{Hom}}(H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})/\text{Torsion},{\mathbb{C}}^{*})\simeq({\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{b_{1}(X)}.

Definition 6.1
.

The
i
-th cohomology jump locus of
X
is defined as:

{{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(X)=\{\rho\in\Char(X)\mid H^{i}(X;L_{\rho})\neq 0\}},

where
L_{\rho}
is the unique rank-one
{\mathbb{C}}
-local system on
X
associated to the representation
\rho\in\Char(X)
.

The jump loci
{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(X)
are closed subvarieties of
\Char(X)
and homotopy invariants of
X
. For projective varieties, they can be seen as topological counterparts of the Green-Lazarsfeld jump loci of topologically trivial line bundles
[
50
,
51
]
. Cohomology jump loci emerged from work of Novikov on Morse theory for closed
1
-forms on manifolds, and provide a unifying framework for the study of a host of questions concerning homotopy types of complex algebraic varieties.

6.2.
Jump loci via constructible complexes

The Albanese map construction allows one to interpret the cohomology jump loci of a smooth connected complex quasi-projective variety as cohomology jump loci of certain constructible complexes of sheaves (or even of perverse sheaves, if the Albanese map is proper) on a semi-abelian variety. This motivates the study of cohomology jump loci of such complexes, with a view towards characterizing important classes of objects (such as perverse sheaves) on semi-abelian varieties.

An
abelian variety
of dimension
g
is a compact complex torus
{\mathbb{C}}^{g}/{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2g}}
, which is also a complex projective variety.
A
semi-abelian variety

G
is an abelian complex algebraic group, which is an extension
of an abelian variety by a complex affine torus.
(Semi-)abelian varieties are naturally associated to smooth (quasi-)projective varieties via the
Albanese map
construction, see
[
60
]
.
Specifically, if
X
is a smooth complex (quasi-)projective variety, the
Albanese map
of
X
is a morphism

\alb\colon X\to\Alb(X)

from
X
to a (semi-)abelian variety
\Alb(X)
such that for any morphism
f\colon X\to G
to a semi-abelian variety
G
, there exists a unique morphism
g\colon\Alb(X)\to G
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\alb\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{\Alb(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{G}

Here,
\Alb(X)
is called the
Albanese variety
associated to
X
.

The Albanese map induces an isomorphism on the torsion-free part of the first integral homology groups, i.e.,

(123)

H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})/\text{Torsion}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}H_{1}(\Alb(X);{\mathbb{Z}}).

In particular, there is an identification:

(124)

\Char(X)\simeq\Char(\Alb(X)),

and the equality of Betti numbers
b_{1}(X)=b_{1}(\Alb(X))
.

By using the projection formula, for any
\rho\in\Char(X)\simeq\Char(\Alb(X))
one gets:

(125)

H^{i}(X;L_{\rho})\simeq H^{i}(X;{\mathbb{C}}_{X}\otimes L_{\rho})\simeq H^{i}\big(\Alb(X);(R\alb_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{X})\otimes L_{\rho}\big).

If, moreover, the Albanese map
\alb\colon X\to\Alb(X)
is proper (e.g., if
X
is projective), then the BBD decomposition theorem
[
6
]

yields that
R\alb_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{X}
is a direct sum of (shifted)
perverse sheaves. In view of (
125
), this provides a description of the cohomology jump loci of
X
in terms of cohomology jump loci of certain perverse sheaves on the semi-abelian variety
\Alb(X)
. This motivates the following.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(G;{\mathbb{C}})
be a bounded constructible complex of
{\mathbb{C}}
-sheaves on a semi-abelian variety
G
.
The
degree
i
cohomology jump locus of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is defined as:

{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}):=\{\rho\in\Char(G)\mid H^{i}(G;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}L_{\rho})\neq 0\}.

Back to the cohomology jump loci of
X
, we note that (
125
) yields the following identification:

(126)

{{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(X)={\mathcal{V}}^{i}(\Alb(X),R\alb_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{X})}.

6.3.
Mellin transformation and applications

Let
G
be a semi-abelian variety defined by an extension

1\to T\to G\to A\to 1,

where
A
is an abelian variety of dimension
g
and
T\simeq({\mathbb{C}}^{*})^{m}
is a complex affine torus of dimension
m
. Set

\Gamma_{G}:={\mathbb{C}}[\pi_{1}(G)],

and note that
\Gamma_{G}
is a Laurent polynomial ring in
m+2g
variables. Moreover,

\Char(G)\simeq\spec\Gamma_{G}.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}_{G}
be the (universal) rank-one local system of
\Gamma_{G}
-modules on
G
, defined by mapping the generators of
\pi_{1}(G)
to the multiplication by the corresponding variables of
\Gamma_{G}
.

Definition 6.3
.

[
42
]
The
Mellin transformation

{\mathcal{M}}_{\ast}\colon D^{b}_{c}(G;{\mathbb{C}})\to D^{b}_{coh}(\Gamma_{G})
is given by

{{\mathcal{M}}_{\ast}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}):=Ra_{\ast}({\mathscr{L}}_{G}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})},

where
a\colon G\to point
is the constant map to a point space, and
D^{b}_{coh}(\Gamma_{G})
denotes the bounded coherent complexes of
\Gamma_{G}
-modules.

The Mellin transformation can be used to completely characterize perverse sheaves on complex affine tori. More precisely, one has the following result due to Gabber-Loeser
[
42
, Theorem 3.4.1 and Theorem 3.4.7]
in the
l
-adic context, and extended to the present form in
[
77
, Theorem 3.2]
:

Theorem 6.4
.

A constructible complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(T;{\mathbb{C}})
on a complex affine torus
T
is perverse if, and only if,

H^{i}({\mathcal{M}}_{\ast}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}))=0
for all
i\neq 0
.

In the context of abelian varieties, the Mellin transformation was used in
[
9
]
for proving generic vanishing results for the cohomology of perverse sheaves. Inducting on the dimension of the complex affine torus
T
, the result of
[
9
]
was extended to the semi-abelian context as follows.

Theorem 6.5
.

[
80
, Theorem 4.3]
For any
{\mathbb{C}}
-perverse sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
on a semi-abelian variety
G
, one has:

H^{i}({\mathcal{M}}_{*}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}))=0
for
i<0
,

and

H^{i}(D_{\Gamma_{G}}({\mathcal{M}}_{*}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})))=0
for
i<0
.

Here
D_{\Gamma_{G}}(-):=\rhomo_{\Gamma_{G}}(-,\Gamma_{G})
is the dualizing functor for
\Gamma_{G}
-modules.

The Mellin transformation can be used to translate the question of understanding the cohomology jump loci of a constructible complex to a problem in commutative algebra.
Specifically, by the projection formula, cohomology jump loci of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(G;{\mathbb{C}})
are determined by those of the Mellin transformation
{\mathcal{M}}_{\ast}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
as follows (see
[
77
]
,
[
80
]
):

(127)

{{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})={\mathcal{V}}^{i}({\mathcal{M}}_{\ast}({\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}))}.

Here, if
R
is a Noetherian domain and
E^{\centerdot}
is a bounded complex of
R
-modules with finitely generated cohomology, the closed points of
{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(E^{\centerdot})
can be described as

{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(E^{\centerdot}):=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\spec R\mid H^{i}(F^{\centerdot}\otimes_{R}R/\mathfrak{p})\neq 0\},

with
F^{\centerdot}
a bounded above finitely generated
free
resolution of
E^{\centerdot}
. One also has the following:

Proposition 6.6
.

[
9
, Lemma 2.8]
Let
R
be a regular Noetherian domain and
E^{\centerdot}
a bounded complex of
R
-modules with finitely generated cohomology. Then
H^{i}(E^{\centerdot})=0
for
i<0
if, and only if,
\hbox{\rm codim}\,{\mathcal{V}}^{-i}(E^{\centerdot})\geq i
for
i\geq 0
.

By using the identification (
127
), together with Proposition
6.6
and standard facts from commutative algebra, the following result of
[
80
]
is a direct consequence of Theorem
6.5
:

Theorem 6.7
.

[
80
, Theorem 4.7]
For any
{\mathbb{C}}
-perverse sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
on a semi-abelian variety
G
, the cohomology jump loci of
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
satisfy the following properties:

(i)

Propagation:

{{\mathcal{V}}^{-m-g}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq{\mathcal{V}}^{0}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}^{1}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\supseteq\cdots\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}^{g}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})}.

Moreover,
{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=\emptyset
if
i\notin[-m-g,g]
.

(ii)

Codimension lower bound:
for all
i\geq 0
,

{\hbox{\rm codim}\,{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\geq i}\ \ {\rm and}\ \ {\hbox{\rm codim}\,{\mathcal{V}}^{-i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\geq i}.

Theorem
6.7
is inspired by, and can be viewed as a topological counterpart of, similar properties satisfied by the Green-Lazarsfeld jump loci of topologically trivial line bundles, see
[
50
,
51
]
.

Remark 6.8
.

Let
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
be a
{\mathbb{C}}
-perverse sheaf so that not all
H^{j}(G;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
are zero.
The propagation property (i) can be restated as saying that the set of integers
j
for which
H^{j}(G;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\neq 0
form an
interval
of consecutive integers. Indeed, let

k_{+}:=\max\{j\mid H^{j}(G;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\neq 0\}\text{ and }k_{-}:=\min\{j\mid H^{j}(G;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\neq 0\}.

Then it’s easy to see that the propagation property (i) is equivalent to:
k_{+}\geq 0
,
k_{-}\leq 0
and

H^{j}(G;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\neq 0\ \iff\ k_{-}\leq j\leq k_{+}.

In this form, the result of Theorem
6.7
(i) provides a generalization of a result of Weissauer
[
132
, Corollary 1]
from the abelian context.

A nice consequence of Theorem
6.7
is the following generic vanishing result:

Corollary 6.9
.

For any
{\mathbb{C}}
-perverse sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
on a semi-abelian variety
G
,

H^{i}(G;{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}}L_{\rho})=0

for any generic rank-one
{\mathbb{C}}
-local system
L_{\rho}
and all
i\neq 0
.
In particular,

(128)

\chi(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\geq 0.

Moreover,
\chi(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
if, and only if,
{\mathcal{V}}^{0}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\neq\Char(G)
.

The above generic vanishing statement was originally proved by other methods in

[
68
, Theorem 2.1]
in the
l
-adic context and further generalized to arbitrary field coefficients in
[
78
, Theorem 1.1]
. For abelian varieties, generic vanishing results were obtained in
[
69
, Theorem 1.1]
,
[
111
, Corollary 7.5]
,
[
131
, Vanishing Theorem]
or
[
9
, Theorem 1.1]
. The signed Euler characteristic property (
128
) is originally due to Franecki and Kapranov
[
40
, Corollary 1.4]
(and compare with the
Example
3.40
for another approach to this signed Euler characteristic property).

Remark 6.10
.

The properties of perverse sheaves from Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
6.9
are collectively termed the
propagation package
for perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties.

6.4.
Characterization of perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties

In this section, we discuss a complete (global) characterization of perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties; see
[
80
]
for complete details.
Motivation is also provided by the following result:

Theorem 6.11
.

[
111
]

If
A
is an abelian variety and
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(A;{\mathbb{C}})
, then

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
is perverse if, and only if, for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\hbox{\rm codim}\,{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(A,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\geq|2i|.

Furthermore, as a consequence of Theorem
6.4
, Proposition
6.6
and Artin’s vanishing theorem
3.59
, one gets the following:

Theorem 6.12
.

[
80
, Corollary 6.8]

{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(T;{\mathbb{C}})
is perverse on a complex affine torus
T
if, and only if,

(i)

For all
i>0
:
{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(T,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=\emptyset
, and

(ii)

For all
i\leq 0
:
\hbox{\rm codim}\,{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(T,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\geq-i
.

In order to unify and generalize the results of Theorems
6.11
and
6.12
to the semi-abelian context, one can make use of the new notions of (co)dimension for the cohomology jump loci, which were introduced in
[
80
]
.
First recall the following.

Definition 6.13
.

A closed irreducible subvariety
V
of
\Char(G)
is called
linear
if
there exists a short exact sequence of semi-abelian varieties

1\to G^{\prime\prime}(V)\to G\overset{q}{\to}G^{\prime}(V)\to 1

and some
\rho\in\Char(G)
such that

V:=\rho\cdot\mathrm{Im~}(q^{\#}\colon\Char(G^{\prime}(V))\to\Char(G))
.

Here
G^{\prime\prime}(V)
and
G^{\prime}(V)
depend on
V
, and
q^{\#}
is induced by
q\colon G\to G^{\prime}(V)
.

With the above definition, one has the following important structure result:

Theorem 6.14
.

[
17
]

For any
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(G;{\mathbb{C}})
, each jump locus
{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is a finite union of linear subvarieties of
\Char(G)
.

Definition 6.15
.

Let
G
be a semi-abelian variety and let
V
be an irreducible linear subvariety of
\Char(G)
. In the notations of Definition
6.13
, let

T^{\prime\prime}(V)
and
A^{\prime\prime}(V)
denote the complex affine torus and, resp., the abelian variety part of
G^{\prime\prime}(V)
. Define:

abelian codimension
:
\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{a}V:=\dim A^{\prime\prime}(V),

semi-abelian codimension
:
\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{sa}V:=\dim G^{\prime\prime}(V).

Similar notions can be defined for reducible subvarieties by taking the minimum among all irreducible components.

Remark 6.16
.

Let
V
be a nonempty linear subvariety of
\spec\Gamma_{G}
.

(1)

If
G=T
is a complex affine torus, then:
\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{sa}V=\hbox{\rm codim}\,V
and
\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{a}V=0
.

(2)

If
G=A
is an abelian variety, we have:
\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{sa}(V)=\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{a}(V)=\frac{1}{2}\hbox{\rm codim}\,(V)
.

In the above notations, the following generalization of Schnell’s result was obtained in
[
80
]
:

Theorem 6.17
.

[
80
, Theorem 6.6]

A constructible complex
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in D^{b}_{c}(G;{\mathbb{C}})
is perverse on
G
if, and only if,

(i)

\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{a}{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\geq i
for all
i\geq 0
, and

(ii)

\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{sa}{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(G,{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})\geq-i
for all
i\leq 0
.

Proof.

The “only if” part is proved by induction on
\dim T
, using Theorem
6.11
as the beginning step of the induction process. For the “if” part, one shows that the two codimension lower bounds in the statement of Theorem
6.17
are sharp. See
[
80
, Section 6]
for complete details.
∎

6.5.
Application: cohomology jump loci of quasi-projective manifolds

The results of Theorem
6.7
, Theorem
6.17
and Corollary
6.9
can be directly applied for the study of cohomology jump loci
{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(X)\subseteq\Char(X)=\Char(\Alb(X))
of a smooth complex quasi-projective
variety
X
. Specifically, one has the following.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety of complex dimension
n
. Assume that

R\alb_{*}({\mathbb{C}}_{X}[n])
is a perverse sheaf on
\Alb(X)
(e.g.,
\alb
is proper and semi-small, or
\alb
is quasi-finite).
Then the cohomology jump loci
{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(X)
satisfy the following properties:

(1)

Propagation property:

{\mathcal{V}}^{n}(X)\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}^{n-1}(X)\supseteq\cdots\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}^{0}(X)=\{{\mathbb{C}}_{X}\};

{\mathcal{V}}^{n}(X)\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}^{n+1}(X)\supseteq\cdots\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}^{2n}(X).

(2)

Codimension lower bound: for all
i\geq 0
,

\hbox{\rm codim}\,_{sa}{\mathcal{V}}^{n-i}(X)\geq i\ \ {\rm and}\ \ \hbox{\rm codim}\,_{a}{\mathcal{V}}^{n+i}(X)\geq i.

(3)

Generic vanishing:
H^{i}(X,L_{\rho})=0

for generic
\rho\in\Char(X)
and all
i\neq n
.

(4)

Signed Euler characteristic property:
(-1)^{n}\chi(X)\geq 0
.

(5)

Betti property:
b_{i}(X)>0
for all
i\in[0,n]
, and
b_{1}(X)\geq n
.

Example 6.19
.

Let
X
be a smooth closed subvariety of a semi-abelian variety
G
. The closed embedding
i\colon X\hookrightarrow G
is a proper semi-small map, and hence the Albanese map
\alb\colon X\to\Alb(X)
is also proper and semi-small. Then
R\alb_{*}({\mathbb{C}}_{X}[\dim X])
is a perverse sheaf on
\Alb(X)
and the jump loci of
X
satisfy the properties listed in Theorem
6.18
.

6.6.
Application: topological characterization of semi-abelian varieties

The Structure Theorem
6.14
together with the propagation package of Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
6.9
can be used to give the following topological characterization of semi-abelian varieties (see
[
80
, Proposition 7.7]
):

Theorem 6.20
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety with proper Albanese map (e.g.,
X
is projective), and assume that
X
is homotopy equivalent to a real torus. Then
X
is isomorphic to a semi-abelian variety.

Proof.

Assume
X
has complex dimension
n
.
By the BBD decomposition theorem
[
6
]
,
R\alb_{*}({\mathbb{C}}_{X}[n])
is a direct sum of shifted semi-simple perverse sheaves on
\Alb(X)
. Denote by
\mathcal{U}
the collection of all simple summands
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}
appearing (up to a shift) in
R\alb_{*}({\mathbb{C}}_{X}[n])
. Then, by using Theorem
6.7
(i) and the identification (
126
), one gets that

(129)

\bigcup_{i=0}^{2n}{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(X)=\bigcup_{\mathcal{U}}{\mathcal{V}}^{0}(\Alb(X),{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}).

Since
X
is homotopy equivalent to a real torus, a direct calculation yields that
\bigcup_{i=0}^{2n}{\mathcal{V}}^{i}(X)
is just an isolated point. Hence, by (
129
), for every simple perverse sheaf
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{U}
, the jump locus
{\mathcal{V}}^{0}(\Alb(X),{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})
is this isolated point, so Corollary
6.9
gives
\chi(\Alb(X),{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet})=0
.

Simple perverse sheaves with zero Euler characteristic on semi-abelian varieties are completely described in
[
80
, Theorem 5.5]
by using the structure Theorem
6.14
and the propagation package for their cohomology jump loci. In particular, it follows that for all
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}\in\mathcal{U}
one has that
{\mathscr{F}}^{\bullet}={\mathbb{C}}_{\Alb(X)}[\dim\Alb(X)]
. So
R\alb_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{X}
is a direct sum of shifted rank-one constant sheaves on
\Alb(X)
. Since
X
and
\Alb(X)
are both homotopy equivalent to tori, and since
b_{1}(X)=b_{1}(\Alb(X))
, one gets that
b_{i}(X)=b_{i}(\Alb(X))
for all
i
. Therefore,

R\alb_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{X}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}_{\Alb(X)}.

Since
\alb
is proper, it follows that all fibers of
\alb
are zero-dimensional. Then it can be seen easily that
\alb
is in fact an isomorphism.
∎

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.20
, it follows that the Albanese map
\alb\colon X\to\Alb(X)
is an isomorphism. One can similarly prove the following special case of a question of Bobadilla-Kollár
[
10
]
, see
[
81
]
for more general results:

Proposition 6.21
.

Let
X
be a complex projective manifold, and denote by
X^{ab}
the
universal free abelian cover of
X
, i.e., the covering associated with the homomorphism
\pi_{1}(X)\to H_{1}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})/Torsion
. If
X^{ab}
is homotopy equivalent to a finite CW complex, then the sheaves
R^{i}\alb_{*}{\mathbb{C}}_{X}
are local systems on
\Alb(X)
for all
i\geq 0
.

Remark 6.22
.

The study of perverse sheaves on semi-abelian varieties has other interesting topological applications, e.g., strong finiteness properties for Alexander-type invariants, generic vanishing of Novikov and
L^{2}
-homology, the study of homological duality properties of smooth complex algebraic varieties, etc., see
[
39
]
,
[
78
]
,
[
77
]
,
[
80
]
for more details.
